GDC ORE Part 1 Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The maxillary sinus floor lies very close to the roots of the upper molars): the sinus floor sits directly above the roots of teeth 16 and 26, and extraction can perforate this thin bone. This creates an oro-antral communication, which allows fluid and air to pass between the mouth and the nasal sinus. The infraorbital nerve and zygomatic process are not directly responsible for this specific complication. Recognising the close root-to-sinus relationship helps a clinician anticipate and manage this risk before extracting upper posterior teeth.
  2. The correct answer is D (The lingula): this small bony spine lies just anterior to the mandibular foramen and is palpated during inferior alveolar nerve block to guide needle placement. The coronoid process and gonial angle are unrelated landmarks for this injection. The mylohyoid line lies more inferiorly and marks a different muscular attachment. Palpating the lingula helps the operator estimate the correct height and depth for depositing solution near the nerve.
  3. The correct answer is A (The mylohyoid line): this oblique ridge on the internal surface of the mandibular body gives attachment to mylohyoid and separates the sublingual space above from the submandibular space below. The oblique line lies on the external surface of the mandible and has a different function. The mandibular canal and mental protuberance are unrelated structures. This landmark is central to predicting how odontogenic infection spreads from mandibular teeth.
  4. The correct answer is C (The glenoid fossa of the temporal bone): the condylar process articulates here to form the temporomandibular joint, so a condylar neck fracture directly disrupts this articulation. The zygomatic arch and pterygoid plate are related structures but do not articulate with the condyle itself. The coronoid process of the opposite ramus has no joint relationship at all. Understanding this articulation explains why condylar fractures can alter occlusion and jaw movement.
  5. The correct answer is B (The maxilla): this paired bone forms the orbital floor, the lateral nasal wall and the anterior three-quarters of the hard palate via its palatine process. The palatine and zygomatic bones contribute to nearby regions but do not form all three structures described. The vomer forms part of the nasal septum rather than the palate or orbit. Recognising the maxilla's multiple contributions is essential for understanding midface anatomy.
  6. The correct answer is A (The maxillary sinus floor lies close to the roots of the upper premolars and molars): inflammation of the sinus mucosa can refer pain to these teeth, mimicking toothache even without any dental pathology present. The mandibular canal and greater palatine foramen are unrelated to maxillary sinus pain referral. The zygomatic process does not transmit sinus pressure to the mandible. This relationship explains the clinical phenomenon sometimes called sinus tooth.
  7. The correct answer is D (The coronoid process and the condylar process): the ramus divides superiorly into these two processes, with temporalis inserting on the coronoid process and the condylar process forming the temporomandibular joint. The alveolar and palatine processes belong to the maxilla, not the mandibular ramus. The zygomatic and frontal processes are unrelated maxillary structures. Knowing this division helps explain the different clinical consequences of fractures at each site.
  8. The correct answer is C (The median palatine suture and the transverse palatine suture): the paired palatine processes meet in the midline at the median palatine suture and join the posterior palatine bones at the transverse palatine suture. The sagittal and coronal sutures belong to the neurocranium, not the palate. The zygomaticomaxillary and frontonasal sutures are facial sutures unrelated to palatal fusion. Failure of fusion at these palatal sutures is the anatomical basis of cleft palate.
  9. The correct answer is A (The lingual plate near the third molar region): the lingual nerve lies superficially close to this bone, so trauma here characteristically produces isolated lingual numbness with normal lip sensation. The mandibular foramen and mental foramen relate to the inferior alveolar nerve and would more typically also affect lip sensation. The gonial angle periosteum is not associated with a specific sensory nerve. This superficial course explains why the lingual nerve is vulnerable during third molar surgery.
  10. The correct answer is D (The mandibular foramen): this opening on the medial ramus is where the inferior alveolar neurovascular bundle enters the mandibular canal, making it a key landmark in sagittal split osteotomy planning. The mental foramen is the exit point, not the entry point, of the nerve. The retromolar fossa and coronoid notch are nearby but unrelated landmarks. Correct identification of the mandibular foramen helps avoid neurovascular injury during surgery.
  11. The correct answer is B (Disc displacement with reduction): a reproducible click that disappears once the mouth is fully open reflects the condyle recapturing the disc during opening. Disc displacement without reduction instead causes a locked, limited opening without a click. Osteoarthritis and myofascial pain dysfunction typically present with different symptom patterns, such as crepitus or diffuse muscle tenderness. This distinction is frequently tested because the two disc conditions require different management.
  12. The correct answer is C (Disc displacement without reduction): here the disc remains permanently anterior to the condyle and is never recaptured, producing sudden, painful, limited opening. Disc displacement with reduction instead produces a reproducible click rather than a locked jaw. Bilateral condylar hyperplasia and acute masseteric spasm present with different clinical pictures. Recognising the sudden locked presentation is key to distinguishing this from the reducible form.
  13. The correct answer is D (The TMJ surfaces are covered by fibrocartilage rather than hyaline cartilage): this is one of several features that make the TMJ unusual compared with most synovial joints in the body. Hyaline cartilage covers most other synovial joints but not the TMJ. Saying the joint has no cartilage at all, or only periosteum, is factually incorrect. This fibrocartilaginous covering contributes to the joint's tolerance of repetitive loading during mastication.
  14. The correct answer is B (Rotation first in the lower compartment, then translation in the upper compartment): normal jaw opening begins with rotation of the condyle in the lower joint space, followed by translation of the condyle-disc complex forward along the eminence in the upper space. The reverse order, or simultaneous equal movement in both compartments, does not reflect normal joint mechanics. Restricting rotation to the upper compartment alone is also incorrect. This sequence explains the biomechanics of normal mouth opening.
  15. The correct answer is C (The superior head of lateral pterygoid): this muscle head attaches to both the joint capsule and the anterior articular disc, directly linking its contraction to disc position and internal derangement. The inferior head of lateral pterygoid instead inserts mainly on the condylar pterygoid fovea. Medial pterygoid and the posterior fibres of temporalis have no direct disc attachment. This relationship underlies much of the pathophysiology of TMJ disc displacement.
  16. The correct answer is C (The stylomandibular ligament): this ligament is classically described as limiting excessive protrusion of the mandible, so laxity here can allow the condyle to translate too far forward and become locked open. The sphenomandibular ligament is instead an accessory ligament that primarily limits excessive inferior displacement of the mandible during wide opening, not protrusion. The temporomandibular ligament limits posterior and lateral movement of the condyle. The pterygomandibular raphe is a fascial structure, not a restraining ligament of the joint.
  17. The correct answer is C (The TMJ functions bilaterally as a single unit despite being two separate joints): movement at one TMJ necessarily affects the other because the mandible is a single rigid bone connecting both joints. Describing the joints as entirely independent, or as only one side driving movement, misrepresents this coupled relationship. Interaction is not limited to forced protrusive movements alone. This bilateral coupling is a key reason TMJ dysfunction can present asymmetrically yet still involve both sides functionally.
  18. The correct answer is A (It allows the disc to conform to convex surfaces on both the condyle and the eminence): the biconcave shape lets the disc sit snugly between two convex bony surfaces during joint movement. It does not prevent condylar movement, fuse to the condyle, or serve as an attachment for facial muscles. This shape is essential for normal, smooth translation and rotation during jaw function. Loss of this normal shape or position underlies internal derangement of the joint.
  19. The correct answer is D (Masseter): this powerful elevator of the mandible arises from the zygomatic arch and inserts on the ramus and angle, and it bulges visibly on clenching, making it easy to palpate. Buccinator is a muscle of facial expression, not mastication, and does not bulge in this way. Medial and lateral pterygoid lie deep and cannot be palpated externally at the angle. This simple bedside test is a practical way to assess bruxism or muscular hypertrophy.
  20. The correct answer is B (Lateral pterygoid): unlike the other muscles of mastication, lateral pterygoid is the principal muscle producing both protrusion and depression of the mandible. Temporalis and masseter are primarily elevators rather than depressors. Medial pterygoid assists elevation and protrusion but is not the principal depressor. This unusual dual action distinguishes lateral pterygoid from the other three muscles of mastication.
  21. The correct answer is A (Unilateral contraction of lateral pterygoid): contraction of lateral pterygoid on one side produces contralateral lateral excursion, forming the basis of grinding movements during chewing. Bilateral contraction of temporalis or masseter instead produces elevation rather than lateral excursion. Relaxation of medial pterygoid alone does not actively produce this movement. This unilateral action is central to understanding normal masticatory function.
  22. The correct answer is C (Medial pterygoid): this muscle runs in a sling arrangement with masseter around the angle of the mandible and assists in elevation, protrusion and grinding movements. Lateral pterygoid instead attaches near the condyle and disc, not in this sling arrangement. Temporalis and buccinator do not form this sling with masseter. This anatomical arrangement helps explain how the two muscles work together at the mandibular angle.
  23. The correct answer is D (The posterior horizontal fibres): these fibres of temporalis run more horizontally and are responsible for retruding the mandible after protrusion. The anterior vertical fibres instead elevate the mandible rather than retrude it. The inferior and medial fibres described in the other options are not the primary retrusive fibres of temporalis. This fibre arrangement allows temporalis to contribute to several different jaw movements.
  24. The correct answer is B (The facial nerve supplies facial expression muscles while the trigeminal nerve supplies sensation): this explains why facial nerve palsy causes loss of movement, such as inability to close the eye, while facial sensation remains normal. Reversing the nerve roles, as in the first option, is incorrect. Both nerves being equally damaged, or the glossopharyngeal nerve controlling eyelid closure, do not fit this classic clinical picture. This distinction is a frequently tested contrast between motor and sensory innervation of the face.
  25. The correct answer is D (Buccinator): this muscle compresses the cheek against the teeth, keeping food on the occlusal table and helping prevent cheek biting during mastication. Orbicularis oris and mentalis act mainly on the lips and chin rather than the cheek itself. Masseter is a muscle of mastication with no role in cheek compression. Temporary paralysis of buccinator after local anaesthetic explains why cheek biting can occur until normal function returns.
  26. The correct answer is B (Orbicularis oris): this muscle encircles the mouth and is essential for closing the lips and achieving a competent lip seal. Buccinator compresses the cheek rather than closing the lips. Zygomaticus major elevates the corner of the mouth during smiling, and temporalis is a muscle of mastication, not facial expression. Damage to orbicularis oris therefore directly impairs the ability to seal the lips.
  27. The correct answer is A (The trigeminal nerve provides sensation while the facial nerve provides movement of facial muscles): this explains why numbness of the lip after third molar surgery does not affect the ability to smile. Reversing these roles, as in some other options, misrepresents the innervation pattern. Attributing both movement and sensation to a single nerve is also incorrect, since these functions are carried by different cranial nerves. This distinction underlies many clinical scenarios involving isolated sensory or motor deficits of the face.
  28. The correct answer is C (The second pharyngeal arch): the muscles of facial expression derive embryologically from this arch, which also explains their shared supply by the facial nerve. The first pharyngeal arch instead gives rise to the muscles of mastication, supplied by the trigeminal nerve. The third and fourth pharyngeal arches contribute to other structures unrelated to facial expression. This embryological origin explains the consistent nerve supply pattern of these muscles.
  29. The correct answer is B (That the mental nerve, and the inferior alveolar trunk proximal to it, are anaesthetised): numbness of the lower lip and chin confirms that solution has reached the mental nerve and therefore the trunk proximal to it, indicating a successful inferior alveolar block. Numbness of the lingual nerve alone would not explain lip and chin numbness. Buccal soft tissue and the greater palatine nerve are unrelated to this specific finding. This clinical sign is routinely used to judge that a block has succeeded before treatment begins.
  30. The correct answer is D (The lingual nerve): its close proximity to the injection site means it is commonly anaesthetised alongside the inferior alveolar nerve, producing numbness and altered taste sensation on that side. The buccal nerve supplies buccal soft tissue rather than the tongue. The auriculotemporal and mental nerves are not responsible for tongue sensation in this scenario. This proximity explains why lingual numbness is a common accompanying finding after an inferior alveolar block.
  31. The correct answer is C (The long buccal nerve): this nerve supplies the buccal mucosa and gingiva adjacent to the mandibular molars, territory not covered by the inferior alveolar nerve, so it must be blocked separately. The lingual nerve supplies the tongue and floor of the mouth rather than buccal tissue. The nasopalatine and greater palatine nerves supply palatal regions, not the buccal mandibular area. This is why a long buccal block is often given alongside an inferior alveolar block.
  32. The correct answer is A (Greater palatine nerve block): this nerve exits at the greater palatine foramen, typically near the second or third maxillary molar, making it the correct choice for anaesthetising the posterior hard palate. The nasopalatine nerve instead supplies the anterior palate near the incisive foramen. The long buccal and infraorbital nerve blocks target different, non-palatal regions. Selecting the correct block depends on matching the surgical site to the nerve's known exit point.
  33. The correct answer is C (Nasopalatine nerve): this nerve travels through the incisive canal and emerges at the incisive foramen, supplying the anterior hard palate from canine to canine. The greater palatine nerve instead supplies the posterior palate. The posterior superior alveolar and infraorbital nerves supply different regions of the maxilla and are unrelated to the anterior palate. This is why the nasopalatine nerve is the correct choice for anterior palatal soft tissue procedures.
  34. The correct answer is B (The middle superior alveolar nerve): when present, this nerve supplies the premolars and the mesiobuccal root of the first molar, a root not always covered by the posterior superior alveolar nerve. The nasopalatine and greater palatine nerves supply palatal tissue rather than this specific root. The lingual nerve is a mandibular branch and has no relevance to a maxillary molar. Awareness of this variable supply is important for achieving full anaesthesia of the maxillary first molar.
  35. The correct answer is A (About 50 to 70 percent): the middle superior alveolar nerve is present in only this proportion of individuals, with its territory covered by neighbouring branches when absent. Figures such as 5 to 10 percent, nearly 100 percent, or less than 1 percent do not reflect the documented prevalence of this nerve. Knowing this variability helps explain why anaesthesia of the maxillary premolars can sometimes require supplementary technique. This anatomical variation is a commonly tested detail of maxillary innervation.
  36. The correct answer is D (The mental nerve): this terminal branch of the inferior alveolar nerve exits at the mental foramen and supplies the lower lip and chin skin. The incisive nerve instead continues within the bone to supply the anterior mandibular teeth, not the overlying skin. The lingual nerve supplies the tongue, and the long buccal nerve supplies buccal soft tissue at the molars. Persistent lip and chin numbness without tongue involvement is therefore explained by the mental nerve's distribution.
  37. The correct answer is D (The mandibular division (V3)): this is the only division of the trigeminal nerve carrying motor fibres, supplying the muscles of mastication along with several other muscles. The ophthalmic and maxillary divisions are purely sensory. Claiming all three divisions carry motor fibres equally is incorrect, since only V3 has this motor component. This unique motor role explains why V3 lesions can affect both sensation and jaw movement.
  38. The correct answer is A (The chorda tympani branch of the facial nerve): this branch travels within the lingual nerve and carries taste sensation, separate from the general sensory fibres of the trigeminal nerve. The glossopharyngeal and hypoglossal nerves are not responsible for taste sensation carried within the lingual nerve. The auriculotemporal nerve is a separate branch of the mandibular division unrelated to taste. This distinction explains why taste can be affected differently from general sensation after lingual nerve trauma.
  39. The correct answer is B (Foramen rotundum): the maxillary nerve (V2) exits the skull through this foramen before crossing the pterygopalatine fossa. Foramen ovale is instead used by the mandibular division (V3). Foramen spinosum transmits the middle meningeal artery, and foramen magnum transmits the spinal cord, neither carrying the maxillary nerve. Correct identification of this pathway is important for understanding the course of V2 toward the dental structures it supplies.
  40. The correct answer is C (The middle superior alveolar nerve): when present, this nerve most often supplies the mesiobuccal root of the first molar, since the posterior superior alveolar nerve frequently does not reach this root. The greater palatine and nasopalatine nerves supply palatal tissue rather than this root. The anterior superior alveolar nerve supplies the incisors and canine instead. Awareness of this variable supply helps explain occasional incomplete anaesthesia during root canal treatment of this tooth.
  41. The correct answer is B (The middle meningeal artery): this branch of the first part of the maxillary artery passes through the foramen spinosum to supply the dura, and its rupture after a temporal bone fracture classically causes an extradural haematoma. The facial and lingual arteries do not supply the dura mater. The posterior superior alveolar artery supplies dental structures rather than intracranial tissue. This vascular relationship is a well recognised cause of a neurosurgical emergency following head trauma.
  42. The correct answer is C (The facial artery): this artery crosses the inferior border of the mandible at the anterior edge of masseter, a site where its pulse can be palpated. The lingual artery passes deep to hyoglossus and is not palpable at this location. The maxillary and superficial temporal arteries follow different courses unrelated to this specific landmark. This palpable pulse point is a useful clinical reference in the head and neck examination.
  43. The correct answer is D (The posterior superior alveolar artery runs alongside the nerve at this site): because the artery and nerve travel together in the pterygopalatine fossa region, aspiration before injection reduces the risk of inadvertent intravascular injection. Claiming no vessels are present at this site is incorrect. The mandibular canal and facial artery are not relevant to this particular injection site. This anatomical relationship is a key reason aspiration is emphasised for posterior superior alveolar blocks.
  44. The correct answer is A (The maxillary artery in relation to lateral pterygoid): the maxillary artery is conventionally divided into three parts by its relationship to this muscle and supplies almost the entire upper and lower dentition. The lingual artery relates to hyoglossus and the facial artery to buccinator in different anatomical contexts, not this dental supply. The superficial temporal artery and temporalis are unrelated to this description. This division helps clinicians understand the course of branches supplying the teeth.
  45. The correct answer is A (Facial veins are valveless and communicate with the cavernous sinus via ophthalmic veins): this anatomical feature allows infection from the danger triangle to spread retrogradely and cause cavernous sinus thrombosis, presenting with proptosis, chemosis and ophthalmoplegia. Facial veins do not have competent valves directing safe flow to the heart. They are not confined solely to the external jugular vein, and the pterygopalatine venous plexus does have intracranial connections. This valveless drainage is the anatomical basis for treating danger triangle infections with particular caution.
  46. The correct answer is D (It is the danger triangle, with valveless veins draining toward the cavernous sinus): infection in this region can spread via valveless facial veins toward the cavernous sinus, risking a life-threatening thrombosis. This area does not lie over the parotid gland or the mandibular canal. It is not supplied entirely by the internal carotid artery either. Recognising this danger zone justifies a low threshold for referral of infections arising here.
  47. The correct answer is C (The lingual artery): this branch of the external carotid artery passes deep to hyoglossus to supply the tongue and floor of the mouth. The facial artery follows a different course across the face rather than deep to hyoglossus. The maxillary artery supplies the deep face and dentition, and the ascending pharyngeal artery supplies the pharynx, neither matching this specific course. Knowledge of this deep course is relevant to surgery involving the tongue and floor of mouth.
  48. The correct answer is B (The external carotid mainly supplies the face, scalp and oral cavity while the internal mainly supplies the brain and orbit): this is the correct contrast between the two carotid territories relevant to dentistry. Reversing these roles, as in the first option, is incorrect. Claiming both arteries supply the oral cavity equally, or that the internal carotid gives rise to the inferior alveolar artery, misrepresents their separate distributions. This distinction underlies why dental local anaesthesia and vascular complications relate almost exclusively to external carotid branches.
  49. The correct answer is C (Its firm, fixed, non-tender character): this combination of features in a lymph node accompanying a chronic oral ulcer should raise suspicion of malignancy rather than simple infection. Tenderness, softness and mobility are instead more typical of reactive infective nodes. The node's location in a particular level does not by itself indicate malignancy. Recognising this clinical pattern is essential for prompt referral of suspicious oral lesions.
  50. The correct answer is A (The submental nodes): level Ia comprises these nodes, lying between the anterior bellies of digastric and draining the lower lip, anterior floor of mouth and chin. The submandibular nodes instead form level Ib, a different subdivision. The upper jugular and posterior triangle nodes belong to different levels entirely. Knowing this subdivision helps in staging and describing regional lymphatic drainage accurately.
  51. The correct answer is B (Level Ib): these submandibular nodes lie within the submandibular triangle and drain most of the oral cavity, the anterior nasal cavity and the submandibular gland region itself. Level II lies along the upper internal jugular vein and drains a different combination of structures. Levels IV and VI relate to more distant regions of the neck. This level is one of the most clinically relevant to general dental practice.
  52. The correct answer is D (Level II): these upper jugular nodes lie along the upper third of the internal jugular vein and receive drainage from the oral cavity, nasopharynx, oropharynx and parotid gland. Level I nodes lie more anteriorly in the submental and submandibular regions. Levels V and VI drain different, more posterior or central regions of the neck. Level II is one of the most commonly palpable node groups in dental infection and early oral cancer spread.
  53. The correct answer is D (Level VI): this central compartment contains the pretracheal, paratracheal and perithyroid nodes, relevant to staging thyroid and related malignancies. Levels III, IV and V lie more laterally along the jugular chain or posterior triangle rather than centrally. Recognising level VI is important in surgical oncology staging of the neck. This central grouping is distinct from the lateral neck levels more familiar in routine dental practice.
  54. The correct answer is C (Levels I and II): these are the most commonly palpable and clinically relevant node groups for a general dentist, since they respond most often to dental infection and early oral cancer spread. Levels III and IV, and levels V and VI, are less directly relevant to everyday dental assessment. This focus on levels I and II reflects their proximity to the oral cavity and jaws. Understanding this relevance helps prioritise clinical examination of the neck in dental patients.
  55. The correct answer is A (Reactive infective nodes are typically tender, mobile and softer than malignant nodes): this pattern helps distinguish a benign infective response, as seen with acute pulpitis, from a malignant node. Describing reactive nodes as firm, fixed and non-tender instead reflects the malignant pattern. Malignant nodes are not always tender, and there is a reliable, if not absolute, clinical distinction between the two. This contrast is a key part of assessing cervical lymphadenopathy in dental patients.
  56. The correct answer is B (Level IV): these lower jugular nodes drain the hypopharynx, larynx, thyroid and cervical oesophagus, lying along the lower third of the internal jugular vein. Level II lies more superiorly along the same vein, while level III lies between levels II and IV. Level V lies in the posterior triangle rather than along the jugular vein. This level is particularly relevant to staging laryngeal and thyroid malignancy.
  57. The correct answer is A (The submandibular duct): this duct, also called Wharton's duct, is the salivary duct most commonly affected by sialolithiasis, producing painful swelling that worsens at mealtimes. The parotid duct and ducts of Rivinus are less commonly affected by stones due to their different anatomical features. The nasolacrimal duct is unrelated to salivary flow. This susceptibility explains the classic mealtime-related presentation of submandibular gland stones.
  58. The correct answer is B (It is long, runs uphill against gravity and narrows to a relatively narrow orifice): these three features together make Wharton's duct particularly prone to stone formation and obstruction, since the narrow orifice impedes passage of any stone that forms along the duct's course. Describing the duct as short, downhill and narrow throughout is the opposite of its true anatomy. The duct does not produce only serous secretion under high pressure, and it does lie within the floor of the mouth rather than outside it. This combination of anatomical features explains its high rate of sialolithiasis compared with other salivary ducts.
  59. The correct answer is D (The sublingual gland): trauma to one of its multiple small ducts near the floor of the mouth can cause a mucous extravasation cyst known as a ranula. The parotid and submandibular glands have different duct anatomy less associated with this presentation. A minor labial salivary gland would not typically present as a floor of mouth swelling in this location. Recognising the sublingual gland's duct anatomy explains the characteristic appearance and location of a ranula.
  60. The correct answer is C (The facial nerve): this nerve divides into five terminal branches within the substance of the parotid gland, making it vulnerable during parotid surgery. The trigeminal nerve does not pass through the parotid gland in this way. The glossopharyngeal and hypoglossal nerves are also not at risk from within the gland itself. This anatomical relationship explains why facial nerve injury is a major recognised risk of parotidectomy.
  61. The correct answer is C (The parotid duct (Stensen's duct)): this duct pierces buccinator and opens as a small papilla on the buccal mucosa opposite the maxillary second molar. The sublingual caruncle and ducts of Rivinus open in the floor of the mouth rather than the cheek. The nasolacrimal duct is unrelated to the oral cavity entirely. Knowing this landmark helps clinicians correctly identify normal versus abnormal findings on the buccal mucosa.
  62. The correct answer is D (The parotid produces a watery, serous secretion): this distinguishes it from the mixed seromucous submandibular gland and the predominantly mucous sublingual gland. Saying the parotid produces mucous secretion is therefore incorrect, as is claiming it produces no secretion at rest. Describing it as identical to the submandibular gland ignores their different secretion types. This distinction in secretion type reflects the underlying histology of each major salivary gland.
  63. The correct answer is B (Mylohyoid): the submandibular gland lies partly above and partly below this muscle within the submandibular triangle. Buccinator and masseter are unrelated to this specific positional relationship. The anterior belly of digastric alone does not define this gland's position relative to the muscle described. This relationship to mylohyoid is important when considering surgical access to the submandibular gland.
  64. The correct answer is A (The sublingual gland): unlike the other major salivary glands, it has multiple small ducts, the ducts of Rivinus, opening directly into the floor of the mouth. The submandibular gland instead drains through a single main duct, Wharton's duct. The parotid gland drains through Stensen's duct, and a minor palatal gland would not fit this description of a major gland. This multiple-duct arrangement is a distinguishing feature of the sublingual gland.
  65. The correct answer is D (The permanent first molar (16, 26, 36 or 46)): this tooth is usually the first permanent tooth to erupt, appearing distal to the second primary molar without any preceding tooth loss, at around six years of age. The permanent second premolar, canine and second molar erupt later in the sequence and are typically preceded by loss of a primary tooth. This is why the first permanent molar is sometimes mistakenly overlooked as a baby tooth. Recognising this eruption pattern helps parents and clinicians anticipate normal dental development.
  66. The correct answer is A (The permanent lower left first molar): in FDI notation, the first digit 3 denotes the lower left permanent quadrant and the second digit 6 denotes the first molar position. The permanent upper left first molar would instead be coded 26. A primary tooth would use a first digit of 5 to 8, not 3. The permanent lower right first molar would be coded 46. Correct interpretation of FDI codes is essential for accurate radiographic and clinical documentation.
  67. The correct answer is C (Around 6 to 10 months): the primary lower central incisors are typically the first primary teeth to erupt, within this age range. The other listed ranges correspond to later primary teeth, such as lateral incisors, canines or second molars. This early eruption is why parents often first notice teething symptoms around this age. Understanding typical eruption timing helps distinguish normal variation from delayed dental development.
  68. The correct answer is B (The third molars): these wisdom teeth typically erupt last, around 17 to 21 years of age, with highly variable timing and a high rate of impaction or absence. The maxillary canines, second premolars and second molars all erupt earlier in the permanent sequence. This late and variable eruption explains why third molars are frequently assessed separately for surgical management. Recognising this typical timing helps clinicians counsel patients appropriately.
  69. The correct answer is B (The primary upper right second molar): in FDI notation, the first digit 5 denotes the primary upper right quadrant and the second digit 5 denotes the second molar position. The permanent upper right second molar would instead be coded 17. A primary lower left tooth would use a first digit of 7, and a primary upper left tooth would use a first digit of 6. Correct interpretation of primary dentition codes avoids confusion in mixed dentition charting.
  70. The correct answer is A (The mandibular central incisors): these typically erupt at around six to seven years of age, alongside the first permanent molars, making them among the earliest permanent teeth to appear. The second premolars and maxillary canines erupt considerably later in the sequence. The maxillary first premolars also erupt after the mandibular central incisors. Recognising this timing helps assess whether a mixed dentition is developing normally.
  71. The correct answer is D (Ludwig's angina): the combination of rapidly spreading, firm, bilateral submandibular swelling with tongue elevation and breathing difficulty is characteristic of this life-threatening bilateral cellulitis. A simple periapical abscess and uncomplicated pericoronitis do not typically produce this severity or bilateral spread. Sialolithiasis presents with obstructive, mealtime-related swelling rather than a spreading cellulitis. Prompt recognition of Ludwig's angina is essential given its risk of airway obstruction.
  72. The correct answer is C (The mylohyoid line): because the roots of the mandibular second and third molars typically lie below this landmark, infection perforating the lingual plate here tracks into the submandibular rather than sublingual space. The mental foramen and retromolar fossa are unrelated to this specific spread pattern. The external oblique ridge lies on the outer surface of the mandible and does not determine this lingual spread pattern. Understanding this landmark is essential for predicting the direction of odontogenic infection spread.
  73. The correct answer is A (Tooth 16): the image shows the upper right quadrant with tooth 16, the first permanent molar, marked with an occlusal restoration. Tooth 14 and tooth 24 are premolars in different quadrants, and tooth 26 lies in the opposite, upper left quadrant. Correct identification of tooth position by FDI notation is essential for accurate charting from an image. Recognising the restored occlusal surface also helps confirm the tooth's identity on the chart.
  74. The correct answer is C (Tooth 36): the image shows the lower left quadrant with tooth 36, the permanent first molar, marked as missing, reflecting its frequent early loss to caries in many populations. Tooth 31 and tooth 33 are incisor and canine positions rather than the first molar. Tooth 38, the third molar, is a different, more distal position in the same quadrant. This pattern of early first molar loss is a well recognised finding in general dental practice.
  75. The correct answer is B (The maxillary second molar): the parotid duct opens on the buccal mucosa opposite this tooth, visible in the image as a small papilla near tooth 17. The maxillary first premolar, lateral incisor and canine are unrelated to this specific anatomical landmark. Correctly locating this papilla is useful when examining the buccal mucosa for normal versus abnormal findings. This landmark also helps clinicians orient themselves during intraoral photography and examination.
  76. The correct answer is D (The submandibular duct): the sublingual caruncle, visible beside the lingual frenum in the image, is the opening point of this duct, also known as Wharton's duct. The parotid duct instead opens on the buccal mucosa, not the floor of the mouth. The ducts of Rivinus open elsewhere in the floor of the mouth via multiple small openings rather than a single caruncle. The nasolacrimal duct is unrelated to the oral cavity. This landmark is clinically relevant when assessing for sialolithiasis.
  77. The correct answer is D (The root cementum and dentine): gingival recession, as shown in the image, exposes the root surface, placing cementum and the underlying dentine at risk of sensitivity and further attachment loss. The pulp chamber is not directly exposed by recession alone unless the recession is severe or combined with other pathology. The enamel of the crown is unrelated to root exposure, and recession does not primarily threaten the periodontal ligament of an adjacent, unaffected tooth. Recognising this risk supports early management of localised recession.
  78. The correct answer is B (A first digit of 5 to 8): in FDI notation, primary teeth use quadrant digits 5 to 8, distinguishing them from permanent teeth which use digits 1 to 4, as shown by the primary tooth still present in the image. The second digit describes tooth position rather than dentition type, so the other options describing second digit ranges are incorrect. Recognising this quadrant coding is essential for correctly charting a mixed dentition. This distinction avoids confusing primary and permanent teeth on a radiograph or chart.
  79. The correct answer is C (The generalised erythema and oedematous gingival margins): these findings, visible in the image, indicate active gingival inflammation rather than simple staining. Extrinsic brown stain and calculus deposits are contributing factors but are not themselves signs of active inflammation. The natural colour of healthy attached gingiva is the opposite of what is shown in this image. Recognising erythema and oedema as inflammatory signs is fundamental to periodontal assessment.
  80. The correct answer is A (Tooth 46): the image shows the lower right quadrant with tooth 46, the permanent first molar, marked with a fully restored occlusal surface. Tooth 36 lies in the opposite, lower left quadrant, while teeth 16 and 26 are upper arch teeth entirely. Correct use of FDI notation ensures the restored tooth is identified accurately from the image. This kind of image-based identification task reflects real radiographic and clinical charting skills.
EMQ Set EMQ-anatomy-1

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely nerve from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Inferior alveolar nerve

B. Lingual nerve

C. Long buccal nerve

D. Mental nerve

E. Nasopalatine nerve

F. Greater palatine nerve

G. Posterior superior alveolar nerve

H. Infraorbital nerve

  1. The correct answer is B (Lingual nerve): permanent numbness of the anterior two-thirds of the tongue, floor of the mouth and lingual gingiva with normal lip sensation is the classic pattern of lingual nerve injury during difficult third molar surgery. The inferior alveolar and mental nerves would instead affect lip and chin sensation. The long buccal nerve supplies buccal soft tissue rather than the tongue. This superficial course near the lingual plate explains the nerve's vulnerability during this procedure.
  2. The correct answer is D (Mental nerve): persistent numbness confined to the lower lip and chin skin, with the tongue entirely unaffected, points specifically to injury of this terminal branch near the implant site. The lingual and long buccal nerves supply different territories not matching this presentation. While the inferior alveolar nerve trunk lies proximal to the mental nerve, the isolated lip and chin distribution described here is the mental nerve's own specific territory. This localisation helps guide investigation of nerve injury after posterior mandibular implant placement.
  3. The correct answer is C (Long buccal nerve): numbness confined to the buccal mucosa and gingiva adjacent to the mandibular molars matches the distribution of this nerve, often blocked alongside a standard mandibular injection. The inferior alveolar and lingual nerves supply the teeth and tongue respectively, not this buccal soft tissue. The mental nerve supplies lip and chin skin rather than buccal gingiva at the molars. This is why the long buccal nerve requires separate anaesthesia before molar extraction.
  4. The correct answer is E (Nasopalatine nerve): this nerve emerges at the incisive foramen just palatal to the central incisors and supplies the anterior hard palate, making it the correct choice for biopsy of tissue near the incisive papilla. The greater palatine nerve instead supplies the posterior palate. The inferior alveolar and mental nerves relate to the mandible, not the palate. Selecting the correct nerve depends on matching the surgical site to its known anatomical exit point.
  5. The correct answer is G (Posterior superior alveolar nerve): this nerve branches off in the pterygopalatine fossa and supplies the maxillary molars, adjacent buccal gingiva and the maxillary sinus mucosa, matching the extraction scenario described. The greater palatine and nasopalatine nerves supply palatal tissue rather than the molars themselves. The infraorbital nerve supplies more anterior maxillary structures. This branch is routinely anaesthetised for maxillary molar extractions.
EMQ Set EMQ-anatomy-2

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely muscle from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Temporalis

B. Masseter

C. Medial pterygoid

D. Lateral pterygoid

E. Buccinator

F. Orbicularis oris

G. Mentalis

H. Mylohyoid

  1. The correct answer is D (Lateral pterygoid): the superior head of this muscle attaches to both the joint capsule and the anterior TMJ disc, directly linking its action to the click described. Masseter and medial pterygoid have no direct disc attachment. Temporalis contributes to elevation and retrusion but not to disc position in this way. This relationship is central to understanding TMJ internal derangement.
  2. The correct answer is B (Masseter): this muscle bulges over the angle of the mandible on firm clenching, making it easy to palpate as a bedside test for bruxism or hypertrophy. Temporalis and medial pterygoid are not palpable at this specific external location in the same way. Lateral pterygoid lies deep within the infratemporal fossa and cannot be palpated externally. This simple clinical test is widely used in general dental assessment.
  3. The correct answer is E (Buccinator): this muscle compresses the cheek against the teeth, keeping food on the occlusal table, so its temporary paralysis after local anaesthetic explains repeated cheek biting. The muscles of mastication listed as other options do not perform this cheek-compressing function. Orbicularis oris and mentalis act on the lips and chin rather than the cheek. This is a classic and clinically relevant temporary side effect of facial nerve or local anaesthetic-related muscle weakness.
  4. The correct answer is F (Orbicularis oris): damage to this muscle of facial expression prevents a competent lip seal, leading to persistent drooling from an anteriorly open mouth posture. Buccinator instead compresses the cheek rather than sealing the lips. The muscles of mastication listed have no role in lip closure. This presentation reflects the essential role of orbicularis oris in maintaining oral competence.
  5. The correct answer is H (Mylohyoid): this muscle attaches along the mylohyoid line and functionally separates the sublingual space above from the submandibular space below. The muscles of mastication and facial expression listed do not have this specific fascial-space-defining role. This separation is central to predicting how odontogenic infection spreads from mandibular teeth. Recognising mylohyoid's role helps explain the different clinical presentations of sublingual versus submandibular space infection.
EMQ Set EMQ-anatomy-3

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely fascial space from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Buccal space

B. Sublingual space

C. Submandibular space

D. Submental space

E. Pterygomandibular space

F. Canine space

G. Infraorbital space

H. Parapharyngeal space

  1. The correct answer is C (Submandibular space): because the roots of the mandibular second molar typically lie below the mylohyoid line, infection perforating the lingual plate here tracks below mylohyoid into this space, producing swelling below the lower border of the mandible. The sublingual space instead lies above mylohyoid and is associated with teeth whose roots lie above this line. The buccal, submental and other spaces listed do not match this specific root position and swelling pattern. This distinction is central to predicting the direction of odontogenic infection spread.
  2. The correct answer is B (Sublingual space): because the roots of the lower canine typically lie above the mylohyoid line, infection perforating the lingual plate here spreads into this space above mylohyoid, elevating the tongue. The submandibular space instead relates to infection from teeth whose roots lie below the mylohyoid line. The buccal and other listed spaces do not match this lingual, tongue-elevating presentation. This distinction helps explain why anterior versus posterior mandibular teeth produce different swelling patterns.
  3. The correct answer is E (Pterygomandibular space): infection or irritation in this space, close to medial pterygoid, classically produces marked trismus, and it is associated with spread following inferior alveolar nerve block or infected lower third molars. The buccal and submandibular spaces produce more visible facial or submandibular swelling rather than isolated trismus. The sublingual space involves floor of mouth swelling rather than this deep trismus pattern. Recognising this space explains why some third molar infections present primarily with restricted opening rather than obvious swelling.
  4. The correct answer is A (Buccal space): infection from a maxillary premolar perforating the buccal cortical plate above the buccinator attachment spreads into this space, producing facial cheek swelling. The sublingual and submandibular spaces relate to mandibular rather than maxillary tooth infection. The pterygomandibular space is associated with a different pattern involving trismus rather than cheek swelling. This spread pattern reflects the relationship between the perforation site and the buccinator attachment described in the theory.
  5. The correct answer is H (Parapharyngeal space): severe untreated odontogenic infection can spread through this space toward the mediastinum, a rare but life-threatening deep neck complication. The buccal, sublingual and submandibular spaces are more superficial and do not typically progress to mediastinal spread. Recognising the parapharyngeal space's deep connections explains why some deep neck infections require urgent surgical and airway management. This potential for mediastinal spread makes early recognition and treatment essential.
EMQ Set EMQ-anatomy-4

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely local anaesthesia technique from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Inferior alveolar nerve block

B. Long buccal nerve block

C. Lingual nerve block

D. Greater palatine nerve block

E. Nasopalatine nerve block

F. Posterior superior alveolar nerve block

G. Infraorbital nerve block

H. Mental nerve block

  1. The correct answer is D (Greater palatine nerve block): depositing solution near the greater palatine foramen anaesthetises this nerve, which is appropriate for periodontal surgery on the posterior hard palate near the second molar. The nasopalatine nerve block instead targets the anterior palate. The inferior alveolar and long buccal blocks target mandibular structures unrelated to the palate. Selecting the correct block depends on matching the surgical site to the nerve's known exit point.
  2. The correct answer is A (Inferior alveolar nerve block): depositing solution near the mandibular foramen anaesthetises this nerve before it enters the mandibular canal, the standard technique for mandibular molar extraction. The long buccal and lingual nerve blocks target different soft tissue territories and would not alone anaesthetise the tooth itself. The greater palatine and nasopalatine blocks are unrelated to mandibular procedures. This technique is the foundation of mandibular local anaesthesia.
  3. The correct answer is B (Long buccal nerve block): this technique is required to anaesthetise the buccal mucosa and gingiva adjacent to the mandibular molars, since the inferior alveolar block alone does not reach this territory. The lingual nerve block would instead anaesthetise the tongue and floor of the mouth. The greater palatine and nasopalatine blocks target palatal tissue, not buccal mandibular soft tissue. This is why the long buccal block is often given alongside a standard mandibular injection.
  4. The correct answer is E (Nasopalatine nerve block): depositing solution at the incisive foramen anaesthetises this nerve, appropriate for biopsy of the anterior palatal soft tissue near the incisive papilla. The greater palatine nerve block instead targets the posterior palate. The inferior alveolar and mental nerve blocks are unrelated to palatal tissue. Correct technique selection depends on recognising the nasopalatine nerve's specific anterior palatal distribution.
  5. The correct answer is F (Posterior superior alveolar nerve block): anaesthetising this branch in the pterygopalatine fossa is appropriate for extracting maxillary molars, since it supplies these teeth along with the adjacent buccal gingiva and sinus mucosa. The greater palatine and nasopalatine blocks target palatal tissue rather than the molar teeth themselves. The inferior alveolar block is unrelated to maxillary structures. This block is routinely used for maxillary molar extraction procedures.
  6. The correct answer is B (Unstimulated salivary flow is already low and falls further with no stimulation overnight): resting flow is only around 0.3 to 0.4 millilitres per minute, and without the stimulation of eating or swallowing overnight, flow and its bicarbonate buffering fall to their lowest point. This leaves the dentition exposed to acid for prolonged periods with minimal salivary protection. Patients on multiple xerostomic medications are especially vulnerable during this window. This is why intensive fluoride and remineralising measures are targeted specifically at bedtime for such patients.
  7. The correct answer is D (The parotid gland produces a serous secretion rich in alpha-amylase): the parotid is the largest major gland and produces a watery, serous secretion dominated by the starch-digesting enzyme alpha-amylase. The submandibular and sublingual glands instead produce mixed serous and mucous secretions. Recognising these differing secretory profiles helps explain regional variation in saliva composition across the mouth. This distinction is a recurring physiology fact tested on the ORE.
  8. The correct answer is A (Bicarbonate concentration rises steeply with flow rate, improving buffering capacity): stimulated saliva contains far more bicarbonate than resting saliva, giving it much greater capacity to neutralise the acid produced after a sugar exposure. Chewing sugar-free gum increases flow rate and therefore buffering, helping plaque pH recover to baseline faster. This is the biochemical rationale behind recommending gum rather than relying on resting saliva alone. The advice directly targets the caries-promoting acid challenge described by the Stephan curve.
  9. The correct answer is C (It creates a net drive for calcium and phosphate to redeposit into demineralised enamel): supersaturation relative to hydroxyapatite means the ionic conditions favour deposition of mineral back into enamel that lost mineral during an acid challenge. This underlies the physiological process of remineralisation. Salivary proteins keep the ions in solution so this deposition occurs in a controlled way rather than as spontaneous calculus formation. Fluoride further enhances this remineralisation process.
  10. The correct answer is B (Statherin and proline-rich proteins): these salivary proteins stabilise calcium and phosphate ions in solution, preventing uncontrolled precipitation as calculus while still permitting regulated remineralisation at the tooth surface. Without them, the supersaturated state of saliva would risk widespread mineral deposition on any available surface. This balance between stability and controlled deposition is an important salivary function. The other listed proteins serve antimicrobial rather than mineral-stabilising roles.
  11. The correct answer is A (Lysozyme): lysozyme is an enzyme that hydrolyses peptidoglycan within bacterial cell walls, directly damaging susceptible bacteria. It is one of several antimicrobial components of saliva alongside lactoferrin, the lactoperoxidase system and secretory immunoglobulin A. Each of these components works through a distinct mechanism. Lysozyme's cell wall degrading action is the specific mechanism tested here.
  12. The correct answer is D (Secretory immunoglobulin A agglutinates bacteria, preventing adhesion rather than activating complement): unlike circulating immunoglobulin G, secretory immunoglobulin A works mainly by clumping bacteria together and blocking their attachment to tooth and mucosal surfaces. It does not primarily activate the complement cascade in the way immunoglobulin G does. It is the dominant antibody in saliva, produced by plasma cells and transported across the epithelium. This functional distinction is frequently examined.
  13. The correct answer is C (Reduced saliva flow simultaneously impairs buffering, remineralisation and antimicrobial defence): Sjogren syndrome reduces saliva volume across the board, so all of saliva's protective functions decline together rather than in isolation. This combined loss explains why xerostomic patients develop rampant caries, particularly at cervical margins and root surfaces. Understanding this integrated loss of function justifies intensive fluoride and saliva substitute regimes. No single protective mechanism can compensate when overall flow is reduced.
  14. The correct answer is B (Rising intrapulpal pressure compromises venous outflow before arterial inflow is affected): because the pulp sits within rigid dentine walls and drains through only a narrow apical foramen, inflammation raises pressure within a low-compliance space. Venous outflow, being lower pressure, is compromised first, worsening congestion and further raising pressure. This vicious cycle explains why irreversible pulpitis progresses to necrosis rather than resolving. It is a key mechanistic fact tested on the ORE.
  15. The correct answer is D (A-delta fibres, myelinated and concentrated at the pulp-dentine border): these fast-conducting fibres mediate the sharp, well-localised pain typical of dentine stimulation such as cold, and the pain resolves quickly once the stimulus is removed. This is characteristic of reversible pulpitis. C fibres instead produce the dull, throbbing pain of established, irreversible pulpitis. Distinguishing these presentations is clinically important for diagnosis.
  16. The correct answer is A (C fibres, unmyelinated and distributed throughout the pulp body): C fibres mediate dull, poorly localised, throbbing pain once inflammation has reached the pulp core, which is the hallmark of irreversible pulpitis. Their slower conduction and widespread distribution explain the diffuse nature of this pain compared with the sharp localisation of A-delta activation. A patient reporting pain severe enough to disturb sleep is describing this pattern. This distinction guides the decision between conservative management and extirpation.
  17. The correct answer is C (Rapid outward fluid movement in dentinal tubules deforms odontoblasts and nerve terminals): the hydrodynamic theory explains that any stimulus causing rapid tubular fluid movement, including evaporative drying from an air syringe, mechanically deforms the odontoblast layer and associated nerve terminals at the pulp-dentine border. This triggers an action potential in A-delta fibres, producing sharp pain. Exposed root dentine from recession leaves tubules open to such stimuli. This mechanism underlies most clinical dentine sensitivity.
  18. The correct answer is D (Cold and evaporation cause the most rapid outward tubular fluid flow): because these stimuli produce the fastest fluid movement within dentinal tubules, they generate the strongest hydrodynamic stimulus and the most reliable pain response. This is why cold testing is the standard clinical method for assessing pulp vitality. Slower or inward fluid movements produce weaker stimulation. This physiological basis explains the reliability of cold as a diagnostic tool.
  19. The correct answer is B (Potassium nitrate reduces nerve fibre excitability, an effect that accumulates with repeated use): rather than blocking fluid movement, potassium ions diffuse along the tubule to directly reduce the excitability of the nerve fibre, so even continued fluid movement is less likely to trigger depolarisation. Because this effect builds up gradually with repeated application, relief typically takes several weeks to become established. This differs from tubule-occluding agents, which act more quickly. Explaining this timeline helps set realistic patient expectations.
  20. The correct answer is A (Agents containing calcium phosphate compounds, oxalates or fluoride varnish): these agents work by physically occluding dentinal tubules, directly reducing the fluid movement that triggers the hydrodynamic stimulus. Because the mechanism is mechanical rather than requiring gradual nerve adaptation, relief can be more immediate than with potassium-based agents. Selecting between mechanisms allows tailored management of dentine hypersensitivity. Both approaches address the same hydrodynamic pathway from different ends.
  21. The correct answer is C (Undifferentiated mesenchymal cells within the pulp): these cells retain the capacity to differentiate into odontoblast-like cells capable of forming reparative or tertiary dentine when primary odontoblasts are lost, for example following a deep carious insult. This regenerative capacity is a key feature of pulp biology. Fibroblasts and nerve fibres do not share this differentiation potential. This mechanism underlies the pulp's limited capacity for self-repair.
  22. The correct answer is B (Osteoblasts): osteoblasts synthesise the organic bone matrix, chiefly type I collagen along with non-collagenous proteins, and then direct its mineralisation with calcium hydroxyapatite. Osteocytes are their descendants embedded in mature bone, while osteoclasts are resorptive cells of a completely different lineage. This distinction is fundamental to understanding bone remodelling physiology. Recognising osteoblast function underpins later concepts such as the RANKL and osteoprotegerin pathway.
  23. The correct answer is D (Osteocytes): osteocytes are former osteoblasts that become embedded within the mineralised matrix they produced, remaining connected through canaliculi and acting as the principal mechanosensors of bone. They detect mechanical loading and help orchestrate the remodelling response accordingly. This sensory role distinguishes them from the matrix-forming osteoblasts and the resorptive osteoclasts. Their extensive network allows coordinated bone adaptation to mechanical demand.
  24. The correct answer is A (Derived from monocyte-macrophage lineage, resorbing bone via hydrogen ions and cathepsin K): osteoclasts are large multinucleated cells of a distinct haematopoietic origin from osteoblasts, and they resorb bone by secreting hydrogen ions to dissolve mineral and proteolytic enzymes such as cathepsin K to degrade the organic matrix. This is the opposite process to the matrix synthesis performed by osteoblasts. Understanding this origin explains why drugs like denosumab, which target RANKL signalling, can suppress osteoclast activity. This mechanism is central to the bisphosphonate-related osteonecrosis discussion.
  25. The correct answer is C (RANKL produced by osteoblasts binding to RANK on osteoclast precursors): this interaction stimulates the differentiation, activation and survival of osteoclast precursors, directly promoting bone resorption. Osteoprotegerin instead acts as a decoy receptor to block this interaction. The balance between RANKL and osteoprotegerin, rather than either signal alone, determines the net direction of remodelling. This pathway is central to understanding medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw.
  26. The correct answer is D (It acts as a decoy receptor, binding RANKL and preventing it from reaching RANK): osteoprotegerin is produced by osteoblasts and neutralises RANKL by binding it directly, so RANKL cannot activate RANK on osteoclast precursors. This inhibits osteoclastogenesis and shifts the balance of remodelling towards formation. The ratio of RANKL to osteoprotegerin at a given site determines the net remodelling direction. This decoy mechanism is distinct from any direct action on osteoclasts themselves.
  27. The correct answer is A (Inhibition of the mevalonate pathway, impairing osteoclast cytoskeletal function and promoting apoptosis): nitrogen-containing bisphosphonates are taken up by bone and internalised by osteoclasts during resorption, where they block the mevalonate pathway. This disrupts the osteoclast cytoskeleton and promotes cell death, dramatically reducing bone turnover. This suppression underlies the reduced healing capacity seen in medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw. The mechanism is specific to nitrogen-containing agents such as alendronic acid and zoledronic acid.
  28. The correct answer is B (Denosumab directly binds RANKL and its effect reverses over months as it clears): unlike bisphosphonates, which remain bound within bone for years, denosumab is a monoclonal antibody whose action clears from the body over months, allowing RANKL signalling and osteoclast function to gradually recover. Both drugs produce a similar clinical risk of osteonecrosis but through different mechanisms and with different reversibility. This distinction is clinically relevant when counselling patients about extraction timing and risk. It also affects how risk is stratified between the two drug classes.
  29. The correct answer is C (The jaws have unusually high remodelling rates due to masticatory demand and microbial challenge): compared with the rest of the skeleton, the jaws undergo more frequent remodelling because of mechanical loading from chewing and the constant low-grade challenge from the oral microbiome through the periodontium. When antiresorptive drugs suppress this remodelling, the jaw's capacity to repair itself after trauma such as extraction is disproportionately impaired. This explains the site-specific risk of medication-related osteonecrosis. It is a key physiological rationale behind pre-extraction risk assessment in these patients.
  30. The correct answer is D (Systolic reflects ventricular contraction and diastolic reflects arterial recoil during relaxation): systolic pressure is generated during ventricular contraction as blood is ejected into the arteries, while diastolic pressure reflects the arterial recoil that maintains pressure during ventricular relaxation. This distinction underlies the clinical interpretation of blood pressure readings taken before invasive dental procedures. Both values together reflect cardiac output and peripheral vascular resistance. Correctly interpreting them supports safe use of adrenaline-containing local anaesthetic.
  31. The correct answer is B (Reassure the patient and remeasure blood pressure before deciding on management): anxiety commonly elevates blood pressure at the chair, so an isolated raised reading in an anxious patient should prompt reassurance and remeasurement rather than immediate treatment refusal. Sustained severe hypertension, by contrast, should prompt referral rather than proceeding with elective treatment. This balanced approach avoids both unnecessary treatment delay and inappropriate risk-taking. It reflects the practical application of blood pressure physiology in a dental setting.
  32. The correct answer is A (Von Willebrand factor): this molecule bridges platelet glycoprotein receptors to exposed subendothelial collagen following vessel injury, mediating initial platelet adhesion. Fibrinogen instead bridges platelets to one another during aggregation via glycoprotein IIb/IIIa receptors. This sequential adhesion followed by aggregation forms the primary platelet plug. Deficiency of von Willebrand factor produces a bleeding disorder affecting this initial adhesion step.
  33. The correct answer is D (Fibrinogen): fibrinogen bridges platelet glycoprotein IIb/IIIa receptors between adjacent platelets, allowing them to aggregate and form the primary platelet plug after initial adhesion has occurred via von Willebrand factor. This aggregation step reinforces the plug before the coagulation cascade stabilises it further with fibrin. Understanding this sequence clarifies how different antiplatelet drugs act at different points. Clopidogrel, for example, impairs a different receptor pathway that also disrupts aggregation.
  34. The correct answer is C (INR is derived from prothrombin time, reflecting the extrinsic and common pathways): warfarin inhibits hepatic synthesis of vitamin K-dependent clotting factors II, VII, IX and X, and its effect is monitored through the international normalised ratio derived from prothrombin time. This test principally reflects the extrinsic and common pathways because tissue factor and factor VII act early in that route. Direct oral anticoagulants act more predictably and do not require this routine monitoring. This distinction is central to safe management of anticoagulated dental patients.
  35. The correct answer is A (Rivaroxaban directly inhibits factor Xa with a predictable, dose-dependent effect): direct oral anticoagulants such as rivaroxaban and apixaban act directly on factor Xa with a more predictable pharmacokinetic profile than warfarin, removing the need for routine coagulation monitoring. Renal function still requires consideration since several of these agents are renally excreted. This contrasts with warfarin's variable effect on vitamin K-dependent factor synthesis. Understanding this mechanism supports appropriate perioperative management for extractions.
  36. The correct answer is B (Aspirin irreversibly inhibits cyclo-oxygenase, blocking thromboxane A2 production): because platelets cannot synthesise new enzyme once cyclo-oxygenase is inhibited, aspirin's antiplatelet effect persists for the entire lifespan of the affected platelet, roughly seven to ten days. This differs from reversible antiplatelet agents whose effect wanes as the drug is cleared. This irreversibility is relevant when considering bleeding risk timelines before surgery. Current UK guidance nonetheless favours continuing aspirin for routine extractions.
  37. The correct answer is D (Blockade of the platelet ADP receptor): clopidogrel specifically blocks the platelet ADP receptor, impairing the aggregation step of primary platelet plug formation rather than affecting the coagulation cascade itself. This differs mechanistically from aspirin's inhibition of cyclo-oxygenase. Both drugs are generally continued for routine dental extractions under current UK guidance. Recognising the distinct mechanisms helps explain why combining antiplatelet agents increases bleeding risk more than either alone.
  38. The correct answer is C (Continue the medication, attend to dose timing, and use local haemostatic measures): current UK guidance for straightforward extractions favours continuing direct oral anticoagulants, with attention to the timing of the dose relative to the procedure, and managing bleeding locally with measures such as sutures and pressure. This is because the systemic risk of thromboembolic events from stopping the medication generally outweighs the local, manageable bleeding risk. Decisions to interrupt anticoagulation belong to the prescribing physician, not routine dental practice. This principle applies broadly across anticoagulant and antiplatelet classes.
  39. The correct answer is A (Central chemoreceptors respond to arterial carbon dioxide tension via cerebrospinal fluid pH): ventilation in health is primarily driven by central chemoreceptors in the medulla and pons responding to changes in arterial carbon dioxide reflected in cerebrospinal fluid pH. Peripheral chemoreceptors provide only a secondary and normally minor response to hypoxia. This hierarchy explains why carbon dioxide, not oxygen, is the dominant driver of resting breathing. This becomes clinically important in conditions such as chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.
  40. The correct answer is B (Blunted central chemoreceptor sensitivity may leave hypoxic drive relatively more significant): in patients who chronically retain carbon dioxide, central chemoreceptor sensitivity may become blunted over time, theoretically leaving peripheral hypoxic drive as a more significant contributor to ventilation. This is a classic teaching point relevant to cautious titration of oxygen and sedative agents in this group, though it is more nuanced in practice than commonly presented. Genuinely hypoxic patients should never be denied oxygen because of this concern. This balance reflects applied respiratory physiology in sedation practice.
  41. The correct answer is D (Both agents can depress central respiratory drive and reduce airway protective reflexes): both nitrous oxide and benzodiazepines such as midazolam can suppress central respiratory drive and reduce protective airway reflexes at higher doses. This is why continuous pulse oximetry, clinical observation and a second appropriately trained team member are mandatory safeguards during conscious sedation. Recognising this shared risk profile underlies safe sedation practice regardless of the specific agent used. Careful titration helps minimise this risk.
  42. The correct answer is A (Both reduce tone in the pharyngeal musculature and tongue, allowing collapse): airway patency depends on maintained tone in the pharyngeal muscles and tongue, and both the supine position and sedative agents reduce this tone, predisposing to obstruction. This is why careful patient positioning and cautious sedative titration are core safety measures. Readiness to manage an obstructed airway is a required competency for any practitioner providing sedation. This mechanism directly links respiratory physiology to practical sedation safety.
  43. The correct answer is C (Titrating sedative dose to the desired clinical end point rather than a fixed amount): because individual patients vary in their response to sedative agents, titrating dose to a clinical end point rather than administering a fixed amount reduces the risk of over-sedation and respiratory depression. This approach reflects careful application of respiratory physiology to sedation safety. Continuous clinical observation supports this titration process. It is considered core competency for safe sedation practice.
  44. The correct answer is B (Presence of a second appropriately trained team member): alongside pulse oximetry and clinical observation of respiratory rate and depth, having a second appropriately trained team member present is a mandatory safeguard during conscious sedation in UK dental practice. This ensures adequate monitoring capacity and support if respiratory depression or airway obstruction occurs. This requirement reflects the recognised respiratory risks of sedative agents. It is a standard safety requirement rather than an optional precaution.
  45. The correct answer is D (An anxious or medicated patient can decompensate quickly near the airway): even outside formal sedation, local anaesthesia for procedures near the airway demands the same vigilance because an anxious or medicated patient can decompensate rapidly, particularly if the airway is already compromised. This requires the clinician to remain alert to respiratory status throughout the procedure. This principle extends respiratory safety awareness beyond sedation into routine local anaesthetic use. Recognising early signs of decompensation allows timely intervention.
  46. The correct answer is C (Advanced glycation end products impairing neutrophil function and promoting inflammation): chronic hyperglycaemia promotes formation of advanced glycation end products that impair neutrophil chemotaxis and phagocytic function while promoting a pro-inflammatory macrophage phenotype. This impairs the host response to periodontal pathogens, predisposing to more severe periodontitis. This mechanism explains the strong association between poorly controlled diabetes and periodontal disease severity. It also underlies delayed healing seen after dental surgery in these patients.
  47. The correct answer is A (Periodontal inflammation raises systemic tumour necrosis factor alpha, worsening insulin resistance): the relationship between diabetes and periodontal disease runs in both directions, with periodontal inflammation raising systemic inflammatory mediators such as tumour necrosis factor alpha that worsen insulin resistance and glycaemic control. Treating the periodontal disease can therefore produce a measurable improvement in glycated haemoglobin. This bidirectional relationship demonstrates the integration of oral and systemic health. It is a favourite examination theme for this reason.
  48. The correct answer is B (Advanced glycation end products causing microvascular changes that impair tissue perfusion): these products cause microvascular changes that reduce blood supply and impair tissue perfusion, directly delaying wound healing after procedures such as extraction in poorly controlled diabetic patients. This same mechanism explains the increased severity of periodontal disease in these patients. Recognising this risk supports careful perioperative planning for diabetic patients undergoing surgery. Good glycaemic control before elective procedures reduces this risk.
  49. The correct answer is D (Adrenaline should be used cautiously and in limited quantity due to heightened sympathetic sensitivity): hyperthyroid patients have an increased metabolic rate, tachycardia and heightened sympathetic sensitivity, meaning adrenaline-containing local anaesthetic carries a risk of precipitating thyroid storm or significant arrhythmia if used excessively in poorly controlled disease. Cautious, limited use reduces this risk while still allowing effective local anaesthesia. This consideration is specific to poorly controlled thyrotoxicosis rather than well-managed disease. Recognising thyroid status before administering adrenaline is an important safety step.
  50. The correct answer is A (Increased sensitivity to sedative and opioid drugs due to slowed metabolism): hypothyroid patients have a reduced metabolic rate that slows drug clearance, increasing sensitivity to sedative and opioid medications. They may also have delayed wound healing and, in severe uncontrolled cases, an increased risk of myxoedema coma under physiological stress, though this is rare in a properly treated patient. Recognising this altered drug sensitivity supports safer prescribing in dental practice. It contrasts with the heightened sympathetic sensitivity seen in hyperthyroidism.
  51. The correct answer is C (Impaired ability to mount a normal cortisol surge in response to procedural stress must be assessed against invasiveness): patients with suppressed or absent endogenous cortisol production cannot mount the normal physiological stress response to surgery, and current UK guidance risk-stratifies steroid cover decisions based on procedure invasiveness, baseline steroid dose and adequate anxiety and pain control. This replaces the older approach of routine empirical cover for every procedure. Inadequate stress response can precipitate an Addisonian crisis if cover is insufficient for a genuinely invasive procedure. This nuanced approach balances risk against unnecessary steroid exposure.
  52. The correct answer is D (Immediate parenteral hydrocortisone and supportive care for a suspected Addisonian crisis): hypotension, hypoglycaemia and collapse following procedural stress in a steroid-dependent patient are the classic features of an Addisonian crisis, which is a genuine dental emergency requiring immediate parenteral hydrocortisone alongside supportive care. Delayed or inadequate treatment risks serious harm. Recognising this presentation promptly is essential for safe management. This reflects the physiological consequence of inadequate cortisol response to stress.
  53.
