The correct answer is B (Historical guidance recommended routine empirical cover; current guidance is risk-stratified by procedure and dose): older practice recommended doubling the oral steroid dose or giving supplementary hydrocortisone routinely for any surgery in steroid-dependent patients, whereas current UK guidance takes a more nuanced approach based on procedure invasiveness, baseline dose and adequate pain and anxiety control. This shift reflects a better understanding of the actual risk in less invasive procedures. It avoids unnecessary steroid exposure while still protecting higher-risk patients undergoing more invasive treatment. This evolution in guidance is directly examinable.
  54. The correct answer is A (It demonstrates the integration of oral and systemic health that the examination aims to test): the bidirectional relationship between diabetes and periodontal disease shows that oral disease is not isolated from systemic physiology, with each condition capable of influencing the other. This integration reflects exactly the kind of applied physiological reasoning the ORE rewards. Recognising and explaining this relationship demonstrates a candidate's ability to connect basic science with clinical practice. It is why this theme recurs frequently across dental science examinations.
  55. The correct answer is D (Sucrose serves both as an energy source and as a substrate for glucan-forming glucosyltransferase): sucrose is uniquely cariogenic because Streptococcus mutans uses it both for energy through glycolysis and as a substrate for glucosyltransferase, which builds sticky extracellular glucans that increase bacterial adhesion and contribute to plaque structure. This dual role allows sucrose to both feed acid production and construct the protective biofilm architecture around the bacteria producing it. Other sugars lack this glucan-building capacity to the same degree. This explains sucrose's particularly strong association with caries.
  56. The correct answer is D (6.7 to 7.0): at rest, before any sugar challenge, plaque pH sits close to neutral in this range. Following a carbohydrate exposure, pH falls rapidly as bacterial acid production outpaces buffering capacity. This baseline value is the starting point of the Stephan curve. Understanding the resting value helps candidates interpret the magnitude of the subsequent pH drop after a sugar challenge.
  57. The correct answer is B (5.5): this is the critical pH for enamel, below which hydroxyapatite becomes soluble and net demineralisation occurs. Root dentine and cementum have a somewhat higher critical pH, making them vulnerable at less acidic conditions. This threshold is central to understanding the Stephan curve and caries risk. Recognising this value helps explain why frequent acid exposures below this threshold drive net mineral loss.
  58. The correct answer is A (Root dentine and cementum have a higher critical pH, approximately 6.0 to 6.7): because these tissues demineralise at a less acidic pH than enamel, exposed root surfaces following gingival recession are vulnerable to root caries at pH levels that would not threaten intact enamel. This explains the particular caries risk in patients with recession or periodontal attachment loss. Understanding this difference guides targeted preventive advice for such patients. It reflects the differing mineral composition and structure of root surfaces compared with enamel.
  59. The correct answer is C (Limiting the frequency of sugar exposures is more important than limiting total quantity): each sugar exposure triggers a Stephan curve, and frequent exposures keep plaque pH depressed below the critical threshold for longer, overwhelming the capacity for remineralisation between challenges. A single larger exposure produces one manageable curve with adequate recovery time, whereas the same amount grazed throughout the day produces repeated, cumulatively more damaging acid challenges. This is the biochemical justification for advising patients to limit frequency rather than only total quantity. It is a key piece of applied dietary advice tested on the ORE.
  60. The correct answer is D (Fluoride inhibits glycolytic enzymes such as enolase within cariogenic bacteria): beyond promoting fluorapatite formation in enamel, fluoride directly reduces bacterial acid production by inhibiting glycolytic enzymes such as enolase within cariogenic bacteria themselves. This dual action, on both the tooth and the bacteria, contributes to fluoride's overall cariostatic effect. Understanding both mechanisms gives a fuller picture of fluoride's protective role. This is examined alongside the more commonly cited fluorapatite mechanism.
  61. The correct answer is B (Lactic acid): lactic acid is the principal organic acid end product of bacterial glycolysis by cariogenic organisms such as Streptococcus mutans and Lactobacillus species, and it is directly responsible for lowering plaque pH and demineralising enamel. This acid production is the biochemical basis of the Stephan curve. Understanding this pathway links dietary carbohydrate intake to caries development at a molecular level. It underpins the rationale for both fluoride therapy and dietary advice.
  62. The correct answer is A (Fluorapatite has a lower critical pH, tolerating more acidic conditions before demineralising): because fluorapatite is less soluble in acid than native hydroxyapatite, the tooth surface can withstand a more acidic environment before net demineralisation begins, effectively raising the safety margin against everyday acid challenges. This is the biochemical basis for topical fluoride therapy. It complements fluoride's separate action of inhibiting bacterial glycolytic enzymes. Together these mechanisms explain fluoride's overall protective effect against caries.
  63. The correct answer is D (Stimulates bone resorption, increases renal calcium reabsorption and activates vitamin D): parathyroid hormone raises serum calcium by stimulating osteoclastic bone resorption, increasing renal reabsorption of calcium while promoting phosphate excretion, and stimulating renal conversion of vitamin D to its active form, calcitriol. This coordinated action across bone, kidney and indirectly intestine restores calcium levels efficiently. Recognising this multi-organ action distinguishes parathyroid hormone's mechanism from that of calcitriol acting alone. This regulation is essential to understanding calcium homeostasis.
  64. The correct answer is C (Calcitriol): the active form of vitamin D increases intestinal absorption of both calcium and phosphate, working together with parathyroid hormone to mobilise skeletal calcium when dietary intake is insufficient. Parathyroid hormone acts more broadly across bone and kidney, while calcitonin has a comparatively minor physiological role in humans. Understanding calcitriol's specific intestinal action clarifies its distinct contribution to calcium homeostasis. Deficiency of active vitamin D impairs this absorption pathway.
  65. The correct answer is B (Skin synthesis under ultraviolet B, then hepatic hydroxylation, then renal hydroxylation): vitamin D is synthesised in the skin from a cholesterol precursor under ultraviolet B radiation, then undergoes sequential hydroxylation, first in the liver to form the storage form and then in the kidney to form the active hormone calcitriol. Disruption at any step, including renal disease, can impair active vitamin D production. This sequence explains why both sunlight exposure and organ function affect vitamin D status. Recognising this pathway underpins understanding of rickets and osteomalacia.
  66. The correct answer is A (Osteomalacia reflects a failure of mineralisation with matrix production intact, unlike osteoporosis): in osteomalacia, bone matrix continues to be laid down but is inadequately mineralised, whereas osteoporosis involves a proportional reduction in both matrix and mineral. This biochemical distinction, although both conditions result in low bone density, has important diagnostic and treatment implications. Recognising the underlying process guides appropriate investigation and management. This distinction is a classic examinable contrast in bone physiology.
  67. The correct answer is D (Mature enamel cannot remodel once formed, unlike bone): unlike bone, which continuously remodels throughout life, mature enamel has no cellular mechanism for remodelling once formed, so any mineralisation defect occurring during tooth development becomes a permanent feature of the erupted tooth. This explains why vitamin D deficiency or calcium metabolism disorders during odontogenesis can produce lasting enamel hypoplasia or hypomineralisation. Recognising this permanence is important when counselling patients about developmental enamel defects. It contrasts sharply with bone's capacity for ongoing repair.
  68. The correct answer is C (Respiratory alkalosis due to excessive carbon dioxide loss): hyperventilation lowers arterial carbon dioxide, raising blood pH and producing the classic symptoms of light-headedness, circumoral tingling and, in more marked cases, carpopedal spasm. This presentation should be recognised and managed by calming the patient and encouraging slow, controlled breathing rather than treated as a more sinister emergency. Understanding this mechanism prevents unnecessary alarm or inappropriate intervention. It is a commonly tested applied acid-base scenario in dental practice.
  69. The correct answer is B (Metabolic acidosis, potentially diabetic ketoacidosis): a characteristic acetone smell on the breath suggests accumulation of ketone bodies from severe metabolic acidosis, most notably diabetic ketoacidosis in a poorly controlled diabetic patient. A dentist noticing this finding before a medical diagnosis is made should recognise its significance and refer urgently. This is an example of applied physiological reasoning that the ORE expects candidates to demonstrate. Prompt recognition can be genuinely life-saving in this scenario.
  70. The correct answer is A (Lungs compensate within minutes, while renal compensation takes hours to days): the lungs regulate carbon dioxide, and therefore carbonic acid, on a fast timescale of minutes through changes in ventilation, while the kidneys regulate bicarbonate reabsorption and hydrogen ion excretion more slowly over hours to days. Together these mechanisms provide both rapid and sustained compensation for acid-base disturbances. Recognising this difference in timescale helps explain the clinical course of both acute and chronic acid-base disorders. It underpins understanding of conditions ranging from acute hyperventilation to chronic respiratory disease.
EMQ Set EMQ-physio-1

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely physiological mechanism from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Advanced glycation end product accumulation in periodontal tissue

B. Suppressed hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis from long-term corticosteroid therapy

C. Increased RANKL to osteoprotegerin ratio at a remodelling site

D. Blunted central chemoreceptor sensitivity to carbon dioxide

E. Deficiency of active vitamin D impairing intestinal calcium absorption

F. Elevated systemic tumour necrosis factor alpha from periodontal inflammation

G. Heightened sympathetic sensitivity due to increased metabolic rate

H. Impaired hepatic synthesis of vitamin K-dependent clotting factors

  1. The correct answer is G (Heightened sympathetic sensitivity due to increased metabolic rate): hyperthyroid patients have an increased metabolic rate and heightened sympathetic sensitivity, so adrenaline-containing local anaesthetic must be used cautiously and in limited quantity to avoid precipitating thyroid storm or significant arrhythmia. This is a distinct endocrine mechanism from the periodontal or bone-related processes described by the other options. Recognising it guides safe local anaesthetic selection in thyrotoxic patients. It reflects the systemic cardiovascular effects of excess thyroid hormone.
  2. The correct answer is F (Elevated systemic tumour necrosis factor alpha from periodontal inflammation): the bidirectional relationship between diabetes and periodontal disease means periodontal inflammation raises systemic tumour necrosis factor alpha, which worsens insulin resistance and glycaemic control. Treating the periodontal disease can therefore measurably improve glycated haemoglobin. This mechanism runs in the opposite direction to option A, which describes hyperglycaemia damaging periodontal tissue rather than periodontal disease affecting glycaemia. Distinguishing the direction of this relationship is important for this scenario.
  3. The correct answer is E (Deficiency of active vitamin D impairing intestinal calcium absorption): malabsorption impairs vitamin D and calcium uptake, leading to inadequately mineralised bone matrix, which produces the soft, deformed bone and bone pain characteristic of osteomalacia in an adult. This differs from rickets only by the patient's skeletal maturity, not the underlying mechanism. Recognising vitamin D deficiency as the cause guides appropriate correction of the deficiency. This mechanism is distinct from the RANKL-driven bone resorption described elsewhere in this cluster.
  4. The correct answer is B (Suppressed hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis from long-term corticosteroid therapy): long-term corticosteroid use suppresses endogenous cortisol production via feedback inhibition of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, impairing the patient's ability to mount a normal cortisol surge in response to surgical stress. This is the physiological basis for considering steroid cover before invasive dental procedures in such patients. Current UK guidance stratifies this decision by procedure invasiveness and baseline dose. Recognising this mechanism prevents an unrecognised Addisonian crisis.
  5. The correct answer is C (Increased RANKL to osteoprotegerin ratio at a remodelling site): a bone remodelling site favouring resorption over formation reflects a relatively high RANKL to osteoprotegerin ratio, which drives osteoclast differentiation and activity. This physiological imbalance is central to understanding medication-related osteonecrosis risk, since antiresorptive drugs act on this same pathway. Recognising the ratio, rather than the absolute level of either molecule, determines net remodelling direction. This distinguishes it from the other endocrine or metabolic mechanisms in this cluster.
EMQ Set EMQ-physio-2

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely haemostatic mechanism from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Von Willebrand factor mediated platelet adhesion to collagen

B. Fibrinogen mediated platelet aggregation via glycoprotein IIb/IIIa

C. Vitamin K-dependent factors II, VII, IX and X

D. Direct inhibition of factor Xa

E. Direct inhibition of thrombin

F. Irreversible inhibition of cyclo-oxygenase and thromboxane A2 production

G. Blockade of the platelet ADP receptor

H. Conversion of fibrinogen to fibrin by thrombin in the common pathway

  1. The correct answer is C (Vitamin K-dependent factors II, VII, IX and X): warfarin inhibits hepatic synthesis of these clotting factors, prolonging the prothrombin time and therefore the international normalised ratio. This is a distinct mechanism from the direct factor Xa or thrombin inhibition used by direct oral anticoagulants. Recognising this mechanism explains why warfarin requires routine monitoring while newer agents generally do not. This is a frequently tested contrast in anticoagulant pharmacology.
  2. The correct answer is E (Direct inhibition of thrombin): dabigatran is a direct thrombin inhibitor, giving it a more predictable anticoagulant effect than warfarin and removing the need for routine monitoring. This differs from apixaban and rivaroxaban, which act on factor Xa instead. Recognising the specific target of each direct oral anticoagulant helps clarify their shared but mechanistically distinct actions. Renal function still requires consideration since dabigatran is renally excreted.
  3. The correct answer is F (Irreversible inhibition of cyclo-oxygenase and thromboxane A2 production): aspirin permanently inactivates cyclo-oxygenase within the platelet, and because platelets cannot synthesise new enzyme, this effect persists for the lifespan of the affected platelet. This distinguishes aspirin from reversible antiplatelet agents such as clopidogrel. Recognising this mechanism is relevant when considering bleeding risk timelines before a procedure. Current UK guidance nonetheless favours continuing aspirin for routine extractions.
  4. The correct answer is G (Blockade of the platelet ADP receptor): clopidogrel specifically blocks the platelet ADP receptor, impairing platelet activation and aggregation as part of primary haemostasis rather than affecting the coagulation cascade. This mechanism is distinct from aspirin's cyclo-oxygenase inhibition, even though both are antiplatelet agents. Recognising this receptor-specific action explains why combining the two drugs increases bleeding risk more than either alone. Both are generally continued for routine dental extractions under current guidance.
  5. The correct answer is A (Von Willebrand factor mediated platelet adhesion to collagen): defective initial adhesion of platelets to exposed subendothelial collagen, despite normal platelet counts, is characteristic of von Willebrand disease, in which this bridging molecule is deficient or dysfunctional. This differs from disorders affecting aggregation or the coagulation cascade itself. Recognising this specific adhesion defect helps distinguish von Willebrand disease from other bleeding disorders. It is an important differential when a patient reports a personal or family history of unusual bleeding.
EMQ Set EMQ-physio-3

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely physiological process from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Rapid bicarbonate-mediated buffering as flow rate increases

B. Net demineralisation below the critical pH for enamel

C. Remineralisation using calcium and phosphate from supersaturated saliva

D. Agglutination of bacteria by secretory immunoglobulin A

E. Formation of the acquired pellicle by salivary mucins

F. Fluoride promoting fluorapatite formation with a lower critical pH

G. Sequestration of free iron by lactoferrin

  1. The correct answer is B (Net demineralisation below the critical pH for enamel): once plaque pH falls below approximately 5.5 following a sugar challenge, hydroxyapatite becomes soluble and net demineralisation occurs, the defining early event of the Stephan curve. This process reverses once pH recovers above the critical threshold and remineralisation resumes. Recognising this threshold explains why frequent sugar exposures are particularly damaging. It underlies the biochemical basis of caries initiation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Remineralisation using calcium and phosphate from supersaturated saliva): as plaque pH recovers after the initial acid challenge, saliva's supersaturation with calcium and phosphate relative to hydroxyapatite drives redeposition of mineral into the partially demineralised enamel. This process can offset earlier mineral loss if sufficient recovery time is allowed between sugar exposures. Frequent challenges that prevent adequate recovery time overwhelm this remineralisation capacity. This balance between demineralisation and remineralisation determines net caries progression.
  3. The correct answer is A (Rapid bicarbonate-mediated buffering as flow rate increases): chewing sugar-free gum increases salivary flow rate, and because bicarbonate concentration rises steeply with flow, this markedly improves the neutralisation of acid produced after a meal compared with resting saliva alone. This is the physiological basis for recommending gum as an adjunct to caries prevention. It complements, but is distinct from, the slower remineralisation process that follows. This mechanism directly links flow rate to buffering capacity.
  4. The correct answer is E (Formation of the acquired pellicle by salivary mucins): mucins in saliva form a thin proteinaceous film on the tooth surface shortly after cleaning, known as the acquired pellicle, which provides lubrication but also serves as the first step in plaque formation. This illustrates that saliva is simultaneously protective and a necessary precursor to biofilm development. Recognising this dual role is important when explaining plaque formation to patients. It distinguishes the pellicle from the later, more organised bacterial biofilm that develops on top of it.
  5. The correct answer is G (Sequestration of free iron by lactoferrin): lactoferrin binds free iron in saliva, depriving iron-dependent bacteria of a nutrient essential for their growth and thereby limiting bacterial proliferation. This is a distinct antimicrobial mechanism from the cell wall disruption caused by lysozyme or the antibody-mediated agglutination performed by secretory immunoglobulin A. Recognising these differing mechanisms clarifies the multiple, complementary ways saliva defends against oral bacteria. This nutrient deprivation strategy is a classic example of innate immune defence.
  6. The correct answer is C (Body of the lesion): the body of the lesion is the largest, most heavily demineralised zone of an enamel caries lesion, lying deep to the intact surface zone. The translucent zone represents the earliest, most subtle porosity at the advancing front, while the dark zone reflects remineralisation trapping air and fluid. Because the surface zone remains relatively intact due to continual saliva bathing, an early lesion like this can arrest and remineralise if the surface is not cavitated.
  7. The correct answer is A (Dead tracts and sclerotic dentine): empty, sclerosed tubules from odontoblast death, with adjacent translucent sclerotic dentine, form as a defensive response ahead of the advancing carious front. Reactionary dentine is laid down by surviving odontoblasts, while reparative dentine follows odontoblast death via newly recruited cells, both nearer the pulp rather than at the advancing front. The zone of demineralisation alone does not describe this specific sclerotic, tubule-obliterating appearance.
  8. The correct answer is D (Reactionary dentine): reactionary dentine is formed by existing, surviving odontoblasts beneath a slowly advancing insult, without cell death occurring. Reparative dentine instead follows odontoblast death and is laid down by newly differentiated odontoblast-like cells, appearing less tubular and more irregular. Sclerotic and peritubular dentine describe different tubule-level mineral changes rather than this reactive tertiary dentine response.
  9. The correct answer is B (Faster lateral spread of caries once the enamel-dentine junction is breached): dentine has lower mineral content and a tubular structure that allows caries to spread laterally much faster once it crosses the enamel-dentine junction, producing the second, wider triangle. Enamel is actually more mineralised than dentine, not less. This appearance is unrelated to dead tract formation or to remineralisation, both of which describe different, non-progressive processes.
  10. The correct answer is B (Cervical burnout artefact): the ill-defined, symmetric radiolucency at the cervical margin with no detectable cavitation on probing is characteristic of cervical burnout, an artefact caused by the natural cervical constriction and reduced radiopaque tissue thickness at that site. True cervical caries would typically be detectable clinically on probing and often appears more asymmetric. Root resorption and recurrent caries beneath a restoration would each present with different radiographic and clinical features.
  11. The correct answer is D (Streptococcus mutans and Lactobacillus species): these are the principal cariogenic bacteria responsible for producing the acid that drives enamel and dentine demineralisation in dental caries. Porphyromonas gingivalis and Fusobacterium nucleatum are periodontal pathogens rather than primary caries organisms. Actinomyces israelii and Prevotella intermedia are also more closely associated with periodontal and endodontic infections than with the initiation of caries.
  12. The correct answer is A (The surface layer must remain intact and non-cavitated): non-operative remineralisation of a white spot lesion depends on the surface zone remaining intact, since a cavitated surface cannot be remineralised by fluoride and saliva alone. Pulp vitality and symptoms to hot are not relevant to whether a superficial enamel lesion can remineralise. Extension into dentine would instead indicate the lesion has progressed beyond the stage amenable to non-operative management.
  13. The correct answer is C (Tubular structure providing a direct pathway for acids and bacteria): dentine's tubules provide a direct conduit for acids and, later, bacteria to advance rapidly toward the pulp once the enamel-dentine junction is breached. Dentine actually has lower mineral content than enamel, not higher, which also contributes to its faster progression. Dentine does mount a cellular defensive response, so the absence of any cellular response is incorrect.
  14. The correct answer is D (The dentine): the classic interproximal caries triangle has its base at the enamel surface with the apex pointing toward the dentine, reflecting the direction of spread from the surface inward. The pulp chamber, lamina dura and periodontal ligament describe entirely different anatomical structures not involved in describing this radiographic triangular pattern.
  15. The correct answer is C (Tertiary (reparative) dentine): reparative dentine is formed by newly recruited odontoblast-like cells closest to the pulp after the original odontoblasts have died, forming the innermost defensive zone. The zone of destruction and zone of demineralisation lie nearer the advancing bacterial front, further from the pulp. Sclerotic dentine describes the translucent tubule-obliterated zone adjacent to the dead tracts, not the innermost reparative response.
  16. The correct answer is B (Reversible pulpitis): sharp, short-lived pain provoked by cold that resolves within seconds of stimulus removal, with no spontaneous pain and no percussion tenderness, is the classic presentation of reversible pulpitis. Irreversible pulpitis instead produces spontaneous, persistent pain lasting well beyond stimulus removal. Periapical granuloma and acute apical abscess both require pulp necrosis to have already occurred, which is inconsistent with this reversible presentation.
  17. The correct answer is A (Irreversible pulpitis): poorly localised, throbbing, spontaneous pain persisting after the stimulus is removed, combined with the classic relief of hot-induced pain by cold, points strongly to irreversible pulpitis. Reversible pulpitis produces only brief, stimulus-provoked pain without this pattern. Cracked tooth syndrome and idiopathic osteosclerosis do not typically produce this hot-pain-relieved-by-cold diagnostic pattern.
  18. The correct answer is A (Increased intrapulpal pressure in the supine position): lying down increases pulpal blood pressure within the rigid, non-compliant pulp chamber, worsening pain in irreversible pulpitis. Blood supply and vascular tone changes described in the other options do not reflect the actual mechanism, and increased salivary flow overnight has no bearing on intrapulpal pain.
  19. The correct answer is B (Radiographic appearance alone cannot reliably distinguish a granuloma from a radicular cyst): both present as well-defined periapical radiolucencies at a non-vital tooth, and histological examination after extraction or endodontic surgery is required to distinguish them definitively. A corticated rim may favour a cyst but is not absolutely diagnostic, and size under 1 cm does not reliably confirm either diagnosis. Vitality testing helps confirm pulp necrosis but does not separate the two entities from each other.
  20. The correct answer is D (Epithelial rests of Malassez): radicular cysts arise from proliferation of these epithelial remnants of Hertwig's root sheath within an existing periapical granuloma, stimulated by chronic inflammation. Reduced enamel epithelium instead gives rise to dentigerous cysts around unerupted teeth. Rests of Serres and dental lamina remnants are associated with other developmental odontogenic cysts, not radicular cysts.
  21. The correct answer is C (Condensing osteitis): an ill-defined radiopacity surrounding the apex of a symptomatic tooth with a long-standing large carious lesion and pulpitis history is characteristic of condensing osteitis, a localised bony reaction to chronic low-grade inflammation. Idiopathic osteosclerosis instead occurs at a vital, asymptomatic tooth. Cementoblastoma and periapical granuloma have different characteristic radiographic appearances and associations.
  22. The correct answer is C (Idiopathic osteosclerosis): a well-defined, round, stable periapical radiopacity at a vital, asymptomatic tooth with no caries or restorative history is the classic ORE-style presentation of idiopathic osteosclerosis, a developmental variant of normal bone. Condensing osteitis instead occurs at a symptomatic or previously diseased tooth. Cementoblastoma is fused to the root and hypercementosis produces a different, root-outline-following radiopaque pattern.
  23. The correct answer is D (Pulp vitality testing of the associated tooth): vitality testing should always be performed first when assessing a periapical radiolucency, since it immediately narrows the differential toward an endodontic cause or a developmental/other lesion. CBCT, biopsy and sialography are all more invasive or specialised investigations that follow, rather than precede, this basic clinical step.
  24. The correct answer is A (Residual cyst): a residual cyst is a radicular cyst that persists in the jaw after the causative tooth has already been extracted. A lateral periodontal cyst develops laterally along a vital tooth root, a dentigerous cyst envelops the crown of an unerupted tooth, and a nasopalatine duct cyst arises in the anterior maxillary midline, none of which fit this post-extraction persistence.
  25. The correct answer is B (Irreversible pulpitis): a dense inflammatory infiltrate with microabscess formation and progressive necrosis is the histological picture of irreversible pulpitis, in which the pulp cannot heal even if the causative factor is removed. Reversible pulpitis instead shows only mild, localised inflammation without necrosis. Periapical granuloma and condensing osteitis describe periapical, not intrapulpal, histological findings.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Dentigerous cyst): a well-defined, unilocular, corticated radiolucency symmetrically enveloping the crown of an unerupted tooth, attached at the amelocemental junction, is the classic appearance of a dentigerous cyst. Odontogenic keratocysts and ameloblastomas are more typically multilocular, and a lateral periodontal cyst develops beside the root rather than around an unerupted crown.
  2. The correct answer is B (High recurrence rate after enucleation): the thin, friable, parakeratinised lining with daughter cysts within the wall gives odontogenic keratocysts a notably high recurrence rate after treatment, making follow-up essential. Malignant transformation risk is not a defining feature of this lesion. Hypercalcaemia and bilateral maxillary involvement are not characteristic associations of odontogenic keratocysts.
  3. The correct answer is A (Calcification of the falx cerebri): Gorlin-Goltz syndrome, suggested by multiple odontogenic keratocysts with multiple basal cell carcinomas, also features calcification of the falx cerebri along with skeletal anomalies such as bifid ribs. Widened periodontal ligament spaces and bilateral parotid enlargement are not features of this syndrome, and multiple dentigerous cysts are not the characteristic jaw finding.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ameloblastoma): a multilocular soap bubble radiolucency in the posterior mandible with marked cortical expansion and root resorption is the classic radiographic appearance of ameloblastoma. Odontogenic keratocysts typically show less buccolingual expansion for a comparable size. Central giant cell granuloma and odontogenic myxoma can appear multilocular but lack this combination as their most classic descriptor.
  5. The correct answer is A (A unicystic ameloblastoma can radiographically mimic a dentigerous cyst): because a unicystic ameloblastoma can appear identical to a simple dentigerous cyst on radiograph, histological examination is mandatory for every excised cystic jaw lesion to avoid missing this locally aggressive neoplasm. Not all dentigerous cysts become malignant, and radicular cysts and odontogenic keratocysts each still require their own histological confirmation, so the other statements are incorrect.
  6. The correct answer is C (Fusion of the radiopaque mass to the tooth root): cementoblastoma is defined by fusion of the radiopaque mass to the root apex, distinguishing it from condensing osteitis, which is an irregular reaction not fused to the tooth. A radiolucent rim can be seen in both lesions, so it is not the discriminating feature. Location in the posterior mandible and young patient age are typical but not the key distinguishing feature from condensing osteitis.
  7. The correct answer is B (Compound odontoma): multiple small, tooth-like denticles in the anterior maxilla preventing eruption of a permanent tooth is characteristic of a compound odontoma. A complex odontoma instead forms a disorganised mass with no tooth-like morphology, typically in the posterior mandible. Ameloblastic fibroma and cementoblastoma do not present with multiple discrete tooth-like structures.
  8. The correct answer is D (An early stage of odontoma before mineralisation is complete): odontomas are often preceded by a purely radiolucent stage before mineralisation is complete, which then evolves into the classic radiopaque mass with a thin radiolucent rim. Odontogenic keratocysts do not mineralise into odontomas, and this presentation is not consistent with malignant change in a dentigerous cyst or a resolving granuloma.
  9. The correct answer is C (Ameloblastoma): ameloblastoma is the neoplasm most classically described as arising within the epithelial lining of a dentigerous cyst, which is why all dentigerous cysts require histological confirmation after enucleation. Pleomorphic adenoma is a salivary gland tumour unrelated to odontogenic cyst linings. Cementoblastoma and squamous cell papilloma are not associated with arising from dentigerous cyst epithelium.
  10. The correct answer is B (Growth along the medullary cavity with less buccolingual expansion): odontogenic keratocysts characteristically grow along the medullary cavity of the mandible with comparatively less buccolingual expansion than other jaw cysts of similar size, sometimes producing a scalloped border. Rapid buccolingual expansion is instead more typical of lesions like dentigerous cysts or ameloblastomas. Growth confined to the periodontal ligament space or perfectly symmetrical expansion do not describe this lesion.
  11. The correct answer is D (Oral lichen planus): bilateral, symmetrical white, lace-like striations (Wickham's striae) on the buccal mucosa in an asymptomatic, stable patient is the classic reticular presentation of oral lichen planus. Leukoplakia is a diagnosis of exclusion for a white patch not fitting a specific pattern like this. Chronic hyperplastic candidiasis and lichenoid reactions can mimic lichen planus but do not typically present with this bilateral, symmetrical lace-like pattern without an identifiable local cause.
  12. The correct answer is A (Erosive and atrophic forms carry a small but recognised malignant transformation risk): this is precisely why long-term periodic review is recommended for patients with erosive or atrophic oral lichen planus. It does not progress to bullous pemphigoid, is not contagious, and does not reliably resolve spontaneously within a fixed timeframe, making the other statements incorrect.
  13. The correct answer is B (Assessing whether the lesion resolves after removal of the suspected causative material): because the lesion is in direct topographical contact with an amalgam restoration, removing the suspected causative material and observing for resolution is a key step in distinguishing an oral lichenoid reaction from idiopathic lichen planus. Biopsy of the unaffected contralateral side would not clarify a unilateral, contact-related lesion. Antifungal therapy and nickel-only patch testing do not address the suspected amalgam-related aetiology.
  14. The correct answer is D (Leukoplakia, a clinical diagnosis of exclusion): a white patch that cannot be rubbed off and cannot be characterised as any other definable lesion is classified as leukoplakia, a clinical term reached only after excluding other diagnoses. Erythroplakia describes a red, not white, lesion. Chronic hyperplastic candidiasis and frictional keratosis are specific definable diagnoses that must first be excluded, not assumed by default.
  15. The correct answer is C (The non-homogeneous, speckled lesion carries a higher malignant transformation risk): non-homogeneous leukoplakia, including speckled, nodular or verrucous forms, carries a higher risk of malignant transformation than homogeneous leukoplakia. This makes the claim that the homogeneous lesion carries higher risk, or that risk is identical or unrelated to appearance, all incorrect.
  16. The correct answer is B (Proliferative verrucous leukoplakia): multifocal white patches that gradually enlarge across multiple sites with progressively worsening dysplasia on repeated biopsy describe proliferative verrucous leukoplakia, a distinct and more aggressive variant with a high malignant transformation rate. Homogeneous leukoplakia carries a lower risk and does not typically behave this aggressively. Oral lichen planus and frictional keratosis do not present as progressively worsening multifocal dysplastic lesions.
  17. The correct answer is D (It carries a substantially higher risk of dysplasia or carcinoma and must be biopsied without delay): erythroplakia, though far less common than leukoplakia, carries a substantially higher risk of harbouring severe dysplasia, carcinoma in situ or invasive carcinoma, and requires urgent biopsy. It is not more common than leukoplakia, cannot be safely left unbiopsied, and is not attributable solely to anaemia.
  18. The correct answer is A (Pseudomembranous candidiasis): removable white plaques revealing an erythematous base beneath, in a patient with local (denture-wearing) and systemic (inhaled corticosteroid) predisposing factors, is classic for pseudomembranous candidiasis (thrush). Erythematous candidiasis presents as red areas without plaques, chronic hyperplastic candidiasis presents as a white patch that does not rub off, and angular cheilitis affects the commissures rather than the buccal mucosa.
  19. The correct answer is C (Chronic hyperplastic candidiasis itself carries a risk of epithelial dysplasia): unlike most other candidal presentations, chronic hyperplastic candidiasis itself carries a recognised risk of dysplasia, which is why biopsy is indicated even with a strong clinical suspicion of a fungal cause. Not all candidal infections are premalignant, biopsy serves purposes beyond species confirmation, and this presentation is not specifically diagnostic of HIV infection.
  20. The correct answer is B (Major aphthous ulcer): an ulcer exceeding 1 cm that is deep, takes weeks to heal, and leaves a scar is characteristic of a major aphthous ulcer. Minor aphthous ulcers are under 1 cm and heal within 7 to 14 days without scarring, while herpetiform ulcers are numerous small pinpoint lesions. A traumatic ulcer would typically have an identifiable mechanical cause rather than this recurrent aphthous pattern.
  21. The correct answer is D (The submandibular duct's upward, tortuous course against gravity with mucous, alkaline, calcium-rich saliva): this combination of anatomical and salivary composition factors favours stagnation and stone formation, explaining why sialolithiasis most commonly affects the submandibular gland. The parotid duct's shorter, straighter course and the sublingual and minor salivary glands' different ductal arrangements make them far less prone to this process.
  22. The correct answer is A (Ranula): a painless, fluctuant swelling on the floor of the mouth arising specifically from the sublingual gland following trauma is termed a ranula, a variant of mucocele at this particular site. A mucus retention cyst arises from duct dilation rather than extravasation and is not specific to the sublingual gland location. Pleomorphic adenoma and sialolithiasis present with different clinical features entirely.
  23. The correct answer is C (Mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue (MALT) lymphoma): patients with Sjogren's syndrome have a significantly elevated lifetime risk of MALT lymphoma arising within affected salivary glands, so persistent unilateral gland enlargement warrants investigation for this. Sialolithiasis and acute bacterial sialadenitis typically present with pain rather than painless persistent enlargement, and pleomorphic adenoma of the contralateral gland would not explain enlargement of the originally affected side.
  24. The correct answer is B (Pleomorphic adenoma): a slow-growing, painless, firm, mobile parotid swelling present for years is the classic presentation of pleomorphic adenoma, the most common salivary gland neoplasm and the most common parotid tumour. Warthin's tumour and mucoepidermoid carcinoma present with different typical features, and sialolithiasis would present with pain related to eating rather than a slow, painless mass.
  25. The correct answer is C (A proportion of stones are radiolucent and ultrasound or sialography should be considered): because some salivary stones are radiolucent, a negative plain radiograph does not exclude sialolithiasis, and further imaging such as ultrasound or sialography should be pursued when symptoms persist. Sialolithiasis is not excluded by a negative occlusal view, the stone location described is consistent with the submandibular system rather than the parotid, and a lateral cephalometric view is not the appropriate next investigation.
  26. The correct answer is A (Hypomature type): enamel of near-normal thickness but with a mottled, opaque appearance and reduced hardness describes the hypomature type of amelogenesis imperfecta. The hypoplastic type instead produces thin, pitted enamel of normal hardness, and the hypomineralised type produces soft, easily lost enamel. Hypocalcified dentine type is not a recognised classification of amelogenesis imperfecta, which is an enamel, not dentine, disorder.
  27. The correct answer is D (Osteogenesis imperfecta type I): the blue-grey translucent appearance, enamel chipping from abnormal dentine, and bulbous crowns with pulp chamber obliteration describe dentinogenesis imperfecta, which is sometimes associated with osteogenesis imperfecta type I. Ectodermal dysplasia is more classically associated with hypodontia rather than this dentine defect. Cleidocranial dysplasia and Gorlin-Goltz syndrome present with different characteristic dental and skeletal features.
  28. The correct answer is B (Fusion): union of two normally separate tooth germs into a single broad crown, sharing or displaying separate root canals, reduces the total tooth count in the arch by one, which is the defining feature of fusion. Gemination instead preserves a normal tooth count since a single germ merely attempts to divide. Dens invaginatus and talon cusp are unrelated developmental anomalies affecting individual tooth morphology rather than tooth number.
  29. The correct answer is C (Mesiodens): a conical supernumerary tooth in the midline of the anterior maxilla, causing rotation of an adjacent incisor, is termed a mesiodens, the most common supernumerary tooth. A paramolar and distomolar are supernumerary teeth found in the molar region rather than the anterior midline. Odontome is a broader term for a hamartomatous dental tumour, not specific to this midline supernumerary presentation.
  30. The correct answer is A (Mandibular second premolar): excluding third molars, the mandibular second premolar is the most commonly congenitally absent permanent tooth, followed by maxillary lateral incisors and maxillary second premolars. Maxillary lateral incisor is the second most commonly absent tooth, not the most common, and mandibular central incisors and maxillary second molars are absent far less frequently.
  31. The correct answer is B (Poorly controlled diabetes mellitus): unusually aggressive, rapidly progressing periodontitis at a young age should prompt investigation for undiagnosed or poorly controlled diabetes, given its well-established bidirectional relationship with periodontal disease through impaired neutrophil function and collagen repair. Iron deficiency anaemia, Sjogren's syndrome and coeliac disease do not typically present with this specific pattern of aggressive periodontal destruction.
  32. The correct answer is D (Iron deficiency anaemia): a smooth, erythematous, depapillated tongue (atrophic glossitis), angular cheilitis and recurrent aphthous ulceration are classic oral features of iron deficiency anaemia. Vitamin D deficiency, hypothyroidism and hyperparathyroidism do not typically present with this specific combination of oral mucosal findings.
  33. The correct answer is A (Postcricoid and oral squamous cell carcinoma): the combination of iron deficiency anaemia, dysphagia and oesophageal webs defines Plummer-Vinson syndrome, which carries an increased risk of postcricoid and oral squamous cell carcinoma and should be recognised as a red-flag combination. Salivary gland lymphoma, basal cell carcinoma of the lip and ameloblastoma are not the malignancies specifically associated with this syndrome.
  34. The correct answer is B (Oral hairy leukoplakia, not premalignant): a corrugated white lesion on the lateral tongue border caused by Epstein-Barr virus that does not rub off describes oral hairy leukoplakia, which is not premalignant despite its name, and can be a presenting feature of undiagnosed HIV infection. Leukoplakia and proliferative verrucous leukoplakia both carry genuine malignant potential, unlike this entity, and chronic hyperplastic candidiasis has a different causative organism and clinical picture.
  35. The correct answer is D (Pemphigus vulgaris): fragile bullae rupturing rapidly into painful erosions, a positive Nikolsky sign, and an intraepithelial suprabasal split on immunofluorescence are all characteristic of pemphigus vulgaris, caused by autoantibodies against desmoglein. Mucous membrane pemphigoid instead shows a subepithelial split with more durable bullae. Erosive lichen planus and erythema multiforme do not typically show this specific suprabasal split pattern on immunofluorescence.
  36. The correct answer is A (Medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw (MRONJ)): exposed, necrotic bone persisting beyond eight weeks in a patient on antiresorptive therapy, with no history of jaw radiotherapy, meets the diagnostic definition of MRONJ. Osteoradionecrosis requires a history of radiotherapy, which is absent here. Chronic osteomyelitis unrelated to medication and condensing osteitis do not fit this specific antiresorptive drug history and presentation.
  37. The correct answer is B (Intravenous, high-dose antiresorptive therapy carries substantially higher risk than oral bisphosphonates): MRONJ risk is far greater with the high-dose intravenous antiresorptive regimens used in oncology than with oral bisphosphonates used for osteoporosis, though the oral agent risk is not zero. The risks are therefore not identical, and denosumab is not the only drug class implicated, since bisphosphonates carry the same risk category.
  38. The correct answer is D (Generally continue the drug and use atraumatic extraction technique): current UK guidance favours continuing antiresorptive therapy around the time of extraction while using atraumatic technique, since drug discontinuation has not been shown to reliably reduce MRONJ risk. Routine stopping three months beforehand, switching to denosumab, or arranging radiotherapy planning are not appropriate or evidence-based responses to this scenario.
  39. The correct answer is D (Dentoalveolar surgery, particularly extractions): dentoalveolar surgery, and extractions specifically, is the principal recognised trigger for MRONJ development in patients on antiresorptive or antiangiogenic therapy. Routine scale and polish, denture fitting and fissure sealant placement are all non-surgical procedures that do not carry this recognised trigger risk.
  40. The correct answer is A (It points away from a purely endodontic cause toward a developmental or other lesion): a periapical radiolucency at a vital, asymptomatic tooth with no caries or restorative history is inconsistent with an endodontic origin, and should instead prompt consideration of a developmental lesion such as a lateral periodontal or nasopalatine duct cyst. A periapical granuloma, condensing osteitis and acute apical abscess all require a non-vital or previously diseased pulp, which is not present here.
  41. The correct answer is D (Radicular cyst): a well-defined, corticated radiolucency over 1 cm with root divergence at a non-vital tooth favours a radicular cyst over a granuloma, since cysts are often larger and more likely to cause root divergence or resorption, although histology remains the only definitive test. Idiopathic osteosclerosis occurs at a vital tooth and would not show this radiolucent, non-vital picture. Cementoblastoma shows a fused radiopaque mass rather than a radiolucency.
  42. The correct answer is A (Condensing osteitis): an ill-defined periapical radiopacity at the apex of a tooth with a large carious lesion and intermittent pulpal pain is the classic presentation of condensing osteitis, a bony reaction to chronic low-grade inflammation. Cementoblastoma is fused to the root with a distinct radiolucent rim rather than being simply ill-defined. Idiopathic osteosclerosis occurs at a vital, asymptomatic tooth, and ameloblastoma is radiolucent rather than radiopaque.
  43. The correct answer is B (Idiopathic osteosclerosis): a well-defined, round, stable periapical radiopacity at a vital, asymptomatic tooth with no caries or restorations, unchanged over years, is the classic appearance of idiopathic osteosclerosis, a developmental variant of normal bone. Condensing osteitis requires a symptomatic or previously diseased tooth. Cementoblastoma is fused to the root, and a radicular cyst would appear radiolucent, not radiopaque.
  44. The correct answer is C (Cementoblastoma): a well-defined, round radiopaque mass fused directly to the root apex with a thin radiolucent rim, in a young patient with a vital tooth, is the classic appearance of cementoblastoma. Idiopathic osteosclerosis is not fused to the root. Condensing osteitis is typically ill-defined and occurs at a symptomatic, non-vital or previously diseased tooth. Hypercementosis follows the root outline without a discrete rim and fused mass appearance.
  45. The correct answer is D (Dentigerous cyst): a well-defined, unilocular, corticated radiolucency symmetrically enveloping the crown of an unerupted tooth, attached at the crown-root junction, is the classic radiographic description of a dentigerous cyst. Odontogenic keratocysts and ameloblastomas are typically multilocular rather than this simple unilocular pattern. A radicular cyst arises from a non-vital erupted tooth's apex, not an unerupted crown.
  46. The correct answer is B (Odontogenic keratocyst): a multilocular radiolucency with a scalloped border extending along the medullary cavity of the ramus with minimal buccolingual expansion is characteristic of an odontogenic keratocyst. A dentigerous cyst is typically unilocular and confined around an unerupted crown. Ameloblastoma more often produces marked expansion for a comparable size, and a compound odontoma appears radiopaque rather than radiolucent.
  47. The correct answer is A (Ameloblastoma): an extensive multilocular soap bubble radiolucency causing marked expansion of both cortical plates and adjacent root resorption is the classic radiographic appearance of ameloblastoma. Odontogenic keratocysts typically produce less buccolingual expansion for a similar size. Cementoblastoma and complex odontoma are both radiopaque lesions, not radiolucent.
  48. The correct answer is C (Compound odontoma): multiple small, discrete, tooth-like denticles surrounded by a thin radiolucent rim, blocking eruption of an anterior maxillary incisor, is characteristic of a compound odontoma. A complex odontoma instead shows a disorganised mass with no tooth-like structures. Mesiodens is a single supernumerary tooth, not multiple denticles, and cementoblastoma is fused to a root rather than associated with an unerupted crown.
  49. The correct answer is D (Complex odontoma): a disorganised radiopaque mass with no tooth-like structures, associated with an unerupted posterior tooth, is characteristic of a complex odontoma, typically found in the posterior mandible. A compound odontoma instead shows multiple discrete tooth-like denticles. Ameloblastoma and odontogenic keratocyst are both radiolucent lesions rather than presenting as a radiopaque mass.
  50. The correct answer is A (Early interproximal enamel caries): a triangular radiolucency with its base at the enamel surface and apex directed toward, but not yet crossing, the enamel-dentine junction represents early interproximal caries confined to enamel. Cervical burnout is located at the cervical constriction rather than the interproximal contact area. Condensing osteitis and periapical granuloma are both periapical, not interproximal, findings.
  51. The correct answer is C (Cervical burnout artefact): a symmetric, ill-defined radiolucency at the cervical constriction with no clinically detectable cavitation on probing is the classic appearance of cervical burnout, an anatomical artefact rather than true disease. Recurrent caries beneath a restoration would require an existing restoration, which is not described here. True cervical caries would typically be detectable clinically, and internal root resorption produces a distinct canal-outline change rather than a cervical radiolucency.
  52. The correct answer is B (Recurrent (secondary) caries): an irregular, asymmetric radiolucency extending from beneath a restoration margin, with a clinically detectable defect on probing, is characteristic of recurrent caries around an existing restoration. Cervical burnout is a symmetric artefact without a clinical defect. Idiopathic osteosclerosis and periapical granuloma are both apical rather than restoration-associated findings.
  53. The correct answer is D (The image is equally consistent with a periapical granuloma or a radicular cyst): a well-defined periapical radiolucency at a non-vital tooth with loss of lamina dura and a widened periodontal ligament space cannot be reliably distinguished between a granuloma and a radicular cyst on radiograph alone, requiring histology for definitive diagnosis. The image therefore cannot confirm one diagnosis over the other, and it does not fit idiopathic osteosclerosis or condensing osteitis, both of which are radiopaque and occur at vital teeth.
  54. The correct answer is C (Calcification of the falx cerebri): bilateral multiple odontogenic keratocysts in a young patient with basal cell carcinomas raises suspicion for Gorlin-Goltz syndrome, which also features calcification of the falx cerebri and skeletal anomalies such as bifid ribs. Bilateral condylar hyperplasia and diffuse alveolar bone loss are not recognised features of this syndrome. Widened periodontal ligament spaces bilaterally are also not a characteristic finding of Gorlin-Goltz syndrome.
  55. The correct answer is A (Nasopalatine duct cyst): a well-defined radiolucency in the anterior maxillary midline, between and apical to two vital central incisors, is characteristic of a nasopalatine duct cyst, a developmental lesion unrelated to pulpal pathology. Radicular cyst and periapical granuloma both require a non-vital tooth, which is not the case here. Dentigerous cyst envelops the crown of an unerupted tooth, not the midline region between erupted vital incisors.
  56. The correct answer is B (Lateral periodontal cyst): a well-defined radiolucency along the lateral aspect of a vital tooth root, distinct from the apex and without a history of trauma or restoration, is characteristic of a lateral periodontal cyst, a developmental lesion. Radicular cyst and periapical granuloma both require pulp necrosis and an apical, not lateral, location. Condensing osteitis would appear radiopaque rather than radiolucent.
  57. The correct answer is D (Sialolith (submandibular duct stone)): a discrete radiopaque structure along the course of the submandibular duct, with pain and swelling worsening at mealtimes, is characteristic of a sialolith, reflecting the submandibular gland's predisposition to stone formation. Cementoblastoma and idiopathic osteosclerosis are both dental, tooth-associated radiopacities rather than ductal structures. Mesiodens is a supernumerary tooth located in the anterior maxilla, unrelated to the submandibular duct.
  58. The correct answer is C (Bilateral cervical burnout artefact requiring no treatment): symmetric, ill-defined cervical radiolucencies on adjacent teeth with normal vitality and no detectable cavitation on probing represent bilateral cervical burnout, an anatomical artefact needing no intervention. Early caries would typically be detectable on probing, and bilateral condensing osteitis or periapical granulomas would each require an associated pulpal or periapical pathology, which is not present here.
  59. The correct answer is B (Dentinogenesis imperfecta): a bulbous root outline with pulp chamber obliteration and a clinically translucent, opalescent tooth appearance describes dentinogenesis imperfecta, an inherited dentine formation defect. Amelogenesis imperfecta primarily affects enamel rather than producing this bulbous, obliterated root and pulp appearance. Hypodontia describes tooth absence, and fusion describes union of two tooth germs, neither of which fits this clinical and radiographic picture.
  60. The correct answer is A (Amelogenesis imperfecta, hypomature type): enamel of near-normal thickness with a mottled appearance, reduced radiographic contrast against dentine, and enamel that chips easily describes the hypomature type of amelogenesis imperfecta. Dentinogenesis imperfecta primarily produces bulbous crowns and pulp obliteration rather than this enamel mottling. Fusion and hypodontia describe entirely different developmental anomalies unrelated to generalised enamel quality.
  61. The correct answer is D (Fusion): a single broad crown with two distinct root canals, where the total tooth count in the quadrant is reduced by one, represents fusion of two tooth germs. Gemination instead preserves a normal tooth count since a single germ merely attempts to divide. Dens invaginatus and talon cusp are morphological anomalies of an individual tooth rather than anomalies affecting overall tooth number.
  62. The correct answer is C (Gemination): a single root and single root canal with a clinically bifid crown, and a normal overall tooth count for the arch, describes gemination, an incomplete attempt of one tooth germ to divide. Fusion instead reduces the total tooth count by one since two germs unite. A supernumerary tooth would add an additional, separate tooth unit, and dens evaginatus describes an additional cusp rather than a bifid crown.
  63. The correct answer is A (Hypercementosis): a smooth, irregular radiopaque thickening following the outline of the root over most of its length, without fusion to a discrete mass, describes hypercementosis, excess cementum deposition along the root surface. Cementoblastoma instead presents as a discrete round mass fused to the root apex. Condensing osteitis and idiopathic osteosclerosis both present as periapical radiopacities rather than a thickening that follows the entire root outline.
  64. The correct answer is B (Cemento-osseous dysplasia): multiple well-defined radiopaque lesions not fused to the roots of vital teeth in the anterior mandible of a middle-aged woman are characteristic of cemento-osseous dysplasia. Cementoblastoma is fused to a single root rather than presenting as multiple separate lesions. Ameloblastoma is radiolucent rather than radiopaque, and complex odontoma is typically a single mass associated with an unerupted tooth rather than multiple periapical lesions at vital teeth.
  65. The correct answer is D (Diabetes-related impaired healing and xerostomia contributing to both caries and periodontal bone loss): poorly controlled diabetes impairs neutrophil function and collagen repair, worsening periodontal bone loss, while the accompanying reduced salivary flow increases caries susceptibility, explaining both findings on this single radiograph. These findings are not coincidental given the clinical history provided. Sjogren's syndrome is not mentioned in the history, and MRONJ requires a history of antiresorptive medication, which is not described here.
  66. The correct answer is A (Persistent periapical granuloma or radicular cyst): a well-defined periapical radiolucency that persists unchanged eighteen months after root canal treatment with adequate obturation indicates failed periapical healing, most likely due to a persistent granuloma or radicular cyst. Successful healing would show radiographic resolution of the lesion over this timeframe. Idiopathic osteosclerosis and condensing osteitis are both radiopaque findings, inconsistent with this persistent radiolucency.
  67. The correct answer is C (Medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw): exposed, necrotic bone with sequestrum formation persisting beyond eight weeks after extraction in a patient on long-term intravenous zoledronic acid, with no radiotherapy history, meets the diagnostic criteria for MRONJ. Osteoradionecrosis requires a radiotherapy history, which is absent. Chronic osteomyelitis of unrelated cause and condensing osteitis do not account for the specific antiresorptive drug history described.
  68. The correct answer is B (A pulp stone (denticle)): a thin, calcified radiopaque bridge partially crossing the pulp chamber, with normal periapical tissues and a normal vitality response, represents a pulp stone (denticle), a benign calcified deposit within the pulp. A periapical granuloma would present as a periapical radiolucency rather than an intrapulpal radiopacity. Condensing osteitis is also a periapical, not intrapulpal, finding, and internal resorption would show canal enlargement rather than a calcified bridge.
  69. The correct answer is D (Internal (canal) resorption): an oval radiolucency within the coronal root canal with an enlarged, asymmetric canal outline compared with the adjacent canal represents internal (canal) resorption, arising from odontoclastic activity within the pulp space. External root resorption would instead show changes to the outer root surface rather than the canal outline itself. Dead tract formation and condensing osteitis do not produce this specific canal-enlarging appearance.
  70. The correct answer is C (External inflammatory root resorption): irregular, patchy resorption of the external root surface with an adjacent poorly defined radiolucency, following orthodontic treatment and loss of vitality, is characteristic of external inflammatory root resorption. Internal resorption instead affects the canal outline from within rather than the external root surface. Physiological resorption applies to primary, not permanent, teeth, and idiopathic osteosclerosis is a stable radiopacity rather than a resorptive process.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-1

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Periapical (radicular) granuloma

B. Radicular cyst

C. Condensing osteitis

D. Idiopathic osteosclerosis

E. Cementoblastoma

F. Lateral periodontal cyst

G. Nasopalatine duct cyst

H. Residual cyst

  1. The correct answer is D (Idiopathic osteosclerosis): a well-defined, round, radiopaque lesion at a vital, asymptomatic tooth with no caries or restorative history that remains stable over years is the classic description of idiopathic osteosclerosis, a developmental variant of normal bone requiring no treatment.
  2. The correct answer is C (Condensing osteitis): an ill-defined periapical radiopacity at a symptomatic tooth with a large carious lesion and pulpitis history, which partially resolves once the causative tooth is treated, is characteristic of condensing osteitis rather than the stable idiopathic osteosclerosis.
  3. The correct answer is H (Residual cyst): a well-defined corticated radiolucency persisting at the site of a previously extracted non-vital tooth represents a residual cyst, a radicular cyst that remains in the jaw after the causative tooth has been removed.
  4. The correct answer is G (Nasopalatine duct cyst): a radiolucency in the anterior maxillary midline, between and apical to two vital central incisors, is a developmental nasopalatine duct cyst rather than an endodontic lesion, since both associated teeth remain vital.
  5. The correct answer is E (Cementoblastoma): a well-defined, round radiopaque mass fused to the root apex of a vital tooth in a young patient, surrounded by a thin radiolucent rim, is the classic description of cementoblastoma.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-2

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Dentigerous cyst

B. Odontogenic keratocyst

C. Ameloblastoma

D. Cementoblastoma

E. Compound odontoma

F. Complex odontoma

G. Radicular cyst

H. Odontogenic myxoma

  1. The correct answer is A (Dentigerous cyst): a well-defined, unilocular, corticated radiolucency symmetrically enveloping the crown of an unerupted canine, attached at the amelocemental junction, is the classic dentigerous cyst appearance.
  2. The correct answer is B (Odontogenic keratocyst): a multilocular, scalloped radiolucency growing along the medullary cavity with minimal buccolingual expansion, occurring multiply alongside basal cell carcinomas, is characteristic of odontogenic keratocysts in the context of Gorlin-Goltz syndrome.
  3. The correct answer is C (Ameloblastoma): a large multilocular soap bubble radiolucency with marked cortical expansion and root resorption of adjacent teeth is the classic radiographic appearance of ameloblastoma.
  4. The correct answer is E (Compound odontoma): a well-defined radiopaque mass containing multiple small, discrete, tooth-like denticles blocking eruption of a permanent incisor represents a compound odontoma, typically found in the anterior maxilla.
  5. The correct answer is F (Complex odontoma): a disorganised radiopaque mass with no tooth-like structures, associated with an unerupted premolar in the posterior mandible, represents a complex odontoma.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-3

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Oral lichen planus

B. Oral lichenoid reaction

C. Leukoplakia (homogeneous)

D. Proliferative verrucous leukoplakia

E. Erythroplakia

F. Pseudomembranous candidiasis

G. Chronic hyperplastic candidiasis

H. Erosive lichen planus

  1. The correct answer is A (Oral lichen planus): bilateral, symmetrical white, lace-like Wickham's striae on the buccal mucosa with no identifiable local cause is the classic reticular presentation of oral lichen planus.
  2. The correct answer is B (Oral lichenoid reaction): a unilateral white, lace-like lesion in direct topographical contact with an amalgam restoration suggests an oral lichenoid reaction to the dental material rather than idiopathic lichen planus, which is typically bilateral and symmetrical.
  3. The correct answer is C (Leukoplakia (homogeneous)): a uniform, flat white patch that cannot be rubbed off or attributed to any other definable condition, with no non-homogeneous features, is classified as homogeneous leukoplakia.
  4. The correct answer is E (Erythroplakia): a red patch that cannot be attributed to any other cause carries a substantially higher risk of dysplasia or carcinoma than leukoplakia and must always be biopsied urgently, particularly on a high-risk site such as the floor of the mouth.
  5. The correct answer is G (Chronic hyperplastic candidiasis): a white patch on the commissure that does not rub off, despite showing candidal hyphae on smear, still warrants biopsy because this specific presentation itself carries a risk of epithelial dysplasia.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-4

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Sialolithiasis

B. Mucocele

C. Ranula

D. Sjogren's syndrome

E. Pleomorphic adenoma

F. Warthin's tumour

G. MALT lymphoma

H. Acute bacterial sialadenitis

  1. The correct answer is A (Sialolithiasis): pain and swelling worsening at mealtimes with a palpable hard mass along the floor of the mouth is the classic presentation of a submandibular sialolith obstructing salivary flow.
  2. The correct answer is B (Mucocele): a painless, fluctuant lower lip swelling following trauma to a minor salivary gland duct, causing mucin extravasation, is the classic presentation of a mucocele.
  3. The correct answer is C (Ranula): a painless, fluctuant, bluish swelling on the floor of the mouth arising specifically from the sublingual gland is termed a ranula, a site-specific variant of mucocele.
  4. The correct answer is G (MALT lymphoma): persistent unilateral parotid enlargement in a patient with established Sjogren's syndrome and secondary rheumatoid arthritis warrants investigation for mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue lymphoma, given the significantly elevated lifetime risk in this population.
  5. The correct answer is E (Pleomorphic adenoma): a slow-growing, painless, firm, mobile parotid swelling present for years is the classic presentation of pleomorphic adenoma, the most common parotid gland neoplasm.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-5

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Two-week-wait urgent referral indicated

B. Routine review in 3 weeks is appropriate

C. Tobacco and alcohol synergistic risk

D. HPV-16 related oropharyngeal cancer

E. Areca nut and betel quid use

F. Reassure and discharge, no follow-up needed

G. Refer for routine biopsy within 6 weeks

H. Plummer-Vinson syndrome

  1. The correct answer is C (Tobacco and alcohol synergistic risk): tobacco and alcohol are the dominant risk factors for oral squamous cell carcinoma and act synergistically together, meaning their combined risk exceeds the sum of each individual risk.
  2. The correct answer is A (Two-week-wait urgent referral indicated): any oral ulcer persisting beyond three weeks with no obvious cause requires an urgent two-week-wait suspected cancer referral without waiting to see if it resolves, and a four-week duration already exceeds this threshold.
  3. The correct answer is D (HPV-16 related oropharyngeal cancer): human papillomavirus, particularly HPV-16, is an increasingly recognised cause of oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma in non-smoking patients, distinct from the traditional tobacco and alcohol pathway.
  4. The correct answer is E (Areca nut and betel quid use): areca nut and betel quid use is a recognised oral cancer risk factor in relevant populations, alongside tobacco and alcohol, and is relevant to this patient's chewing habit.
  5. The correct answer is H (Plummer-Vinson syndrome): the combination of iron deficiency anaemia, dysphagia and oesophageal webs defines Plummer-Vinson syndrome, which carries an increased risk of postcricoid and oral squamous cell carcinoma and should be recognised as a red-flag combination.
EMQ Set EMQ-oralpath-6

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Diabetes mellitus

B. Iron deficiency anaemia

C. HIV infection

D. Sjogren's syndrome

E. Pemphigus vulgaris

F. Mucous membrane pemphigoid

G. Plummer-Vinson syndrome

H. Coeliac disease

  1. The correct answer is A (Diabetes mellitus): unusually aggressive, rapidly progressing periodontitis at a young age with no other obvious local risk factors should prompt investigation for undiagnosed or poorly controlled diabetes mellitus.
  2. The correct answer is B (Iron deficiency anaemia): a smooth, erythematous, depapillated tongue (atrophic glossitis), angular cheilitis and recurrent aphthous ulceration are classic oral features of iron deficiency anaemia.
  3. The correct answer is C (HIV infection): a corrugated white lesion on the lateral tongue border caused by Epstein-Barr virus that does not rub off is oral hairy leukoplakia, which can be a presenting feature of undiagnosed HIV infection.
  4. The correct answer is E (Pemphigus vulgaris): fragile bullae rupturing rapidly into painful erosions, a positive Nikolsky sign, and an intraepithelial suprabasal split on biopsy are characteristic of pemphigus vulgaris, caused by autoantibodies against desmoglein.
  5. The correct answer is H (Coeliac disease): recurrent aphthous-like ulceration found alongside villous atrophy on small bowel biopsy indicates coeliac disease as the underlying cause of the oral ulceration, one of the systemic conditions to consider with atypical or major aphthous presentations.
  6. The correct answer is C (The acquired pellicle): The acquired pellicle forms within minutes of a clean enamel surface being exposed to saliva, as salivary glycoproteins such as proline-rich proteins, statherins and mucins adsorb onto hydroxyapatite. It selectively binds specific bacterial adhesins, effectively creating a receptor surface that determines which organisms attach first. It is not a passive coating and also supplies some protection and nutrients to early colonisers.
  7. The correct answer is A (Its organisation as a structured biofilm encased in an extracellular matrix): Dental plaque is organised as a structured biofilm encased in an extracellular matrix that adheres tightly to tooth surfaces, restorations and mucosa. This structural organisation, rather than any single species, is what makes plaque resistant to removal by rinsing alone. Mechanical disruption through brushing and interdental cleaning is therefore the cornerstone of prevention.
  8. The correct answer is D (Consuming residual oxygen and lowering the local redox potential): Early Gram-positive facultative anaerobic colonisers consume the small amount of oxygen present at the tooth surface, lowering the local redox potential and creating conditions more favourable for anaerobic organisms. This prepares the environment for later obligate anaerobic colonisers. They also provide binding sites for subsequent bacteria through coaggregation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Coaggregation): Coaggregation describes the process where one bacterial species binds directly to specific receptors on the surface of another already-attached species. This bridges the transition from early to late colonisers as the biofilm matures over several days. It is distinct from phagocytosis, opsonisation and chemotaxis, which are host immune processes rather than bacterial attachment mechanisms.
  10. The correct answer is B (Fusobacterium nucleatum): Fusobacterium nucleatum is often described as the key bridging organism because it coaggregates with both early Gram-positive colonisers and late Gram-negative anaerobes. This physically links the two bacterial communities together within the maturing plaque architecture. The other listed organisms are either early colonisers or late anaerobes but do not fulfil this specific bridging role.
  11. The correct answer is D (It is strongly acidogenic and aciduric, tolerating a low pH environment): Streptococcus mutans is strongly acidogenic, producing large quantities of lactic acid from dietary sugars, and aciduric, meaning it can survive and continue metabolising at a low pH that inhibits many other oral bacteria. This dual property allows it to dominate the biofilm under frequent carbohydrate loading. Neither red complex membership nor obligate anaerobic metabolism accurately describes this organism.
  12. The correct answer is A (Increased bacterial adhesion and a thicker, more protective biofilm matrix): The glucosyltransferases convert sucrose into sticky, insoluble glucan polymers that increase bacterial adhesion and thicken the biofilm matrix. This further protects the acidogenic community from saliva's buffering and clearance mechanisms. It does not neutralise lactic acid, kill early colonisers, or inhibit neutrophil migration directly.
  13. The correct answer is C (Lactobacillus becomes prominent once a lesion has formed, contributing to progression rather than initiation): Lactobacillus species become more prominent once a carious lesion has already formed, contributing to its progression rather than initiating it, while Streptococcus mutans drives the initial demineralisation. This distinction between aetiology and progression is a useful one to remember for examination reasoning. Neither species is uninvolved in the caries process.
  14. The correct answer is D (Porphyromonas gingivalis, Tannerella forsythia and Treponema denticola): Socransky's red complex comprises Porphyromonas gingivalis, Tannerella forsythia and Treponema denticola, and its presence in subgingival plaque is strongly associated with deep pocketing, attachment loss and bleeding on probing. The other listed groupings correspond to early colonisers, the orange complex, or organisms not classified within the red complex.
  15. The correct answer is B (It disproportionately disrupts subgingival community balance and dysregulates the host immune response): Porphyromonas gingivalis is considered a keystone pathogen because even at relatively low abundance it can disproportionately disrupt the balance of the entire subgingival community and dysregulate the host immune response. This tips a stable, inflamed relationship into a destructive one. It does not need to be the most numerous organism present to exert this effect.
  16. The correct answer is C (Degrading host proteins, disabling complement regulation and impairing neutrophil function): Gingipains are cysteine proteases produced by Porphyromonas gingivalis that degrade host proteins, disable complement regulation and interfere with neutrophil function.
