This combination allows the bacterial community to persist despite an active host inflammatory response. The other options describe unrelated processes attributable to different organisms in the biofilm.
  17. The correct answer is A (Preceding and supporting subsequent colonisation by the red complex organisms): The orange complex, including Fusobacterium nucleatum, Prevotella intermedia and Campylobacter species, tends to precede and support colonisation by the red complex organisms. This illustrates the ecological succession that characterises subgingival biofilm maturation. It does not eliminate red complex species or form the earliest pellicle layer.
  18. The correct answer is A (A polymicrobial mixture of aerobic and anaerobic organisms, with anaerobes often predominating): Odontogenic infections are typically polymicrobial, with a mixture of aerobic and anaerobic organisms usually involved. Anaerobes often predominate once an abscess has become established, reflecting the low oxygen tension within necrotic pulp tissue and deep periodontal pockets. This is not typically a monomicrobial process caused by a single species.
  19. The correct answer is C (It provides additional cover against the anaerobic organisms that predominate in established abscesses): Metronidazole is added to provide additional anaerobic cover, since anaerobic organisms often predominate once an odontogenic infection has become established and may not respond adequately to amoxicillin alone. It does not target the acquired pellicle or treat viral infections. This underpins the rationale for combined antibiotic regimens in severe infections.
  20. The correct answer is B (The submandibular space): Perforation of the lingual cortical plate below the mylohyoid muscle attachment results in spread of infection into the submandibular space, following the predictable anatomical pathways that determine odontogenic infection spread. Perforation above this attachment would instead track into the sublingual space. The buccal and pterygomandibular spaces relate to different perforation sites.
  21. The correct answer is D (Swelling that obliterates the nasolabial fold): Infection tracking superiorly from an infected upper canine into the canine space produces swelling that characteristically obliterates the nasolabial fold. The floor-of-mouth, periorbital and pterygomandibular findings described in the other options relate to different infection pathways from different teeth. This distinction reflects the anatomical relationship between the tooth apex and surrounding muscle attachments.
  22. The correct answer is B (Ludwig's angina): Ludwig's angina is a rapidly progressive, bilateral cellulitis involving the submandibular, sublingual and submental spaces, most often arising from an infected lower second or third molar. The hallmark features include a firm, board-like floor-of-mouth swelling, tongue elevation, drooling and a muffled voice, with a real risk of airway compromise. It requires urgent hospital admission rather than routine dental management.
  23. The correct answer is D (It is a diffuse cellulitis that pushes the tongue posteriorly and threatens the airway): Ludwig's angina is a diffuse cellulitis rather than a discrete pus collection, and the swelling pushes the tongue posteriorly, creating a real risk of airway compromise. Airway assessment and often securing the airway must therefore precede any attempt at surgical drainage or source control. It does not present as a single fluctuant collection amenable to immediate incision.
  24. The correct answer is A (The cavernous sinus is a shared venous structure connecting both orbits): The cavernous sinus is a shared venous structure connecting both orbits, which explains why infection reaching it via valveless facial and ophthalmic veins produces bilateral eye involvement. This bilateral nature is a key differentiating feature from unilateral periorbital cellulitis. The other anatomical relationships described do not connect the two orbits in this way.
  25. The correct answer is C (They are valveless, permitting bidirectional flow of infected material): Facial and ophthalmic veins are valveless, permitting infected thrombus to travel directly from the face into the cranial venous system rather than being restricted to one-way drainage away from the skull. This anatomical feature explains why dental infection can reach the cavernous sinus. Valved, bidirectionally-restricted drainage would not permit this spread.
  26. The correct answer is C (The bilateral nature of the eye involvement): The bilateral nature of the eye involvement most reliably differentiates cavernous sinus thrombosis from a simple unilateral periorbital cellulitis, since the cavernous sinus is a shared venous structure serving both orbits. Fever and facial swelling alone are less specific findings. Percussion tenderness of the causative tooth does not distinguish between these two conditions.
  27. The correct answer is A (The third, fourth and sixth cranial nerves and the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve): The third, fourth and sixth cranial nerves and the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve all pass through or near the cavernous sinus, explaining the ophthalmoplegia seen in cavernous sinus thrombosis. The other cranial nerve groupings listed are not anatomically related to the cavernous sinus. This ophthalmoplegia accompanies proptosis and chemosis in the clinical picture.
  28. The correct answer is D (Horizontal gene transfer via plasmids): Horizontal gene transfer via plasmids allows bacteria to acquire resistance genes from unrelated species, including between different species, independent of bacterial reproduction. This is a key mechanism by which antimicrobial resistance spreads through a bacterial population. Vertical transmission and spontaneous mutation describe different, less efficient routes of resistance acquisition.
  29. The correct answer is B (Enzymatic drug inactivation through beta-lactamase production): Beta-lactamase production is an example of enzymatic drug inactivation, in which the enzyme hydrolyses the antibiotic's core ring structure before it can bind its target, conferring penicillin resistance. Efflux pumps, reduced permeability and altered target sites are separate resistance mechanisms not described by this scenario. Each mechanism reduces or eliminates antibiotic effectiveness differently.
  30. The correct answer is A (Definitive treatment such as drainage or extraction, without antibiotics): UK stewardship guidance is built around the principle that antibiotics are not a substitute for definitive dental treatment. A well-defined, drainable fluctuant swelling with no systemic involvement should be managed by removing the source of infection through drainage, extraction or root canal treatment, not by prescribing antibiotics. Antibiotics do not resolve the underlying dental cause of infection.
  31. The correct answer is C (Evidence of spreading cellulitis with trismus and facial swelling beyond the tooth): Evidence of spreading cellulitis with trismus and facial swelling beyond the immediate dental area is one of the specific circumstances in which antibiotics are indicated in dentistry. A small, well-localised fluctuant swelling or an asymptomatic enamel lesion does not meet this threshold. Antibiotics treat systemic spread rather than replacing definitive source control.
  32. The correct answer is B (Clarithromycin): Clarithromycin is an appropriate first-line alternative to amoxicillin for a penicillin-allergic patient requiring antibiotics for a spreading odontogenic infection, alongside metronidazole where anaerobic cover is needed. Vancomycin, gentamicin and ciprofloxacin are not first-line agents for straightforward odontogenic infections in UK guidance. The choice reflects appropriate, targeted stewardship-consistent prescribing.
  33. The correct answer is D (It is not routinely recommended, though individualised cardiology-led decisions may apply to the highest-risk patients): Antibiotic prophylaxis for infective endocarditis prevention is not routinely recommended for dental procedures in patients with structural cardiac risk factors under current UK guidance, though individualised decisions are sometimes made in consultation with a cardiologist for those at genuinely highest risk. It is not mandatory for every prosthetic device or proven to eliminate all endocarditis risk.
  34. The correct answer is D (Bacteraemia occurs far more often from routine activities such as toothbrushing than from one dental visit): Bacteraemia occurs far more often from routine activities such as toothbrushing than from an isolated dental procedure, so the balance of evidence suggests routine antibiotic prophylaxis offers little benefit while contributing to adverse effects and antimicrobial resistance. Dental bacteraemia does occur and immunity to endocarditis-causing organisms is not universal. This underpins current UK guidance against routine prophylaxis.
  35. The correct answer is B (A short, appropriately targeted course guided by clinical severity rather than routine broad-spectrum use): A short, appropriately targeted course guided by clinical severity reflects stewardship-consistent prescribing, rather than routinely prescribing broad-spectrum agents or extending courses beyond the point needed for the immune system and definitive treatment to take over. Withholding antibiotics when spreading systemic infection is present would be inappropriate. Random rotation between antibiotic classes has no clinical basis.
  36. The correct answer is C (Excessive use creates selection pressure favouring survival and dominance of resistant organisms): Excessive or inappropriate antibiotic use creates selection pressure that favours the survival and dominance of resistant bacterial strains within a population, since susceptible organisms are killed while resistant ones persist and multiply. Antibiotics do not mutate human DNA, and resistant bacteria are not inherently less able to reproduce. This principle underpins the drive for responsible prescribing.
  37. The correct answer is A (Cleaning, because organic debris can shield microorganisms and inactivate chemical disinfectants): Cleaning, the physical removal of visible debris, blood and organic material, must always occur before disinfection or sterilisation because organic debris can shield microorganisms from subsequent processes and inactivate chemical disinfectants. Sterilisation or packaging without prior cleaning would not reliably decontaminate an instrument. This mandatory sequence is set out in HTM 01-05.
  38. The correct answer is C (An ultrasonic bath or washer-disinfector): An ultrasonic bath or washer-disinfector is preferred under HTM 01-05 over manual scrubbing because it improves consistency of contamination removal and reduces the risk of sharps injury to staff compared with manual handling. Soaking alone or wiping with dry gauze would not achieve adequate cleaning. This preference reflects both safety and quality assurance considerations.
  39. The correct answer is B (Sterilisation destroys all microbial life including spores, while disinfection does not necessarily destroy spores): Sterilisation is the complete destruction or removal of all forms of microbial life, including bacterial spores, whereas disinfection reduces viable microorganisms to a level not harmful to health without necessarily destroying all spores. The two terms are not interchangeable, and temperature requirements or debris removal do not define this distinction. Sterilisation is required for instruments contacting bone, blood or other bodily fluids.
  40. The correct answer is D (A vacuum autoclave): A vacuum autoclave actively removes air from the chamber before introducing pressurised steam, ensuring steam penetrates hollow and complex instruments such as handpieces far more reliably than alternative methods. This is the required standard in UK primary dental care for such instruments. Dry heat ovens and ultraviolet cabinets do not achieve equivalent steam penetration of narrow lumens.
  41. The correct answer is A (Air pockets may remain within the lumen, preventing adequate steam penetration and sterilisation): A non-vacuum, displacement autoclave relies on air being pushed out by steam rather than actively removed, which can leave air pockets that prevent adequate sterilisation of instrument lumens such as those in a handpiece. It does not sterilise more rapidly, extend cleaning time, or remove the need for traceability records. This is precisely why vacuum autoclaves are required for such instruments.
  42. The correct answer is A (It must not be reprocessed or reused, regardless of the decontamination cycle undertaken): Single-use items, where specified by the manufacturer, must never be reprocessed or reused regardless of the decontamination cycle undertaken, since their materials or design may not withstand sterilisation or may harbour biofilm that cannot be reliably removed once used. They cannot safely be reused even once. Soaking in chlorhexidine does not make reprocessing acceptable.
  43. The correct answer is D (Infection status cannot always be known or disclosed by the patient): Standard precautions are applied to every patient regardless of known infection status because infection status cannot always be known or disclosed. This baseline approach includes personal protective equipment, safe sharps handling, hand hygiene and safe waste management for all patients. It is not restricted to surgical procedures or particular age groups.
  44. The correct answer is B (Encourage gentle bleeding under running water without scrubbing the wound): The affected area should be encouraged to bleed gently under running water without scrubbing, since scrubbing could force pathogens deeper into tissue. It should then be washed thoroughly with soap and water before the incident is reported. A tourniquet or repeated squeezing are not part of the correct first aid protocol for a sharps injury.
  45. The correct answer is C (Post-exposure prophylaxis for HIV is most effective when started as soon as possible after exposure): Post-exposure prophylaxis for HIV is most effective when started as soon as possible after exposure, which is why prompt referral following a significant sharps injury is time-critical. HIV transmission via sharps injury, though less efficient than Hepatitis B, remains a genuine risk. Prophylaxis is not restricted to Hepatitis B exposures alone, and there is a defined effective time window for HIV.
  46. The correct answer is D (Hepatitis B virus): Hepatitis B virus is highly infectious via blood and body fluid exposure, considerably more so than Hepatitis C or HIV for an equivalent exposure, which is precisely why vaccination against Hepatitis B is a mandatory occupational requirement for UK dental healthcare workers. Hepatitis A is not primarily bloodborne in the same clinically relevant occupational sense. This explains the differing vaccination policies.
  47. The correct answer is A (No vaccine currently exists against Hepatitis C, so standard precautions are the primary prevention): No vaccine currently exists against Hepatitis C, making standard precautions and safe sharps handling the primary preventive measures against this bloodborne virus. Hepatitis C is bloodborne and can establish chronic infection in a majority of those infected. This absence of a vaccine is precisely why Hepatitis B vaccination is mandated instead.
  48. The correct answer is C (Hepatitis B virus): Hepatitis B virus can cause acute hepatitis and, in a proportion of adult infections, progresses to chronic infection with long-term risks of cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma. Rhinovirus, influenza virus and herpes simplex virus type 1 are not bloodborne viruses associated with this pattern of chronic liver disease. This risk underpins the mandatory vaccination policy for dental staff.
  49. The correct answer is B (Appropriate consent): Appropriate consent must be obtained from the source patient before their bloodborne virus status can be sought as part of a post-exposure risk assessment. This respects patient confidentiality while still allowing the exposed worker's risk to be properly assessed. A court order or waiver from the treating dentist is not the standard route for this consent.
  50. The correct answer is B (Routine generation of blood and aerosols alongside sharp instruments near mucous membranes): Dental procedures routinely generate blood and aerosols and involve sharp instruments in close proximity to mucous membranes, which together explain the increased occupational risk of bloodborne virus exposure in dentistry. Sharp instruments remain a routine feature of dental treatment. Dental patients are not inherently more likely to carry bloodborne viruses than other patients.
  51. The correct answer is C (Ideally within hours, and certainly within seventy-two hours): Post-exposure prophylaxis with antiretroviral medication for HIV is considered most effective when commenced ideally within hours of exposure and certainly within seventy-two hours. Waiting for laboratory confirmation of seroconversion or delaying for months would substantially reduce its effectiveness. This time-critical window is why prompt referral after a significant exposure matters.
  52. The correct answer is A (Salivary lysozyme and antimicrobial peptides providing rapid, non-specific chemical defence): Salivary lysozyme and antimicrobial peptides provide rapid, non-specific chemical defence and are components of innate immunity, alongside physical barriers and cellular components such as neutrophils and macrophages. B lymphocytes, helper T cells and cytotoxic T cells are components of adaptive immunity, which is specific and memory-based rather than rapid and non-specific.
  53. The correct answer is D (Neutrophils): Neutrophils form the front-line cellular defence against subgingival plaque bacteria within the gingival crevice, and both a numerical deficiency and a functional defect in their activity, as seen in certain rare congenital disorders, are associated with severe, early-onset periodontal destruction. Erythrocytes, platelets and eosinophils do not play this specific defensive role in the gingival crevice.
  54. The correct answer is B (Much of the tissue damage results from the host's own dysregulated inflammatory response to the biofilm): Much of the tissue damage seen in periodontitis, including alveolar bone loss, results substantially from the host's own dysregulated inflammatory and immune response to the bacterial challenge, rather than from bacterial toxins alone. Bone loss is not independent of the biofilm, and neutrophils are not solely responsible for all collagen breakdown observed. This host-response concept is a key integrated theme in periodontology.
  55. The correct answer is C (Interleukin-1, interleukin-6 and tumour necrosis factor-alpha): Interleukin-1, interleukin-6 and tumour necrosis factor-alpha are pro-inflammatory cytokines implicated in driving collagen breakdown and osteoclastic resorption of alveolar bone in periodontitis, alongside matrix metalloproteinases. Insulin, glucagon, cortisol and the other hormones listed are not the cytokines specifically driving this inflammatory bone loss process. This reflects the host-mediated destructive pathway in periodontal disease.
  56. The correct answer is A (B lymphocytes): B lymphocytes are the cells primarily responsible for producing antibodies as part of the adaptive immune response, providing specific, memory-based protection. Neutrophils, macrophages and complement proteins are components of innate immunity rather than antibody-producing adaptive cells. This distinction is central to understanding how the two arms of immunity interact in periodontal disease.
  57. The correct answer is D (Tissue destruction results substantially from a dysregulated host response to a dysbiotic biofilm): Periodontitis is now understood as fundamentally an inflammatory disease because tissue destruction results substantially from a dysregulated host response to a dysbiotic biofilm, rather than from bacteria directly invading and destroying bone without host involvement. The immune system plays a central, not absent, role in this process. The disease process is not independent of subgingival biofilm composition.
  58. The correct answer is D (Type I, immunoglobulin E-mediated hypersensitivity to natural rubber latex proteins): Latex allergy is a genuine Type I, immunoglobulin E-mediated hypersensitivity reaction to proteins found in natural rubber latex, and localised contact urticaria with no systemic symptoms is consistent with this mechanism. Type IV, Type II and Type III reactions describe different immunological mechanisms not typically responsible for this presentation. This distinction guides whether strict latex avoidance is required.
  59. The correct answer is A (Irritant contact dermatitis from friction and occlusion, not a true allergy): Irritant contact dermatitis is a much more common but non-immunological reaction to friction, occlusion or exposure to glove powder, and does not require the same strict avoidance protocol as a true allergy. There is no feature here suggesting a Type I or Type IV latex allergy or anaphylaxis. It is still uncomfortable for the affected individual and worth addressing.
  60. The correct answer is C (Use of latex-free gloves, dam and other clinical materials throughout treatment): A documented latex allergy requires the use of latex-free gloves, dam and other clinical materials throughout treatment, given the risk of localised or, in severe cases, systemic anaphylactic reactions. Continuing to use any latex-containing materials, even briefly or selectively, would place the patient at ongoing risk. Verbal information alone does not remove the physical exposure risk.
  61. The correct answer is B (A vasovagal episode related to anxiety or pain): A vasovagal episode, by far the more common event following local anaesthetic administration, typically arises from anxiety, pain or the sight of the needle, presenting with pallor, sweating, bradycardia and brief loss of consciousness that resolves rapidly once the patient is laid supine with legs raised. A true allergic reaction or anaphylaxis would not typically resolve this quickly with repositioning alone.
  62. The correct answer is A (The preservative or sulphite-based antioxidant added to the formulation): A true allergic reaction to local anaesthetic is genuinely rare, and where a reaction does occur it is more often attributable to the preservative methylparaben or the sulphite-based antioxidant added to formulations containing adrenaline, rather than to the anaesthetic molecule itself. Modern amide local anaesthetics such as lidocaine are seldom the true allergen. Buffering agents and colourants are not typically implicated.
  63. The correct answer is D (Mislabelling a faint as an allergy can permanently deny the patient the most effective anaesthetic agents): Mislabelling a vasovagal faint as an allergy can permanently and unnecessarily deny a patient the most effective local anaesthetic agents, while failing to recognise a true allergic reaction risks a dangerous re-exposure at a future appointment. The two conditions do not require identical management, and a straightforward vasovagal episode does not always require hospital admission. Correctly identifying the event matters for future treatment planning.
  64. The correct answer is B (Hepatitis B is highly transmissible via blood exposure and is preventable by an available vaccine): Hepatitis B is highly transmissible via blood exposure and is preventable by an available vaccine, which together justify making vaccination a mandatory occupational health requirement for UK dental healthcare workers. Hepatitis B does progress to chronic infection in a proportion of adults, and Hepatitis C is not more infectious per exposure than Hepatitis B. No equivalent vaccine exists for Hepatitis C, unlike Hepatitis B.
  65. The correct answer is C (Antibody to hepatitis B surface antigen): Antibody to hepatitis B surface antigen is the specific antibody level measured some weeks after completion of the vaccine course to confirm successful seroconversion. A proportion of individuals do not seroconvert adequately and may require a booster course or further investigation. The other listed antibodies relate to entirely different viral infections and are not used to confirm Hepatitis B vaccine response.
  66. The correct answer is C (A dysregulated host inflammatory response to an accumulating dental biofilm): Generalised gingival erythema of the type shown reflects a dysregulated host inflammatory response to an accumulating dental biofilm at the gingival margin, consistent with gingivitis. It is not due to absence of bacterial colonisation, purely mechanical injury, or a viral exanthem unrelated to plaque. This finding should prompt reinforcement of plaque control measures.
  67. The correct answer is B (Calculus, formed by mineralisation of an established bacterial biofilm): The hard, mineralised deposit visible on the lingual surfaces of the lower incisors is calculus, formed by mineralisation of an established bacterial biofilm through deposition of calcium and phosphate salts from saliva. It is not the acquired pellicle, which forms within minutes and is not visible or hard, nor is it a carious cavitation or fibrous hyperplasia.
  68. The correct answer is D (Extrinsic staining from tobacco use and dietary chromogens on the tooth surface): Brown discolouration on posterior teeth in a patient who smokes is most likely extrinsic staining from tobacco use and dietary chromogens adhering to the tooth surface, distinct from intrinsic developmental defects. Active subgingival deposition of red complex bacteria and a reaction to amoxicillin do not typically produce this surface appearance. Extrinsic stain can usually be removed by professional cleaning.
  69. The correct answer is A (An established subgingival biofilm driving a host inflammatory response at the gingival margin): Swollen gingival tissue with visible calculus deposits and bleeding on gentle probing reflects an established subgingival biofilm driving a host inflammatory response at the gingival margin, consistent with gingivitis or early periodontal involvement. This is not explained by trauma from an orthodontic appliance, a fungal infection, or normal healthy architecture. Removal of the calculus and improved plaque control would be the appropriate response.
  70. The correct answer is D (A recognised side effect of prolonged chlorhexidine use causing extrinsic tooth discolouration): Brownish extrinsic staining associated with gingival recession in a patient using long-term chlorhexidine mouthwash is a recognised side effect of prolonged chlorhexidine use, causing extrinsic tooth discolouration rather than reflecting an allergic reaction or ongoing red complex colonisation staining the enamel directly. The staining does not indicate that the chlorhexidine has failed to reduce plaque biofilm, since its antiplaque effect is separate from its staining tendency.
EMQ Set EMQ-micro-1

For each clinical scenario below, select the ONE most likely causative organism or pathogen group from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Streptococcus mutans

B. Porphyromonas gingivalis

C. Fusobacterium nucleatum

D. Streptococcus anginosus group

E. Aggregatibacter actinomycetemcomitans

F. Candida albicans

G. Treponema denticola

H. Lactobacillus species

  1. The correct answer is H (Lactobacillus species): they become prominent once a carious lesion has already formed, contributing to its progression rather than initiating it, unlike Streptococcus mutans which drives the earliest demineralisation. This distinction between aetiology and progression is a recurring examination theme. High counts of an aciduric organism within an already-cavitated lesion are most consistent with this pattern.
  2. The correct answer is B (Porphyromonas gingivalis): it is considered a keystone pathogen because even at relatively low abundance it disproportionately disrupts subgingival community balance and dysregulates the host immune response. This is strongly associated with deep pocketing and attachment loss seen in established periodontitis. The other organisms listed do not carry this specific keystone designation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Streptococcus anginosus group): these facultative anaerobic streptococci are commonly implicated in dental abscess formation as part of the typical polymicrobial mixture found in odontogenic infections. The other organisms listed are either associated with caries, periodontal disease, or fungal infection rather than typical abscess pus culture. Odontogenic abscesses usually yield a mixed aerobic-anaerobic flora.
  4. The correct answer is C (Fusobacterium nucleatum): it is the key bridging organism because it coaggregates with both early Gram-positive colonisers and late Gram-negative anaerobes, physically linking the two communities as the biofilm matures. No other organism in the list fulfils this specific bridging role. This bridging function is central to understanding biofilm ecological succession.
  5. The correct answer is E (Aggregatibacter actinomycetemcomitans): this Gram-negative organism is classically associated with rapidly progressive, localised attachment loss around the first molars and incisors in aggressive periodontitis affecting younger patients. The other organisms listed are more closely associated with caries, chronic periodontitis, or abscess formation rather than this specific aggressive, localised pattern. This association is a well-recognised examination theme.
EMQ Set EMQ-micro-2

For each scenario below, select the ONE most appropriate protocol or consequence resulting from the described infection control breach from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Encourage gentle bleeding under running water, then wash and report immediately

B. Reprocess through cleaning, disinfection and sterilisation before reuse

C. Discard the item and never reprocess it under any circumstances

D. Refer promptly for a formal bloodborne virus risk assessment with consent sought

E. Repeat the full sterilisation cycle in a vacuum autoclave from the beginning

F. Undertake a full incident report and review of decontamination room workflow

G. Recall the affected patient batch and inform the practice's clinical governance lead

H. Escalate immediately for consideration of post-exposure prophylaxis

  1. The correct answer is A (Encourage gentle bleeding under running water, then wash and report immediately): this is the mandatory first aid step at the moment of a sharps injury, since scrubbing could force pathogens deeper into tissue. Reporting and risk assessment follow this immediate first aid. The other options describe later steps in the overall incident pathway, not the first action required.
  2. The correct answer is C (Discard the item and never reprocess it under any circumstances): single-use items, where specified by the manufacturer, must never be reprocessed regardless of any cleaning already undertaken, since their materials or design may not withstand sterilisation or may harbour biofilm. Returning it to stock after cleaning does not make reuse acceptable. This protects both the current and future patients from cross-infection risk.
  3. The correct answer is H (Escalate immediately for consideration of post-exposure prophylaxis): because the source patient's Hepatitis B surface antigen positive status is already known, the most urgent next clinical step after first aid and reporting is prompt referral for consideration of prophylaxis, which is time-critical for effectiveness. A formal risk assessment would ordinarily precede this when the source status is unknown, but here it is already established. Delay would reduce the effectiveness of any intervention.
  4. The correct answer is E (Repeat the full sterilisation cycle in a vacuum autoclave from the beginning): a failed steam penetration indicator on a non-vacuum cycle means the instrument cannot be considered adequately sterilised, particularly for a handpiece with a narrow lumen. It must not be used until properly reprocessed. This reflects the requirement for reliable steam penetration set out in HTM 01-05.
  5. The correct answer is F (Undertake a full incident report and review of decontamination room workflow): inadequate segregation of clean and dirty instrument workflows is a systemic infection control failure requiring a formal review of practice processes, not simply a single-instrument fix. This protects against ongoing cross-contamination risk across the whole decontamination pathway. HTM 01-05 requires appropriate segregation of these workflows.
EMQ Set EMQ-micro-3

For each scenario below, select the ONE most likely mechanism or management approach from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Vasovagal episode managed by laying the patient supine with legs raised

B. Type I, IgE-mediated hypersensitivity requiring avoidance of the specific allergen

C. Irritant contact dermatitis requiring no strict avoidance protocol

D. Anaphylaxis requiring immediate intramuscular adrenaline and emergency referral

E. Innate immune response mediated by neutrophils and complement

F. Adaptive immune response mediated by memory B and T lymphocytes

G. Reaction to a preservative or antioxidant additive rather than the anaesthetic itself

H. Neutrophil dysfunction associated with early-onset periodontal destruction

  1. The correct answer is D (Anaphylaxis requiring immediate intramuscular adrenaline and emergency referral): widespread urticaria, facial angioedema, wheeze and hypotension developing over minutes represent a severe, systemic allergic reaction requiring emergency management rather than simple repositioning. A vasovagal episode would not typically produce angioedema or wheeze. This presentation demands immediate emergency treatment.
  2. The correct answer is H (Neutrophil dysfunction associated with early-onset periodontal destruction): neutrophils form the front-line cellular defence against subgingival plaque bacteria, and a congenital defect in their function is classically linked to severe, early-onset periodontal destruction even in the primary dentition. The other options describe hypersensitivity reactions or adaptive immune mechanisms, not this innate cellular defect. This link illustrates the importance of neutrophil function to periodontal health.
  3. The correct answer is A (Vasovagal episode managed by laying the patient supine with legs raised): pallor and brief loss of consciousness at the sight of a needle, with rapid full recovery after repositioning, is characteristic of a vasovagal episode related to anxiety rather than a true allergic reaction. This is by far the more common event following, or even before, local anaesthetic administration. It does not require adrenaline or emergency referral.
  4. The correct answer is G (Reaction to a preservative or antioxidant additive rather than the anaesthetic itself): methylparaben present in some multi-dose vials is a recognised cause of reactions previously misattributed to the amide anaesthetic molecule. True allergy to modern amide local anaesthetics such as lidocaine is genuinely rare. Correctly identifying the causative agent avoids unnecessarily denying the patient effective anaesthetic agents in future.
  5. The correct answer is C (Irritant contact dermatitis requiring no strict avoidance protocol): dry, cracked skin from friction and occlusion with glove use, in the absence of true allergic sensitisation on patch testing, is a much more common but non-immunological reaction. It improves with measures such as barrier cream rather than requiring strict material avoidance. This should be distinguished from a true Type I latex hypersensitivity reaction.
  6. The correct answer is B (300 mg, the absolute maximum stated in UK dental guidance): lidocaine's maximum recommended dose is 4.4 mg/kg body weight, but UK dental guidance caps this at an absolute maximum of 300 mg regardless of patient weight. This absolute ceiling protects larger patients from an mg/kg calculation exceeding a safe total dose. Calculating both the mg/kg figure and checking it against the absolute maximum is essential exam knowledge for every local anaesthetic used in dentistry.
  7. The correct answer is D (It should be avoided because of an association with prolonged paraesthesia): GDC and UK guidance are clear that articaine should not be used for inferior alveolar nerve blocks because of its association with prolonged paraesthesia, thought to relate to its higher concentration and possible direct neurotoxicity near the nerve trunk. Articaine's good bone penetration instead makes it popular for buccal infiltration of mandibular molars. Choosing an alternative amide anaesthetic for a mandibular block avoids this recognised risk.
  8. The correct answer is A (Prilocaine 3% with felypressin 0.03 IU/mL as the vasoconstrictor): prilocaine with felypressin is preferred when the clinician wishes to minimise cardiovascular stimulation, such as in patients taking non-selective beta-blockers where interaction with adrenaline could theoretically provoke a hypertensive response. Felypressin produces vasoconstriction through direct action on vascular smooth muscle rather than adrenergic receptors. This avoids the theoretical interaction risk that adrenaline-containing solutions carry in this patient group.
  9. The correct answer is C (Early central nervous system local anaesthetic systemic toxicity): circumoral tingling, metallic taste, and tinnitus are classic early central nervous system signs of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity, occurring at lower plasma concentrations before cardiovascular effects develop. This pattern reflects sodium channel blockade affecting excitable neural tissue first. Recognising these early warning signs allows the injection to be stopped before progression to seizures or cardiovascular collapse.
  10. The correct answer is D (Stop the injection, give high-flow oxygen, and monitor vital signs): immediate management of suspected LAST begins with stopping the injection, calling for help, maintaining the airway with high-flow oxygen, and continuously monitoring vital signs while arranging urgent hospital transfer. Intravenous lipid emulsion is a hospital-based intervention, not something expected of a general dental practitioner. Supportive measures and rapid escalation are the practitioner's correct immediate role.
  11. The correct answer is B (Mepivacaine 3% plain, given without any vasoconstrictor added): mepivacaine 3% plain is used without a vasoconstrictor for very short procedures or where vasoconstrictors are contraindicated, though its pulpal anaesthesia duration is shorter than lidocaine with adrenaline. Its maximum dose is 4.4 mg/kg up to 300 mg. This makes it the appropriate choice when a vasoconstrictor-free local anaesthetic is specifically required.
  12. The correct answer is A (Reduced systemic absorption and a prolonged duration of anaesthesia): adrenaline added to lidocaine reduces systemic absorption, prolongs the duration of anaesthesia, reduces bleeding at the surgical site, and lowers the peak plasma concentration of the local anaesthetic itself. This vasoconstrictor effect is the main pharmacological rationale for including adrenaline in dental local anaesthetic cartridges. It has no independent analgesic action of its own.
  13. The correct answer is C (88 milligrams of articaine within the cartridge): a 2.2 mL cartridge of articaine 4% contains 88 mg of drug, since 4% equals 40 mg per mL and 2.2 mL multiplied by 40 mg/mL gives 88 mg. Candidates must be comfortable converting cartridge volume and percentage concentration into an absolute milligram dose under exam pressure. This calculation is essential for checking a patient's total dose against the maximum of 7 mg/kg up to 500 mg.
  14. The correct answer is D (Anaemic patients and infants, who are at greater methaemoglobinaemia risk): prilocaine's metabolite ortho-toluidine can oxidise haemoglobin to methaemoglobin, so doses should be kept conservative in patients who are already anaemic, in those with pre-existing methaemoglobinaemia, and in infants. This is a specific additional risk not shared equally by other amide local anaesthetics. The maximum recommended dose of prilocaine is 6 mg/kg up to 400 mg.
  15. The correct answer is A (High doses can exert an oxytocic effect on uterine smooth muscle): felypressin is a synthetic vasopressin analogue that at high doses has an oxytocic effect on uterine smooth muscle, so it is used with caution in pregnancy even though dental doses are far below any dose shown to cause harm. This caution is specific to felypressin's mechanism rather than a general local anaesthetic toxicity concern. It does not cross the placenta to sedate the fetus or cause maternal hypertension.
  16. The correct answer is C (Paracetamol 1 g four times daily, maximum 4 g daily): paracetamol is first-line for most dental pain because of its favourable safety profile, with no significant effect on platelet function and no association with gastric ulceration. The standard adult dose is 1 g four times daily with a maximum daily dose of 4 g in 24 hours. Prescribers must always check for other paracetamol-containing preparations the patient may already be taking.
  17. The correct answer is B (NSAIDs such as ibuprofen, given their effect on prostaglandins): NSAIDs are used with caution in significant renal impairment because prostaglandins help maintain renal blood flow, particularly in states of reduced renal perfusion, and NSAID-induced prostaglandin inhibition can precipitate acute kidney injury. Paracetamol lacks this renal risk. This is why NSAID use requires particular caution in patients with chronic kidney disease.
  18. The correct answer is D (Acute kidney injury from the recognised triple whammy interaction): combining an NSAID with an ACE inhibitor and a diuretic creates the recognised triple whammy risk of acute kidney injury, since each drug independently reduces renal perfusion or prostaglandin-mediated compensation. This combination should prompt caution or avoidance of NSAID prescribing. It is unrelated to alcohol reactions, paraesthesia, or methaemoglobinaemia, which involve entirely different drugs.
  19. The correct answer is A (The patient is a CYP2D6 poor metaboliser of codeine to morphine): codeine is a prodrug that depends on hepatic CYP2D6 metabolism to its active metabolite morphine, and a significant proportion of the population are poor metabolisers who gain little analgesic benefit from standard doses. Conversely, ultra-rapid metabolisers are at risk of exaggerated opioid effect. This pharmacogenetic variability explains inconsistent codeine efficacy across different patients.
  20. The correct answer is B (Codeine is contraindicated in children under 12 years of age): codeine is specifically contraindicated in children under 12 and in breastfeeding mothers because of case reports of fatal respiratory depression in ultra-rapid metaboliser infants exposed through breast milk. This is a firmly established UK prescribing restriction. Paracetamol and NSAID combinations remain the appropriate analgesic approach in this age group instead.
  21. The correct answer is C (They risk premature closure of the fetal ductus arteriosus): NSAIDs are avoided in the third trimester of pregnancy because of the risk of premature closure of the ductus arteriosus, a serious fetal cardiovascular complication. This is a well-established and recurrently tested obstetric pharmacology point relevant to dental prescribing. Paracetamol remains the appropriate analgesic choice throughout pregnancy instead.
  22. The correct answer is D (Amoxicillin 500 mg three times daily for 5 days): amoxicillin is first-line for dental infections in UK guidance, typically prescribed at 500 mg three times daily for 5 days, chosen for its broad spectrum against mixed oral flora, good bioavailability, and favourable side-effect profile. It is given as an adjunct alongside definitive treatment such as drainage, never as a substitute for it. This regimen applies once systemic signs of spreading infection justify antibiotic use.
  23. The correct answer is A (It characteristically provokes a widespread maculopapular rash in this condition): amoxicillin characteristically provokes a widespread maculopapular rash in patients with unrecognised glandular fever, so it should be avoided or used with great caution when infectious mononucleosis is suspected. This reaction is not a true penicillin allergy but a well-documented drug-disease interaction. An alternative antibiotic should be considered in this specific clinical scenario.
  24. The correct answer is C (Avoid alcohol during treatment and for 48 hours afterwards): metronidazole produces a disulfiram-like reaction of flushing, nausea, vomiting, tachycardia, and headache when combined with alcohol, so patients must be advised to avoid alcohol during treatment and for 48 hours after finishing the course. This interaction applies to alcohol in any form, not selectively to spirits. It is one of the most reliably tested drug interactions in dental pharmacology.
  25. The correct answer is B (Closer INR monitoring is needed as metronidazole potentiates warfarin): metronidazole significantly potentiates warfarin by inhibiting its hepatic metabolism, increasing INR and bleeding risk, so patients on warfarin who require metronidazole need closer INR monitoring during and after the course. This interaction is a recurrently tested exam point distinct from the alcohol interaction. It is unrelated to glucose control or renal function.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Severe or persistent diarrhoea suggesting possible C. difficile infection): clindamycin has a strong association with Clostridioides difficile-associated diarrhoea and pseudomembranous colitis, so patients should be warned to stop the drug and seek medical attention promptly if severe or persistent diarrhoea develops. Any patient developing diarrhoea during or shortly after a clindamycin course should be evaluated for C. difficile infection rather than simply reassured. This restricted role reflects current UK dental prescribing guidance.
  2. The correct answer is D (Provide drainage or extraction without routinely prescribing antibiotics): antibiotics alone should never be prescribed for a localised dental abscess in an otherwise well patient when definitive treatment can be provided; the correct management is drainage of pus and/or extraction. Antibiotics are reserved as an adjunct only if there are systemic signs of spreading infection. This reflects the core antimicrobial stewardship principle in UK dental prescribing.
  3. The correct answer is C (Routine prophylaxis is not recommended for most such patients): current UK guidance, following NICE, no longer recommends routine antibiotic prophylaxis for infective endocarditis prevention before dental procedures in patients with cardiac conditions previously considered at risk. Case-by-case discussion is reserved only for the smallest very-high-risk subgroup under current updated guidance. This represents a significant shift away from historically routine prophylactic prescribing.
  4. The correct answer is B (Metronidazole, since it is not a penicillin-class antibiotic): metronidazole is used either as an alternative to amoxicillin or in combination with it for spreading dental infections, and it is not a penicillin-class antibiotic, making it a safe choice in a documented penicillin allergy. It is particularly effective against anaerobic bacteria commonly implicated in dental infections. Amoxicillin and co-amoxiclav must both be avoided entirely in a true penicillin allergy.
  5. The correct answer is A (Sufficient systemic absorption inhibits CYP2C9 and potentiates warfarin): although miconazole oral gel is applied topically, sufficient systemic absorption occurs to inhibit the hepatic cytochrome P450 enzyme CYP2C9, markedly potentiating warfarin's anticoagulant effect and increasing INR and bleeding risk. This is one of the most reliably tested drug interactions in ORE Part 1 pharmacology. Miconazole gel is therefore contraindicated in patients taking warfarin.
  6. The correct answer is D (Interaction with statins that increases myopathy and rhabdomyolysis risk): fluconazole is a significant inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 and interacts with statins metabolised through these pathways, increasing the risk of myopathy and rhabdomyolysis, as well as potentiating warfarin and interacting with sulfonylureas to increase hypoglycaemia risk. This broad interaction profile requires careful medication review before prescribing fluconazole. Felypressin, buccal midazolam, and amoxicillin are not part of this interaction pattern.
  7. The correct answer is B (Nystatin suspension, which has negligible systemic absorption): nystatin suspension is a topical polyene antifungal with negligible systemic absorption, making it a reasonable choice where drug interactions such as warfarin potentiation are a concern. Both miconazole and fluconazole carry significant CYP2C9-mediated warfarin interactions and should be avoided in this patient. Nystatin requires drug contact time in the mouth but avoids the interaction risk entirely.
  8. The correct answer is C (Correcting denture hygiene and reducing nocturnal denture wear): oral candidiasis is commonly precipitated by denture wear, particularly with poor denture hygiene or nocturnal wearing, and management always begins with identifying and correcting this predisposing factor alongside antifungal treatment. Antifungal therapy alone without addressing denture hygiene will simply result in recurrence. Prophylactic antibiotics and stopping anticoagulants are not appropriate management steps for this condition.
  9. The correct answer is A (A minimum of 30% oxygen is administered throughout the sedation): nitrous oxide inhalation sedation is administered with a minimum of 30% oxygen, giving it an extremely favourable safety profile while preserving protective airway reflexes and the patient's ability to respond to verbal commands throughout. This is the defining feature of conscious sedation as opposed to general anaesthesia. Its low blood-gas solubility also allows rapid reversibility on cessation.
  10. The correct answer is D (Diffusion hypoxia as nitrous oxide rapidly leaves the bloodstream): administering 100% oxygen at the end of the procedure prevents diffusion hypoxia, which can occur as nitrous oxide rapidly leaves the bloodstream and dilutes alveolar oxygen concentration during recovery. This short recovery period is a mandatory step in every inhalation sedation appointment. It is unrelated to methaemoglobinaemia, which involves a different mechanism entirely.
  11. The correct answer is C (Potentiation of GABA-A receptor activity, increasing chloride channel opening): midazolam produces sedation, anxiolysis, and anterograde amnesia through potentiation of GABA-A receptor activity, increasing chloride channel opening frequency and hyperpolarising the neuronal membrane. This mechanism is distinct from local anaesthetic sodium channel blockade or opioid metabolism pathways. It underlies both the sedative benefit and the respiratory depression risk of midazolam.
  12. The correct answer is B (Verrill's sign, a slight ptosis of the upper eyelids): Verrill's sign, a slight ptosis, is the characteristic clinical endpoint used to judge adequate titrated sedation with intravenous midazolam. Midazolam is titrated slowly to this clinical endpoint rather than to a fixed dose. This distinguishes it from unrelated clinical signs such as Chvostek's sign or abnormal respiratory patterns.
  13. The correct answer is D (Flumazenil has a shorter half-life than midazolam, risking re-sedation): flumazenil has a shorter half-life than midazolam, so there is a genuine risk of re-sedation once the flumazenil effect wears off, meaning the patient must be monitored for a prolonged period afterwards. Flumazenil should not be relied upon as a routine reversal strategy to allow faster discharge. It is reserved for managing over-sedation as a clinical emergency.
  14. The correct answer is A (Continuous pulse oximetry with a trained second appropriate person present): continuous pulse oximetry monitoring and the availability of resuscitation equipment and a trained second appropriate person are mandatory throughout any IV sedation procedure under UK conscious sedation standards. This ensures early detection of respiratory depression, a recognised risk of midazolam sedation. Hourly recording and electroencephalogram monitoring are not the required standard.
  15. The correct answer is C (INR is below 4.0 and local haemostatic measures are used): current UK guidance supports proceeding with routine dental extractions, including multiple extractions, without stopping warfarin provided the INR is below 4.0 on the day of treatment and local haemostatic measures are used. The bleeding risk from stopping anticoagulation and precipitating a thromboembolic event generally outweighs the bleeding risk of the dental procedure. INR should ideally be checked within 24 hours, but up to 72 hours is acceptable for a patient who is stably anticoagulated; a result from one week prior is not appropriate.
  16. The correct answer is B (Proceed without interruption, using local haemostatic measures as needed): current UK dental guidance supports most routine extractions being carried out without interrupting a DOAC such as apixaban, with local haemostatic measures such as sutures and an absorbable haemostatic dressing considered adequate for most patients. Bridging with heparin or blanket hospital referral is not required for routine extractions. Any decision to interrupt anticoagulation should be discussed with the prescribing physician where there is doubt.
  17. The correct answer is D (Risk is significantly higher with high-dose intravenous oncological therapy): MRONJ risk is significantly higher in patients receiving high-dose intravenous bisphosphonates for oncological indications, such as bone metastases, than in patients taking oral bisphosphonates for osteoporosis, where risk is low but not zero. This risk stratification guides how cautiously an extraction should be approached. Age alone does not determine this risk difference.
  18. The correct answer is B (Routine additional steroid cover is often unnecessary in this scenario): current UK guidance has moved toward an individualised, risk-based approach, recognising that for straightforward, minimally invasive procedures with effective local anaesthesia in patients on lower maintenance doses, routine additional steroid cover is often unnecessary. Higher-dose or longer-term systemic corticosteroid patients undergoing more invasive procedures should have their management discussed with their prescribing physician instead. Abrupt cessation of corticosteroids is never appropriate.
  19. The correct answer is C (Do not routinely stop it, given its long skeletal half-life): current UK guidance does not recommend routinely stopping bisphosphonate therapy before dental extraction, since the drug has a very long skeletal half-life and stopping it briefly makes no meaningful difference to MRONJ risk while removing its therapeutic benefit. The emphasis instead is on risk assessment, informed consent, and minimising surgical trauma. Substituting with a DOAC is not a relevant or appropriate alternative.
  20. The correct answer is B (Schedule the extraction for the point of lowest plasma drug concentration): timing an extraction to the point of lowest plasma drug concentration, such as immediately before the next dose is due, is a commonly recommended practical measure for patients on DOACs undergoing dental extraction. This reduces bleeding risk without requiring interruption of anticoagulation. Doubling a subsequent dose or arbitrary weekend scheduling are not recognised management approaches.
  21. The correct answer is D (Adrenaline 1:1000 concentration, 500 micrograms given intramuscularly into the thigh): Resuscitation Council UK adult dosing for anaphylaxis is adrenaline 1:1000, 500 micrograms, which is 0.5 mL of the 1:1000 (1 mg/mL) solution, given by intramuscular injection into the anterolateral thigh without delay, repeated after 5 minutes if there is no improvement. Adrenaline is the single most important drug in anaphylaxis management and should never be withheld. Every UK dental practice is required to stock adrenaline 1:1000 for this purpose.
  22. The correct answer is A (Give oral glucose as a sugary drink, tablets, or gel): in a conscious, cooperative patient with hypoglycaemia who can safely swallow, management is to give oral glucose as a sugary drink, glucose tablets, or a glucose gel placed in the buccal sulcus. Glucagon by intramuscular injection is reserved for a patient who is unconscious or unable to safely swallow. Hydrocortisone and adrenaline are not indicated for uncomplicated hypoglycaemia.
  23. The correct answer is C (Hydrocortisone 100 mg given by intramuscular injection promptly): management of adrenal crisis is to call for emergency help, lie the patient flat with legs raised, give oxygen, and give hydrocortisone 100 mg by intramuscular injection, a standard drug stocked in the UK dental emergency kit specifically for this indication. This addresses the underlying inability to mount an adequate cortisol response to physiological stress. Glucagon and adrenaline are indicated for different emergencies entirely.
  24. The correct answer is B (Buccal midazolam given between the gum and the cheek): if a seizure continues beyond 5 minutes, buccal midazolam is the first-line emergency drug in UK dental practice, given into the buccal mucosa between the gum and the cheek, since it is more practical to administer than rectal diazepam and does not require intravenous access. An emergency ambulance should be called if the seizure does not resolve. Glucagon, hydrocortisone, and adrenaline address entirely different emergencies.
  25. The correct answer is A (No drug treatment is required; management is positional and reassurance-based): vasovagal syncope requires no drug treatment; management is positional, with the patient placed supine with legs raised to restore venous return and cerebral perfusion, alongside reassurance. Rapid spontaneous recovery is expected once this position is adopted. It is important to distinguish this benign presentation from more serious causes of collapse requiring specific pharmacological management, such as hypoglycaemia or anaphylaxis.
  26. The correct answer is D (Miconazole inhibits CYP2C9, potentiating warfarin and raising the INR): the exhibit shows a patient on warfarin newly prescribed miconazole oral gel, and although applied topically, sufficient systemic absorption occurs for miconazole to inhibit CYP2C9, markedly potentiating warfarin and raising the INR, increasing bleeding risk. This is one of the most reliably tested drug interactions in ORE pharmacology. Miconazole gel is contraindicated in patients taking warfarin for this reason.
  27. The correct answer is C (Stop clindamycin and evaluate the patient for C. difficile infection): the exhibit shows a patient on day 6 of clindamycin developing severe watery diarrhoea, a presentation that should prompt stopping the drug and evaluating for C. difficile infection rather than reassurance. Clindamycin's strong association with C. difficile-associated diarrhoea and pseudomembranous colitis makes prompt assessment essential. Increasing the dose or switching antibiotics without assessment would be inappropriate.
  28. The correct answer is B (Avoid amoxicillin and prescribe an appropriate non-penicillin alternative instead): the exhibit shows a documented history of anaphylaxis to penicillin, so amoxicillin must never be prescribed regardless of a referring GP's suggestion, and an appropriate non-penicillin alternative such as metronidazole should be used instead. Reducing the dose or adding an antihistamine does not make a true penicillin allergy safe. The documented allergy takes precedence over any external suggestion.
  29. The correct answer is D (Prilocaine 3% with felypressin 0.03 IU/mL as the vasoconstrictor): the exhibit shows a patient on a non-selective beta-blocker who is anxious about palpitations, and prilocaine with felypressin minimises the theoretical risk of interaction with adrenaline because felypressin acts directly on vascular smooth muscle rather than adrenergic receptors. This makes it the preferred choice for minimising cardiovascular stimulation in this scenario. Adrenaline-containing solutions carry the theoretical interaction risk this patient's history raises.
  30. The correct answer is C (Proceed with informed consent, minimise trauma, consider specialist referral): the exhibit shows a patient on high-dose intravenous zoledronic acid for bone metastases requiring extraction, and current guidance emphasises risk assessment, patient discussion and informed consent, minimising surgical trauma, and considering referral to a specialist for higher-risk patients on intravenous antiresorptive therapy. Stopping the bisphosphonate makes no meaningful difference to MRONJ risk given its long skeletal half-life. Avoiding extraction entirely is not appropriate when the tooth is non-restorable and symptomatic.
EMQ Set EMQ-pharm-1

For each clinical scenario below, select the ONE most appropriate drug or treatment from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Amoxicillin 500 mg three times daily

B. Metronidazole 400 mg three times daily

C. Paracetamol 1 g four times daily

D. Ibuprofen 400 mg three times daily

E. Prilocaine 3% with felypressin

F. Nystatin suspension

G. Buccal midazolam

H. Adrenaline 1:1000 intramuscularly

  1. The correct answer is E (Prilocaine 3% with felypressin): a cardiac patient taking a non-selective beta-blocker requires a local anaesthetic minimising cardiovascular stimulation, and prilocaine with felypressin achieves this because felypressin acts directly on vascular smooth muscle rather than through adrenergic receptors. This avoids the theoretical interaction risk that adrenaline-containing anaesthetics carry with non-selective beta-blockers. It is the preferred choice in this specific clinical scenario.
  2. The correct answer is A (Amoxicillin 500 mg three times daily): amoxicillin is first-line antibiotic therapy for a spreading dental infection with facial swelling, given alongside drainage rather than as a substitute for it. Its broad spectrum against mixed oral flora and favourable side-effect profile make it the standard choice. It should never be prescribed for a localised abscess in a well patient when drainage alone is sufficient.
  3. The correct answer is F (Nystatin suspension): a patient on warfarin with oral candidiasis should avoid antifungals that interact with anticoagulation, and nystatin suspension has negligible systemic absorption, avoiding the CYP2C9-mediated warfarin potentiation seen with miconazole and fluconazole. This makes nystatin the safest antifungal choice when a drug interaction affecting INR must be avoided. It does require adequate contact time in the mouth to be effective.
  4. The correct answer is G (Buccal midazolam): a seizure continuing beyond 5 minutes without spontaneous recovery is managed with buccal midazolam as the first-line emergency drug in UK dental practice, given into the buccal mucosa between the gum and cheek. This does not require intravenous access, making it practical for the dental setting. An emergency ambulance should be called if the seizure does not resolve promptly.
  5. The correct answer is H (Adrenaline 1:1000 intramuscularly): a patient in anaphylaxis with stridor and hypotension requires immediate intramuscular adrenaline 1:1000, the single most important drug in anaphylaxis management, given into the anterolateral thigh without delay. This should never be withheld while awaiting other interventions. Every UK dental practice is required to stock adrenaline 1:1000 for exactly this emergency.
EMQ Set EMQ-pharm-2

For each described adverse reaction or interaction below, select the ONE causative drug from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Metronidazole

B. Miconazole oral gel

C. Codeine

D. Amoxicillin

E. Clindamycin

F. Ibuprofen

G. Articaine

H. Prilocaine

  1. The correct answer is A (Metronidazole): metronidazole produces a disulfiram-like reaction of flushing, nausea, vomiting, tachycardia, and headache when combined with alcohol, so patients must avoid alcohol during treatment and for 48 hours afterwards. This is a well-known and clinically important interaction distinct from metronidazole's separate warfarin-potentiating effect. It is one of the most recurrently tested exam points in dental pharmacology.
  2. The correct answer is B (Miconazole oral gel): miconazole oral gel produces a marked rise in INR through inhibition of hepatic CYP2C9, and sufficient systemic absorption occurs from topical application in the mouth to cause this clinically significant interaction with warfarin. This is one of the most reliably tested drug interactions in ORE Part 1 pharmacology. It is contraindicated in patients taking warfarin for this reason.
  3. The correct answer is C (Codeine): codeine is contraindicated in breastfeeding mothers because of case reports of fatal respiratory depression in ultra-rapid CYP2D6 metaboliser infants exposed through breast milk, since codeine is converted to active morphine by this hepatic enzyme. This same pharmacogenetic variability also affects analgesic response directly in adult patients. It is also specifically contraindicated in children under 12 years old.
  4. The correct answer is D (Amoxicillin): amoxicillin characteristically provokes a widespread maculopapular rash in patients with unrecognised glandular fever, or infectious mononucleosis, so it should be avoided or used with great caution whenever this diagnosis is suspected. This reaction is a well-documented drug-disease interaction rather than a true penicillin allergy. An alternative antibiotic should be considered in this clinical scenario.
  5. The correct answer is E (Clindamycin): clindamycin has a strong association with Clostridioides difficile-associated diarrhoea and pseudomembranous colitis, reflecting its disruption of normal gut flora, and current UK guidance reserves it as a genuine penicillin-allergy alternative rather than a routine first-line antibiotic. Patients should be warned to stop the drug and seek attention if severe diarrhoea develops. This restricted role reflects the seriousness of this specific adverse effect.
EMQ Set EMQ-pharm-3

For each medical emergency presentation below, select the ONE correct emergency drug and dose from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Adrenaline 1:1000, 500 micrograms IM

B. Glucagon 1 mg IM

C. Hydrocortisone 100 mg IM

D. Buccal midazolam

E. Oral glucose

F. No drug treatment required

G. Flumazenil IV

H. Adrenaline 1:1000, 1 mg IM

  1. The correct answer is E (Oral glucose): a conscious diabetic patient with hypoglycaemia who remains able to swallow safely should be given oral glucose, either as a sugary drink, glucose tablets, or a glucose gel placed in the buccal sulcus. Glucagon by intramuscular injection is reserved for patients who are unconscious or unable to safely swallow. Emergency ambulance transfer should be arranged if the patient does not respond promptly.
  2. The correct answer is C (Hydrocortisone 100 mg IM): a patient with Addison's disease becoming hypotensive and confused under stress requires hydrocortisone 100 mg by intramuscular injection, a standard drug in the UK dental emergency kit stocked specifically for adrenal crisis. This addresses the underlying inability to mount an adequate cortisol response. Supportive measures and urgent hospital transfer should accompany this treatment.
  3. The correct answer is A (Adrenaline 1:1000, 500 micrograms IM): facial urticaria, wheeze, and hypotension after an antibiotic injection indicate anaphylaxis, requiring immediate adrenaline 1:1000, 500 micrograms, by intramuscular injection into the anterolateral thigh, repeated after 5 minutes if needed. Adrenaline should never be withheld while awaiting other interventions in this life-threatening presentation. This is the single most tested drug emergency fact in dental pharmacology.
  4. The correct answer is F (No drug treatment required): a brief loss of consciousness at the sight of an injection with rapid recovery once laid flat with legs raised is characteristic of vasovagal syncope, which requires no drug treatment. Management is positional, restoring venous return and cerebral perfusion. It is important to distinguish this benign presentation from more serious causes of collapse requiring specific pharmacological management.
  5. The correct answer is D (Buccal midazolam): a seizure continuing beyond 5 minutes during dental treatment is managed with buccal midazolam as the first-line emergency drug in UK dental practice, given into the buccal mucosa. It does not require intravenous access, making it practical for the dental setting. An emergency ambulance should be called if the seizure does not resolve.
EMQ Set EMQ-pharm-4

For each patient factor below, select the ONE correct prescribing precaution from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Avoid articaine for an inferior alveolar nerve block

B. Avoid NSAIDs in the third trimester

C. Avoid felypressin as a general precaution

D. Reduce the paracetamol dose

E. Avoid amoxicillin

F. Avoid clindamycin as routine first-line

G. Check the INR before invasive treatment

H. Use miconazole with extreme caution

  1. The correct answer is B (Avoid NSAIDs in the third trimester): NSAIDs are avoided in the third trimester of pregnancy because of the risk of premature closure of the fetal ductus arteriosus, a serious fetal cardiovascular complication. Paracetamol remains the appropriate analgesic choice at this stage instead. This is a well-established and recurrently tested prescribing precaution.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reduce the paracetamol dose): paracetamol dose should be reduced in patients under 50 kg and used cautiously in those with hepatic impairment or chronic alcohol excess, given the risk of hepatotoxicity from cumulative dosing. This precaution reflects paracetamol's known dose-dependent liver toxicity. It applies regardless of which other analgesic is co-prescribed.
  3. The correct answer is G (Check the INR before invasive treatment): a patient on warfarin requiring an invasive dental extraction should have their INR checked, generally within 24 hours before the procedure, with treatment proceeding if the INR is below 4.0 and local haemostatic measures are used. This ensures anticoagulation is at a safe level for the planned procedure. Stopping warfarin unilaterally is not the recommended approach.
  4. The correct answer is C (Avoid felypressin as a general precaution): current UK guidance is to avoid felypressin-containing local anaesthetic in pregnancy as a general precaution because of its oxytocic effect on uterine smooth muscle, preferring lidocaine with adrenaline instead. This is a precautionary avoidance rather than a dose-threshold distinction, since the oxytocic risk is theoretical rather than dose-defined in current guidance.
  5. The correct answer is E (Avoid amoxicillin): a documented penicillin allergy means amoxicillin must never be prescribed, and an appropriate non-penicillin alternative such as metronidazole or, in more severe cases, clindamycin should be used instead. This precaution applies regardless of the severity or type of dental infection present. Failing to check for allergy history before prescribing risks a serious anaphylactic reaction.
  6. The correct answer is A (It preferentially reacts with tin, suppressing formation of the weak gamma-2 tin-mercury phase): in high-copper amalgam, the copper preferentially reacts with tin during trituration, consuming it before the unstable, corrosion-prone gamma-2 phase can form. This gives high-copper amalgam superior compressive strength, reduced creep and better long-term marginal integrity than older low-copper formulations. It does not increase mercury requirements, reduce strength or slow setting for extended carving.
  7. The correct answer is C (The tin-mercury gamma-2 phase, which is weak and corrosion-prone): in low-copper amalgams, silver and tin react with mercury to form gamma-1 and gamma-2 phases, and the gamma-2 phase is mechanically weak and prone to corrosion, driving marginal breakdown over time. High-copper amalgams suppress this phase by consuming tin with copper instead. The gamma and eta phases described in the other options are not the recognised cause of this failure pattern.
  8. The correct answer is A (Children under 15 years old and pregnant or breastfeeding women): since July 2018, UK regulation implementing the Minamata Convention prohibits amalgam use for deciduous teeth and for children under 15 and pregnant or breastfeeding women, except where strictly necessary. This restriction does not extend to all adult NHS patients, allergy patients specifically, or edentulous patients, none of whom are named in the regulation. The rule reflects environmental and precautionary mercury-exposure concerns in these specific groups.
  9. The correct answer is D (Bulk mercury for amalgam preparation was banned, requiring pre-capsulated products only): since 2018, bulk mercury for amalgam preparation has been banned in the UK, so only pre-capsulated, pre-dosed amalgam capsules may be used, reducing the risk of mercury spillage and operator exposure. There is no mandated minimum amalgamator run time, separators remain mandatory regardless of practice size, and rubber dam is not a universal legal requirement for every amalgam placement.
  10. The correct answer is B (An amalgam separator installed in the practice's suction and drainage system): amalgam separators are mandatory in any practice handling amalgam or its waste, to prevent mercury from entering the wastewater system, with amalgam waste then disposed of as hazardous waste through a registered carrier. An ultrasonic bath, a lead-lined cabinet and a mercury vapour monitor are not what UK regulation specifically mandates to protect the wastewater system from amalgam mercury.
  11. The correct answer is A (Dental amalgam, since it tolerates a degree of moisture contamination well): amalgam is indicated for large posterior restorations under heavy occlusal load and is more tolerant of a degree of moisture contamination than composite, which requires reliable isolation to bond successfully. Composite would be compromised by poor moisture control, feldspathic porcelain is a laboratory-fabricated ceramic unsuitable for direct chairside placement, and zinc oxide eugenol lacks the strength for a definitive load-bearing restoration.
  12. The correct answer is C (Bis-GMA, UDMA or TEGDMA, forming the base resin matrix): composite resin's organic resin matrix is typically based on Bis-GMA, UDMA or TEGDMA monomers, which are reinforced with inorganic filler particles bonded via a silane coupling agent. Zinc oxide and polyacrylic acid describe polycarboxylate cement, fluoroaluminosilicate glass with polyacrylic acid describes glass ionomer cement, and calcium sulphate hemihydrate reacting with water describes gypsum products.
  13. The correct answer is D (Nanohybrid or hybrid composite, blending fine and larger filler particles): nanohybrid and hybrid composites combine fine and larger filler particles to give excellent aesthetic and mechanical performance for both anterior and posterior restorations, having largely superseded older macrofilled composites. An unfilled resin lacks the strength filler provides, and flowable composite deliberately reduces filler content for handling rather than combining particle sizes for optimal performance.
  14. The correct answer is B (Blue light around 450 to 480 nanometres activating camphorquinone): light-cured composite resin is most commonly initiated by blue light in the 450 to 480 nanometre range, which activates camphorquinone, a photoinitiator within the resin. Ultraviolet, infrared and green light with the other listed initiators are not the standard mechanism used for composite polymerisation in clinical practice.
  15. The correct answer is A (Around 2 to 3 per cent by volume during polymerisation): as composite monomers form covalent bonds during curing, the material contracts by approximately 2 to 3 per cent by volume, generating contraction stress at the tooth-restoration interface. The much larger and much smaller percentages in the other options do not reflect the documented magnitude of composite polymerisation shrinkage.
  16. The correct answer is C (Placing and curing composite in increments no thicker than about 2 millimetres): incremental placement and curing in layers no thicker than about 2 millimetres is the standard technique to reduce the effective configuration factor and manage polymerisation shrinkage stress. Bulk-filling in one increment or using thicker layers increases shrinkage stress, and leaving deeper layers uncured would produce an incompletely polymerised, unreliable restoration.
  17. The correct answer is D (Approximately 35 to 37 per cent phosphoric acid): etch-and-rinse (total-etch) systems typically use phosphoric acid at 35 to 37 per cent concentration to etch both enamel and dentine, removing the smear layer and demineralising the surface for micro-retentive bonding. The lower and higher percentage ranges in the other options do not reflect the standard clinical concentration used for this technique.
  18. The correct answer is A (Selective enamel etching combined with a self-etch adhesive system): selective enamel etching combined with a self-etch system reduces technique sensitivity and the risk of over-etching dentine while still achieving reliable enamel bond strength, making it a popular contemporary approach. Total-etch without a primer, self-etching only enamel while leaving dentine untreated, and substituting eugenol for phosphoric acid are not recognised or effective bonding protocols.
  19. The correct answer is B (An acid-base reaction between fluoroaluminosilicate glass and polyacrylic acid): conventional glass ionomer cement sets through an acid-base reaction in which polyacrylic acid attacks a fluoroaluminosilicate glass powder, releasing fluoride, aluminium and calcium ions that crosslink the polyacrylate chains. Free-radical addition polymerisation describes composite resin, the exothermic acid reaction describes zinc phosphate cement, and cationic ring-opening polymerisation describes polyether.
  20.
