The correct answer is C (Its ability to act as a fluoride reservoir, recharging with fluoride uptake): glass ionomer releases fluoride over an extended period and can be recharged by fluoride uptake from toothpaste or topical applications, acting as a fluoride reservoir for the surrounding tooth structure. Radiopacity, calcium chelation adhesion and the RMGIC snap-set mechanism are real properties of glass ionomer materials but do not explain this specific fluoride uptake and re-release behaviour.
  21. The correct answer is D (An initial light-cured snap set giving immediate working strength): the resin component of RMGIC allows an initial light-cured snap set for immediate working strength, before the slower acid-base maturation reaction continues. RMGIC does not eliminate fluoride release, does not require 24 hours before any loading, and is generally less brittle and more moisture-tolerant during setting than conventional glass ionomer cement.
  22. The correct answer is A (Glass ionomer cement or resin-modified glass ionomer cement): the combination of fluoride release, chemical adhesion to tooth structure and reasonable aesthetics makes glass ionomer or resin-modified glass ionomer the classic material choice for a high-caries-risk patient wanting a tooth-coloured posterior restoration, despite inferior wear resistance to composite. Zinc phosphate cement, nickel-chromium alloy and feldspathic porcelain do not provide the fluoride release and adhesion this scenario prioritises.
  23. The correct answer is A (Its initially low pH from the highly exothermic setting reaction): zinc phosphate cement's reaction between zinc oxide powder and phosphoric acid liquid is highly exothermic and initially produces a low pH, making it irritant to the pulp if placed directly on dentine without a protective liner. It has no adhesive bond to tooth structure, does not release fluoride and contains no eugenol, so the other options do not describe its actual chemistry.
  24. The correct answer is C (Residual eugenol can inhibit composite polymerisation and weaken the resin bond): eugenol from zinc oxide eugenol cement can interfere with composite resin's polymerisation and plasticise and weaken resin-based materials, so residual eugenol at the interface is a genuine concern before bonding a composite restoration. Zinc oxide eugenol does not release fluoride, its radiopacity is not the relevant clinical issue here, and its chemical bond is not strong enough to make removal risk pulp exposure.
  25. The correct answer is D (It chemically adheres to tooth structure and is relatively kind to the pulp): polycarboxylate cement chemically adheres to tooth structure through calcium chelation and is relatively kind to the pulp, unlike zinc phosphate cement, which relies purely on mechanical interlocking and is more irritant. It does not contain fluoroaluminosilicate glass, so its fluoride release is not greater than resin-modified glass ionomer's, and it does adhere chemically rather than having none at all.
  26. The correct answer is A (Resin cement, providing the highest bond strength of the luting cements): resin cements provide the highest bond strength of any luting cement and are the cement of choice for bonding all-ceramic restorations such as lithium disilicate, where the resin bond also contributes to fracture resistance. Zinc phosphate cement relies purely on mechanical interlocking, zinc oxide eugenol is chosen for its sedative effect rather than bond strength, and conventional glass ionomer is chosen mainly for fluoride release rather than maximum adhesive strength.
  27. The correct answer is B (Trisodium phosphate, which acts as a retarder in the reaction): alginate sets through a reaction between potassium or sodium alginate and calcium sulphate, catalysed by trisodium phosphate, which acts specifically as a retarder to control the working time. Platinum salts catalyse PVS setting, camphorquinone is activated by curing light in composite, and polyacrylic acid chelates calcium in glass ionomer and polycarboxylate cements, none of which control alginate's working time.
  28. The correct answer is C (Shrinkage from syneresis, caused by water loss on exposure to air): alginate undergoes syneresis, meaning water loss and resulting shrinkage, when left exposed to air, which is why it must be cast promptly, ideally within 10 to 15 minutes. Expansion from continued setting, no measurable change, and swelling from imbibition all describe different or incorrect behaviours, since imbibition specifically results from water immersion rather than air exposure.
  29. The correct answer is D (Sulphur in the latex gloves inhibited the platinum catalyst): addition silicone (PVS) is sensitive to contamination by latex gloves or sulphur-containing materials, which can inhibit the platinum catalyst and cause incomplete setting, exactly as described in this scenario. PVS contains no alginate or eugenol component, and doubling a retarder concentration is not a documented cause of incomplete PVS set from latex glove contact.
  30. The correct answer is A (Excellent detail reproduction is maintained despite moisture or saliva present): polyether is intrinsically hydrophilic, giving excellent detail reproduction even with some moisture or saliva present in the impression field. It does not become more flexible with moisture, its working time is relatively short rather than extended, and it does not form a permanent chemical bond to tooth structure.
  31. The correct answer is A (Polyether, because its inherent hydrophilicity aids detail reproduction in moisture): when a field cannot be perfectly isolated and remains slightly moist, polyether's inherent hydrophilicity gives it an advantage for accurate detail reproduction, whereas addition silicone is naturally hydrophobic despite modern surfactant modifications. Addition silicone is generally preferred instead where multiple pours or delayed pouring are anticipated, not immediate pouring, and neither material specifically tolerates sulphur contamination well.
  32. The correct answer is C (Type IV or V die stone, needing less water for a dense mix): Type IV and V die stones need less water for a workable mix, producing denser, stronger, more abrasion-resistant casts suitable for die work in crown and bridge fabrication. Type I and II plaster suit impression registration and simple study models respectively, and Type III dental stone is used more broadly than solely for articulator mounting, but none matches the high-strength die requirement described here.
  33. The correct answer is D (Feldspathic porcelain, chosen for its outstanding translucency and aesthetics): feldspathic porcelain offers outstanding translucency and aesthetics, making it valuable for veneers where maximum translucency and natural appearance are the overriding priority in a low-load restoration. Zirconia is chosen instead for high-strength, higher-load situations, cobalt-chromium is a framework alloy rather than an aesthetic ceramic, and lithium disilicate prioritises strength over feldspathic porcelain's superior translucency.
  34. The correct answer is A (Lithium disilicate, with a flexural strength of roughly 350 to 400 megapascals): lithium disilicate is a glass-ceramic reinforced with needle-shaped crystals, giving a flexural strength of roughly 350 to 400 megapascals while retaining good translucency, matching the description exactly. Zirconia exceeds 1000 megapascals but is a polycrystalline ceramic rather than a glass-ceramic with needle-shaped crystals, feldspathic porcelain is weaker still, and cobalt-chromium is a metal alloy, not a ceramic.
  35. The correct answer is B (Airborne particle abrasion combined with a specific zirconia primer): because zirconia cannot be etched with hydrofluoric acid, airborne particle abrasion combined with a specific zirconia primer is required to achieve a durable resin cement bond. Prolonged phosphoric acid etching with silane is the glass-ceramic protocol, eugenol immersion would not roughen a crystalline ceramic surface, and trisodium phosphate has no role in ceramic surface conditioning.
  36. The correct answer is C (Glass ionomer cement or resin-modified glass ionomer cement, for fluoride release): the pattern of extensive existing restorations across all four posterior sextants indicates high caries susceptibility, making fluoride release and chemical adhesion to tooth structure clinical priorities for the new lesion on tooth 24. Zinc phosphate cement, feldspathic porcelain and nickel-chromium alloy do not provide the fluoride release this high-caries-risk scenario calls for.
  37. The correct answer is B (Dental amalgam, since it tolerates moisture contamination better than composite): with isolation unreliable and heavy occlusal load anticipated on tooth 37, amalgam's tolerance of a degree of moisture contamination makes it the more appropriate choice than composite, which depends on a dry field for bonding. Resin cement is a luting agent rather than a direct restorative, and polyether is an impression material with no restorative role here.
  38. The correct answer is A (Feldspathic porcelain, for its outstanding translucency in a low-load veneer): with maximum translucency as the overriding priority for a minimally loaded anterior veneer on tooth 21, feldspathic porcelain's outstanding translucency and aesthetics make it the most appropriate ceramic. Zirconia's strength advantage is unnecessary here and comes at the cost of translucency, cobalt-chromium is a framework alloy, and zinc phosphate cement is a luting agent, not a veneer material.
  39. The correct answer is D (Cobalt-chromium alloy, chosen for its rigidity and strength-to-weight ratio): cobalt-chromium is the standard base metal alloy for removable partial denture frameworks, since its rigidity allows thin, well-tolerated connectors and clasps while providing adequate strength for the bilateral free-end saddle shown. High-noble gold alloy is used mainly for fixed restorations, feldspathic porcelain is a ceramic unsuited to a metal framework, and zinc oxide eugenol has no structural role in a denture framework.
  40. The correct answer is C (Resin cement, chosen for the highest bond strength of any luting cement): resin cement provides the highest bond strength of any luting cement and is the cement of choice for bonding lithium disilicate, where the adhesive bond also contributes to the crown's fracture resistance. Zinc oxide eugenol is chosen for its sedative effect on temporary work, polycarboxylate cement adheres via calcium chelation but with lower bond strength, and conventional glass ionomer is chosen mainly for fluoride release rather than maximum bond strength.
EMQ Set EMQ-materials-1

For each scenario below, select the ONE most appropriate material from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Dental amalgam

B. Composite resin

C. Conventional glass ionomer cement

D. Resin-modified glass ionomer cement

E. Zinc oxide eugenol cement

F. Zinc phosphate cement

G. Resin cement

H. Addition silicone (PVS)

I. Alginate

J. Polyether

  1. The correct answer is H (Addition silicone (PVS)): PVS produces no by-product during its addition polymerisation setting reaction, giving it excellent long-term dimensional stability so it can be poured multiple times over several days without significant distortion, making it the material of choice for this scenario. Alginate would distort from syneresis or imbibition over that timeframe, and polyether, while stable, is not specifically noted for tolerating multiple delayed pours as well as PVS.
  2. The correct answer is E (Zinc oxide eugenol cement): ZOE is useful for temporary cementation of crowns and bridges where later retrievability is required, since its bond is deliberately weaker than a permanent luting cement, allowing the temporary crown to be removed without damage. Resin cement and zinc phosphate cement are intended for definitive, harder-to-remove cementation, and composite resin is not used as a luting cement in this way.
  3. The correct answer is C (Conventional glass ionomer cement): conventional GIC sets by a self-contained acid-base reaction requiring no curing light, releases fluoride, and chemically bonds to tooth structure, making it the material of choice for the atraumatic restorative treatment technique in high-caries-risk paediatric patients. Resin-modified glass ionomer requires a curing light for its snap set, which is unavailable in this scenario, and composite resin also requires light-curing and provides no fluoride release.
  4. The correct answer is G (Resin cement): resin cement provides the highest bond strength of any luting cement and is the cement of choice for bonding lithium disilicate, where the resin bond also contributes to the restoration's fracture resistance, matching the scenario exactly. Zinc phosphate and polycarboxylate cements provide weaker mechanical or chemical bonds without contributing meaningfully to fracture resistance.
  5. The correct answer is I (Alginate): alginate is inexpensive, hydrophilic, easy to use and well tolerated, making it the material of choice for study models, orthodontic records and primary denture impressions, exactly matching this low-cost, easy-to-use scenario. PVS and polyether are reserved for definitive impressions requiring superior dimensional stability, which is unnecessary and more costly for study models.
EMQ Set EMQ-materials-2

For each material property or setting reaction described below, select the ONE material it describes from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Dental amalgam

B. Composite resin

C. Glass ionomer cement

D. Zinc phosphate cement

E. Zinc oxide eugenol cement

F. Addition silicone (PVS)

G. Alginate

H. Polyether

I. Zirconia

J. Lithium disilicate

  1. The correct answer is C (Glass ionomer cement): glass ionomer cement sets by an acid-base reaction between fluoroaluminosilicate glass and polyacrylic acid liquid, releasing fluoride, aluminium and calcium ions as the glass surface is attacked, matching the description exactly. Zinc phosphate cement's reaction does not release fluoride, and the other listed materials set by entirely different mechanisms.
  2. The correct answer is D (Zinc phosphate cement): zinc phosphate cement is formed from a highly exothermic reaction between zinc oxide powder and phosphoric acid liquid, and its initially low pH means a protective liner is required if placed directly on dentine, matching the description exactly. Glass ionomer cement's acid-base reaction is comparatively milder and it chemically bonds to dentine, unlike zinc phosphate.
  3. The correct answer is F (Addition silicone (PVS)): PVS sets by an addition polymerisation reaction between a vinyl-terminated and a hydride-functional polysiloxane, catalysed by platinum salts, producing no by-product, matching the description precisely. Polyether sets by a different cationic ring-opening mechanism, and alginate sets by an acid-base reaction with calcium sulphate, not addition polymerisation.
  4. The correct answer is I (Zirconia): zirconia's excellent fracture toughness derives partly from a stress-induced phase transformation mechanism within its polycrystalline structure, distinguishing it from glass-ceramics like lithium disilicate, which rely on crystal reinforcement within a glassy matrix rather than a phase transformation. Dental amalgam and composite resin have no comparable ceramic phase-transformation toughening mechanism.
  5. The correct answer is A (Dental amalgam): amalgam sets through trituration of mercury with a silver-tin-copper alloy powder, and elevated copper content in high-copper formulations suppresses the unstable, corrosion-prone gamma-2 phase, matching the description exactly. None of the other materials listed involve a mercury-alloy trituration reaction.
EMQ Set EMQ-materials-3

For each material failure or complication described below, select the ONE most likely cause from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Marginal leakage from polymerisation shrinkage

B. Incomplete set from latex glove contamination

C. Desiccation cracking from early moisture exposure

D. Corrosion and marginal breakdown from the gamma-2 phase

E. Pulpal irritation from direct placement without a protective liner

F. Inhibited polymerisation from residual eugenol contamination

G. Distortion from imbibition after storage in water

H. Nickel hypersensitivity reaction

I. Bond failure from acid-etching a polycrystalline ceramic

J. Reduced fluoride release from incorporating a resin component

  1. The correct answer is F (Inhibited polymerisation from residual eugenol contamination): eugenol from a prior zinc oxide eugenol dressing can interfere with composite resin polymerisation and plasticise the resin, explaining why the base-composite interface fails to fully cure in this scenario. Marginal leakage from shrinkage and pulpal irritation from a missing liner describe different failure mechanisms unrelated to eugenol contamination.
  2. The correct answer is B (Incomplete set from latex glove contamination): sulphur in latex gloves can inhibit the platinum catalyst in addition silicone, causing an incomplete or tacky set exactly as described. Desiccation cracking and imbibition distortion both relate to hydrocolloid materials such as alginate rather than to PVS catalyst inhibition.
  3. The correct answer is D (Corrosion and marginal breakdown from the gamma-2 phase): older low-copper amalgams contain the weak, corrosion-prone gamma-2 tin-mercury phase, which causes exactly this pattern of marginal breakdown and corrosion after years of clinical service. High-copper amalgams suppress this phase, so this failure pattern specifically implicates an older low-copper formulation rather than any other listed cause.
  4. The correct answer is I (Bond failure from acid-etching a polycrystalline ceramic): zirconia cannot be etched with hydrofluoric acid like a glass-ceramic, so attempting to do so fails to create a retentive surface and results in early debonding, matching the scenario precisely. Zirconia instead requires airborne particle abrasion and a specific primer, which was not used here.
  5. The correct answer is H (Nickel hypersensitivity reaction): nickel-chromium alloys carry a recognised risk of nickel hypersensitivity, which can present as contact dermatitis in susceptible patients wearing a nickel-chromium denture clasp. This is a distinct biocompatibility issue from the corrosion, contamination and bonding failures described in the other options, none of which involve an allergic mechanism.
  6. The correct answer is A (Class I, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class I cavities occur in pits and fissures of occlusal surfaces of premolars and molars, exactly as described here. Class II would require proximal extension, Class V occurs on the cervical third away from a pit or fissure, and Class VI describes wear on cusp tips or incisal edges.
  7. The correct answer is B (Class II, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class II cavities occur on proximal surfaces of premolars and molars and typically extend onto the occlusal surface, matching this disto-occlusal pattern exactly. Class I is confined to pits and fissures, Class III applies to incisors and canines, and Class IV requires incisal edge involvement.
  8. The correct answer is C (Class III, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class III cavities occur on the proximal surfaces of incisors and canines that do not involve the incisal edge, which fits this scenario precisely. Class IV is excluded because the incisal edge is intact, Class II applies to premolars and molars, and Class V is a cervical lesion.
  9. The correct answer is D (Class IV, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class IV cavities occur on proximal surfaces of incisors and canines that involve the incisal edge, usually resulting from trauma or extensive caries, which matches this fracture exactly. Class III is excluded specifically because the incisal edge is involved here, unlike a simple proximal lesion.
  10. The correct answer is B (Class V, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class V cavities occur on the cervical third of the buccal or lingual surface of any tooth, away from a pit or fissure, and are frequently caused by erosion or abrasion rather than caries alone, which fits this erosive presentation. Class I requires a pit-and-fissure location.
  11. The correct answer is D (Class VI, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class VI, added later and not part of Black's original system, describes cavities or wear affecting incisal edges or cusp tips, matching this attrition pattern. Class V is a cervical lesion and Class IV requires a proximal cavity extending to the incisal edge, neither of which applies here.
  12. The correct answer is A (Mesio-occluso-distal, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this describes a Class II cavity that involves both proximal surfaces plus the occlusal surface, as seen when caries has spread across the tooth from mesial to distal. A disto-occlusal or mesio-occlusal cavity involves only one proximal surface, not both.
  13. The correct answer is C (Toothbrush abrasion or acid erosion, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class V lesions are frequently caused by abrasion or erosion rather than caries alone, because the cervical buccal surface is exposed to brushing forces and dietary or gastric acid. Attrition and pit-and-fissure caries do not typically produce cervical lesions.
  14. The correct answer is D (Class I, as indicated by the clinical findings described): Class I cavities include lingual pits of maxillary incisors as well as pits and fissures of posterior occlusal surfaces, so this lingual pit lesion is correctly classified as Class I. Class III requires a proximal surface lesion, which is not present here.
  15. The correct answer is A (Monitor with preventive advice and repeat radiographs, as indicated by the clinical findings described): a lesion confined to outer enamel represents an early, non-cavitated stage that can arrest or remineralise with fluoride and dietary advice, so operative intervention would cause unnecessary loss of sound tooth structure without improving outcomes.
  16. The correct answer is B (ICDAS 1 or 2, as indicated by the clinical findings described): these scores represent early enamel changes visible only after air drying or with the naked eye, without any cavitation, matching this chalky white spot lesion. ICDAS 5 to 6 would require frank cavitation with dentine exposure, which is absent here.
  17. The correct answer is C (Selective caries removal, leaving affected dentine over the pulp): this approach deliberately leaves a layer of affected but not infected dentine to avoid pulp exposure, and is sealed with a well-adapted restoration, reducing exposure risk without increasing failure when the marginal seal is good.
  18. The correct answer is C (Enamel demineralisation can remineralise without operative treatment, as indicated by the clinical findings described): the caries process is dynamic, with cycles of demineralisation and remineralisation depending on diet, plaque control, and fluoride exposure, so a non-cavitated enamel lesion can be arrested rather than requiring a restoration.
  19. The correct answer is A (A cavitated dentine lesion cannot be kept clean and progresses rapidly): once cavitation has formed, self-cleansing is impossible and dentine caries progresses more rapidly than enamel caries, so operative treatment becomes necessary rather than monitoring.
  20. The correct answer is D (Metabolising fermentable carbohydrates to produce demineralising acid, as indicated by the clinical findings described): cariogenic bacteria ferment dietary sugars within the biofilm, generating acid that lowers local pH and drives demineralisation of enamel and dentine, which is the central mechanism of the caries process.
  21. The correct answer is B (No operative intervention; continue monitoring, as indicated by the clinical findings described): an arrested lesion that has remained stable over time reflects successful remineralisation and plaque control, so restoring it would remove sound tooth structure unnecessarily when the disease process has already been controlled.
  22. The correct answer is A (Localised enamel breakdown with underlying dentinal shadowing, as indicated by the clinical findings described): ICDAS 3 to 4 represents this intermediate stage, more advanced than the early changes of scores 1 to 2 but not yet the frank cavitation with visible dentine seen at scores 5 to 6.
  23. The correct answer is D (Reduced pulp exposure risk without increasing restoration failure): trials have shown that selective caries removal, provided the marginal seal is good, lowers the chance of exposing the pulp during excavation while restoration success remains comparable to complete removal.
  24. The correct answer is B (It prevents saliva and blood contamination that compromises the bond): even small amounts of moisture contamination weaken the bond between composite and tooth structure, leading to microleakage, postoperative sensitivity, and premature restoration failure, which rubber dam isolation reliably prevents.
  25. The correct answer is C (Incremental layering in increments of no more than 2 millimetres): layering reduces the effective configuration factor at each increment, minimising marginal gap formation, cuspal flexure, and postoperative sensitivity that would otherwise result from curing a large bulk of composite at once.
  26. The correct answer is B (Shrinkage stress pulls the material away from the cavity walls): composite shrinks during polymerisation, and curing too large a bulk generates shrinkage stress that exceeds the bond strength at the cavity margins, risking marginal gap formation and postoperative sensitivity.
  27. The correct answer is C (Around 15 to 30 seconds, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this etching time on enamel margins with 35 to 37 per cent phosphoric acid produces adequate micromechanical retention for bonding, following the sequence of preparation, etching, bonding agent application, and incremental placement and curing.
  28. The correct answer is A (To re-establish a tight, anatomical proximal contact, as indicated by the clinical findings described): matrix systems for Class II composites are used to recreate a proper contact point and contour, preventing food packing and secondary caries that would otherwise result from an open or poorly contoured contact.
  29. The correct answer is D (Children under 15 and pregnant or breastfeeding women): the Minamata Convention's restrictions on dental amalgam particularly limit its use in these groups, contributing to composite resin becoming the material of choice for most direct restorations in UK practice.
  30. The correct answer is D (The ratio of bonded to unbonded surfaces in a cavity): a high configuration factor increases shrinkage stress during polymerisation because there is less unbonded surface to relieve that stress, which is why incremental placement is used to manage it.
  31. The correct answer is B (Moisture contamination and excessive polymerisation shrinkage stress, as indicated by the clinical findings described): omitting rubber dam risks bond-compromising contamination, and curing a large bulk in one increment generates excessive shrinkage stress, both of which are recognised causes of postoperative sensitivity after composite placement.
  32. The correct answer is A (To refine occlusion and contour of the restoration): finishing and polishing follow incremental placement and curing in the standard bonding sequence, allowing the clinician to adjust the restoration's shape and occlusal contacts for comfort and longevity.
  33. The correct answer is C (An onlay covering the weakened cusp, as indicated by the clinical findings described): onlays extend to cover one or more cusps, protecting a cusp weakened by extensive caries or a previous large restoration, without requiring the full coronal coverage of a crown when the remaining tooth structure is otherwise sound.
  34. The correct answer is A (Cuspal coverage protects the cracked cusp from further propagation): cracked cusp syndrome is a recognised indication for an onlay or crown because covering the affected cusp splints it against the flexing forces that would otherwise extend the crack toward the pulp or root.
  35. The correct answer is C (Indirect restorations allow improved proximal contact and contour): a large multi-surface cavity often makes it difficult to achieve reliable proximal contact directly, whereas a laboratory-fabricated or CAD/CAM restoration can be shaped precisely outside the mouth before cementation.
  36. The correct answer is D (A full coverage crown, as indicated by the clinical findings described): full coverage is indicated following root canal treatment on a posterior tooth where the marginal ridge is compromised, because the loss of structural integrity substantially increases fracture risk under normal occlusal forces without cuspal protection.
  37. The correct answer is B (To provide adequate space for restoration thickness without pulpal exposure): occlusal or incisal reduction of 1.5 to 2 millimetres gives sufficient bulk for the restorative material's strength and aesthetics while avoiding over-reduction that would risk exposing the pulp.
  38. The correct answer is C (Reduced retention and resistance form of the final crown): axial walls should taper approximately 6 to 10 degrees, and over-tapering beyond this range reduces the surface area resisting displacing forces, compromising the crown's long-term retention on the prepared tooth.
  39. The correct answer is D (An inadequate ferrule predicts a significantly higher failure rate): teeth with an inadequate ferrule, ideally at least 1.5 to 2 millimetres of encircling sound tooth structure, show significantly higher failure rates regardless of post design or material used.
  40. The correct answer is B (It favours an indirect restoration over a direct composite): cases where reliable isolation cannot be achieved are a recognised indication for indirect restorations, since composite bonding depends heavily on moisture control that a subgingival margin can make difficult to guarantee directly.
  41. The correct answer is A (Indirect pulp capping, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this technique is used when caries approaches but does not expose the pulp, leaving a layer of affected dentine in situ and placing a biocompatible liner to encourage tertiary dentine formation before the definitive restoration is placed.
  42. The correct answer is B (Direct pulp capping, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this is used for a small, mechanical or carious exposure in a tooth with no history of spontaneous pain and readily controlled haemorrhage, suggesting a healthy pulp capable of forming a dentine bridge beneath a calcium hydroxide or calcium silicate material.
  43. The correct answer is A (Pulpotomy, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this removes the coronal, and sometimes radicular, pulp while keeping the radicular pulp vital, typically in immature permanent teeth with an open apex, allowing continued apexogenesis so that root development and apical closure can continue.
  44. The correct answer is C (Calcium hydroxide or a calcium silicate cement, as indicated by the clinical findings described): these biocompatible liners, including mineral trioxide aggregate, encourage tertiary dentine formation over affected dentine left in situ, protecting the pulp before the definitive restoration is placed.
  45. The correct answer is D (The history suggests irreversible pulpitis, which predictably fails with pulp capping): spontaneous, lingering pain is characteristic of irreversible pulpitis, and case selection is critical since direct pulp capping of an exposure in this context is inappropriate and predictably fails.
  46. The correct answer is D (Continued physiological root development and apical closure, as indicated by the clinical findings described): apexogenesis describes the ongoing natural development of the root and closure of the apex, which pulpotomy aims to preserve by keeping the radicular pulp vital in an immature permanent tooth.
  47. The correct answer is C (Both encourage continued apexogenesis and root development, as indicated by the clinical findings described): calcium hydroxide and MTA-type materials are used in pulpotomy of immature permanent teeth specifically because they support ongoing apexogenesis, allowing root development and apical closure to continue after treatment.
  48. The correct answer is B (Reversible pulpitis, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this presents as sharp, short-lived pain provoked by a stimulus such as cold, resolving promptly once the stimulus is removed, and the pulp remains capable of healing once the underlying cause is treated.
  49. The correct answer is A (Irreversible pulpitis, as indicated by the clinical findings described): spontaneous, often severe pain that lingers after the stimulus is removed and worsens at night due to increased pulpal blood pressure is characteristic, and the pulp cannot recover, requiring root canal treatment or extraction.
  50. The correct answer is A (Pulp necrosis, as indicated by the clinical findings described): the tooth shows no response to vitality testing because the pulp is dead, and the tooth may be entirely asymptomatic or present later with percussion tenderness, a sinus tract, or periapical radiolucency as bacteria progress periapically.
  51. The correct answer is D (Pulpal nerve fibres provide poor localisation of pain): pain becomes better localised only once inflammation spreads to the periapical periodontal ligament, which has proprioceptive fibres, explaining why early pulpal pain is diffuse and hard for the patient to pinpoint.
  52. The correct answer is C (It measures electrical impedance difference between oral mucosa and periodontal tissue): this gives a precise reading of canal length, used alongside a periapical radiograph rather than radiography alone, since radiographs can distort with angulation and superimposition.
  53. The correct answer is B (The apical constriction, as indicated by the clinical findings described): working length determination establishes the length to which instrumentation and obturation should extend, ideally to this narrowest part of the canal, which lies close to but not exactly at the anatomical apex.
  54. The correct answer is C (Instrumentation alone cannot adequately clean irregular canal anatomy): sodium hypochlorite dissolves organic tissue and is a potent antimicrobial, reaching areas such as fins, isthmuses, and lateral canals that mechanical instruments cannot physically access.
  55. The correct answer is B (A hypochlorite accident from extrusion beyond the apex): sodium hypochlorite extruded into periapical tissue causes sudden severe pain, swelling, and ecchymosis, and extrusion beyond the apex must always be avoided during irrigation.
  56. The correct answer is D (Gutta-percha, as indicated by the clinical findings described): obturation, most commonly using gutta-percha with a sealer, fills the cleaned and shaped canal system three-dimensionally, sealing it against bacterial recontamination from either the coronal or apical route.
  57. The correct answer is A (Ledging, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this occurs when a false canal pathway is created, usually from failing to maintain patency in a curved canal, meaning the original canal path can no longer be negotiated to full working length.
  58. The correct answer is B (Perforation, commonly sealed with MTA, as indicated by the clinical findings described): perforation is an iatrogenic communication most often occurring at furcation areas or during post space preparation, with a guarded prognosis depending on size and location, and it is commonly sealed promptly with MTA.
  59. The correct answer is A (Leaving the fragment in situ with the patient informed and monitored): management of instrument separation depends on fragment position, and an apically lodged fragment beyond a curve may sometimes be left in situ if the canal is otherwise well cleaned and shaped.
  60. The correct answer is D (Cyclic fatigue and torsional stress generated by canal curvature): repeated flexing within a curved canal subjects nickel-titanium rotary instruments to cyclic fatigue, and binding can cause torsional stress, both of which increase the risk of fracture within the canal.
  61. The correct answer is C (To fill microscopic irregularities and improve the seal against bacteria): gutta-percha alone cannot adapt perfectly to every canal irregularity, so a sealer is used alongside it to achieve a three-dimensional seal against bacterial recontamination.
  62. The correct answer is D (Radiography confirms file position and canal anatomy that the locator cannot show): while apex locators give a precise electrical reading, a periapical radiograph provides visual confirmation of file position and canal anatomy, since radiography alone distorts with angulation and superimposition.
  63. The correct answer is A (Retreatment is indicated due to signs of endodontic failure): a new or persistent periapical radiolucency combined with ongoing symptoms in a previously root-filled tooth indicates failure of the original treatment, warranting retreatment rather than observation alone.
  64. The correct answer is B (An inadequate coronal seal allowing coronal microleakage, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this is now understood as one of the most significant predictors of failure, since bacteria can recontaminate a well-obturated canal system through a poorly sealed coronal restoration.
  65. The correct answer is C (Periradicular (apical) surgery, as indicated by the clinical findings described): The correct answer is periradicular (apical) surgery: when orthograde retreatment cannot resolve persistent periapical disease, or a cyst does not resolve with orthograde treatment, surgical access to the apex, removal of diseased tissue, and a retrograde root-end filling, commonly with MTA, may be considered.
  66. The correct answer is A (Absence of a preoperative periapical lesion, or only a small one): factors favouring a good prognosis include this along with a well-sealed coronal restoration placed promptly, thorough canal cleaning and shaping, and absence of complications such as perforation.
  67. The correct answer is C (MTA, as indicated by the clinical findings described): during periradicular surgery, following resection of the root apex and removal of diseased periapical tissue, a retrograde root-end filling is placed, commonly using mineral trioxide aggregate for its favourable sealing and biocompatibility properties.
  68. The correct answer is B (These conditions may not resolve with cleaning and shaping of the canal alone): extraradicular infection or a cyst lies outside the root canal system, so orthograde cleaning and obturation cannot always reach and resolve it, which is why surgery may become necessary.
  69. The correct answer is D (Retention of the core, not reinforcement of the root): posts do not strengthen a root canal treated tooth, and if poorly selected or placed can actually increase root fracture risk, since their role is purely to retain the core material.
  70. The correct answer is C (At least 4 to 5 millimetres, as indicated by the clinical findings described): preserving this amount of apical gutta-percha maintains the apical seal established during obturation, while still allowing adequate post space for retention of the core in the coronal portion of the canal.
  71. The correct answer is D (Their modulus of elasticity is closer to dentine): because fibre posts flex more similarly to dentine, under excessive load they are more likely to flex or debond rather than cause a catastrophic, unrestorable vertical root fracture.
  72. The correct answer is A (A metal post more often causes catastrophic vertical root fracture): this is a more common and more serious failure mode with rigid metal posts, compared with a fibre post, which is more likely to flex or debond under the same excessive load.
  73. The correct answer is B (Weakening of the root, increasing the risk of fracture): excessive removal of radicular dentine during post space preparation thins the remaining root walls, increasing the risk of root fracture, which is why conservative preparation is a key principle of post and core design.
  74. The correct answer is B (A post and core to retain the core material, then a crown): a post and core is indicated when a root-filled tooth has insufficient remaining coronal structure to retain a core and crown directly, and an adequate ferrule supports a good long-term prognosis for this approach.
  75. The correct answer is D (Replant immediately, handling the tooth only by the crown): immediate replantation at the scene is the first and best option if feasible, handling only the crown to avoid damaging remaining periodontal ligament cells, without scrubbing the root surface.
  76. The correct answer is C (Hank's balanced salt solution, as indicated by the clinical findings described): this cell culture medium best preserves periodontal ligament cell viability for several hours, ranking ahead of cold milk, the patient's own saliva, and saline, while tap water and dry storage are the most damaging options.
  77. The correct answer is A (Tap water is hypotonic and causes rapid lysis of PDL cells): because tap water is hypotonic relative to periodontal ligament cells, it causes rapid cell lysis, making it one of the most damaging storage options alongside dry storage.
  78. The correct answer is D (Replacement resorption, with the root gradually replaced by bone): if dry time exceeds approximately 60 minutes, most periodontal ligament cells will not survive, and replacement resorption is the expected outcome, although the tooth may still function for some years.
  79. The correct answer is B (Reassurance and monitoring; a primary tooth should never be replanted): replantation of a primary tooth risks damaging the developing permanent tooth germ beneath it, so the correct management is reassurance and monitoring of the developing successor rather than replantation.
  80. The correct answer is A (A flexible splint for around two weeks, as indicated by the clinical findings described): a flexible splint allows physiological movement and favours periodontal healing over a rigid splint, generally used for around two weeks for an uncomplicated avulsion with a closed apex, longer with an associated alveolar fracture.
  81. The correct answer is C (A satisfactory root filling length with a favourable healing prognosis): a well-condensed filling extending close to, but not beyond, the radiographic apices with no periapical radiolucency indicates the working length and obturation quality were appropriate, supporting a good outcome.
  82. The correct answer is A (An underfilled root canal, increasing the risk of persistent infection): a gap of several millimetres between the filling and the apex leaves unobturated canal space where bacteria can persist, increasing the risk of ongoing or future periapical disease.
  83. The correct answer is B (Assess for retreatment given new evidence of endodontic failure): a new periapical radiolucency developing around a previously well-obturated root filling, combined with new symptoms, indicates failure of the original treatment and warrants assessment for retreatment.
  84. The correct answer is D (Secondary (recurrent) caries at the restoration margin, as indicated by the clinical findings described): The correct answer is secondary (recurrent) caries at the restoration margin: a radiolucency directly beneath a restoration margin extending into dentine is characteristic of recurrent caries developing where the marginal seal has broken down, allowing bacterial ingress beneath the restoration.
  85. The correct answer is C (Monitor with preventive measures rather than restore immediately): a lesion confined to enamel on a bitewing is managed non-operatively with preventive measures and review, since operative treatment at this early, non-cavitated stage causes unnecessary loss of sound tooth structure.
  86. The correct answer is C (A furcal perforation during canal instrumentation, as indicated by the clinical findings described): a radiolucent track running from the filled canal toward the furcation, separate from the intended canal path, is characteristic of perforation, which most often occurs at furcation areas and requires prompt sealing, commonly with MTA.
  87. The correct answer is A (Ledging with loss of the original canal path): a false pathway created partway along the canal, with the true apical canal left unfilled beyond that point, indicates a ledge has formed, usually from failing to maintain patency in a curved canal.
  88. The correct answer is B (Leave the fragment in situ with the patient informed and monitored): management of instrument separation depends on fragment position, and an apically lodged fragment beyond a curve may reasonably be left in situ if the remaining canal is well cleaned and shaped.
  89. The correct answer is D (Overextension of the root filling with associated periapical inflammation): gutta-percha extending beyond the apex indicates the working length was established beyond the apical constriction, and the accompanying periapical radiolucency suggests an inflammatory response to the extruded material.
  90. The correct answer is B (Selective caries removal, leaving affected dentine over the pulp): for a deep lesion close to the pulp in a tooth without symptoms suggesting irreversible pulpitis, selective removal reduces pulp exposure risk while a well-sealed restoration arrests the remaining caries.
  91. The correct answer is D (Missed canals during the original root canal treatment): upper first molars typically have multiple canals in the mesiobuccal root as well as the distobuccal and palatal roots, so unfilled buccal canal spaces alongside a filled palatal canal suggest canals were missed.
  92. The correct answer is A (Replacement resorption (ankylosis), as indicated by the clinical findings described): The correct answer is replacement resorption (ankylosis): a root outline showing patchy replacement by adjacent bone rather than a clear periodontal ligament space is characteristic of replacement resorption, typically following prolonged extra-oral dry time before replantation.
  93. The correct answer is C (Continued root development with a narrowing apex over time): in an immature tooth with a short dry time and favourable storage, ongoing root development and apical narrowing on serial radiographs indicate the pulp has regained a blood supply, supporting deferral of root canal treatment.
  94. The correct answer is D (An adequate ferrule supporting a favourable long-term prognosis): 3 millimetres of sound coronal structure circumferentially exceeds the recommended minimum of 1.5 to 2 millimetres, providing a ferrule that resists lateral forces and supports a favourable outcome for the restored tooth.
  95. The correct answer is C (Expected early postoperative change, reviewed rather than treated further): a mildly widened periodontal ligament space shortly after obturation, without a discrete radiolucency, reflects transient postoperative inflammation rather than established chronic disease, and is appropriately monitored with review.
  96. The correct answer is A (Reversible pulpitis, as indicated by the clinical findings described): brief, stimulus-provoked pain that resolves quickly once the stimulus is removed is characteristic of reversible pulpitis, even where the carious lesion appears radiographically close to the pulp, provided there is no spontaneous or lingering pain.
  97. The correct answer is B (Successful healing with a favourable long-term prognosis, as indicated by the clinical findings described): an intact lamina dura with no periapical radiolucency at one-year review, combined with the absence of symptoms, indicates the root canal treatment has been successful.
  98. The correct answer is A (Recurrent caries beneath the restoration progressing to pulp necrosis): a marginal radiolucency beneath a longstanding restoration suggests bacterial ingress and recurrent caries, which over time can progress through the pulp to cause necrosis and periapical radiolucency at the root apex.
  99. The correct answer is B (A poorer prognosis, possibly from extraradicular infection or a cyst): a large periapical lesion that fails to resolve despite an apparently well-obturated canal over an extended review period suggests extraradicular infection or a cyst that orthograde treatment alone cannot resolve.
  100. The correct answer is C (Insufficient apical gutta-percha was preserved during post space preparation): generally at least 4 to 5 millimetres of apical gutta-percha should be preserved to maintain the apical seal, so leaving only 2 millimetres risks compromising the seal established during obturation.

EMQ Set EMQ-restorative-5

For each scenario below, select the ONE most appropriate option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Class I

B. Class II

C. Class III

D. Class IV

E. Class V

F. Class VI

G. Monitor with preventive measures, do not restore

  1. The correct answer is A (Class I): this option correctly matches the clinical scenario described, based on the recognised diagnostic or management criteria for this presentation as covered in the restorative and endodontic curriculum.
  2. The correct answer is B (Class II): this option correctly matches the clinical scenario described, based on the recognised diagnostic or management criteria for this presentation as covered in the restorative and endodontic curriculum.
  3. The correct answer is D (Class IV): this option correctly matches the clinical scenario described, based on the recognised diagnostic or management criteria for this presentation as covered in the restorative and endodontic curriculum.
  4. The correct answer is E (Class V): this option correctly matches the clinical scenario described, based on the recognised diagnostic or management criteria for this presentation as covered in the restorative and endodontic curriculum.
  5. The correct answer is G (Monitor with preventive measures, do not restore): this option correctly matches the clinical scenario described, based on the recognised diagnostic or management criteria for this presentation as covered in the restorative and endodontic curriculum.
  6. The correct answer is D (Gingivitis confined to the soft tissue): gingivitis produces erythema, oedema and bleeding on probing without any loss of periodontal attachment or alveolar bone. It is reversible with effective plaque control and professional cleaning alone. Periodontitis, by contrast, always shows clinical attachment loss and radiographic bone loss, neither of which is present here. Peri-implant mucositis cannot apply since no implant is described.
  7. The correct answer is A (Irreversible loss of attachment and alveolar bone): periodontitis is defined by clinical attachment loss and radiographic bone loss, which are irreversible and distinguish it from reversible gingival inflammation. Bleeding, erythema and plaque can all occur in simple gingivitis and do not by themselves indicate periodontitis. Because the damage is structural rather than purely inflammatory, periodontitis needs staging, grading and long-term maintenance rather than hygiene measures alone.
  8. The correct answer is A (Stage I): Stage I, initial periodontitis, is defined by interdental clinical attachment loss of 1 to 2 millimetres and radiographic bone loss confined to the coronal third of the root, with no tooth loss due to periodontitis. Stage II requires 3 to 4 millimetres of attachment loss while remaining in the coronal third. Stages III and IV both require attachment loss of 5 millimetres or more and bone loss extending beyond the coronal third.
  9. The correct answer is D (Stage II): Stage II is moderate periodontitis, defined by clinical attachment loss of 3 to 4 millimetres with bone loss still confined to the coronal third of the root. Stage I is limited to 1 to 2 millimetres of attachment loss. Stages III and IV both require 5 millimetres or more of attachment loss and bone loss extending into the middle or apical third.
  10. The correct answer is A (Stage III): Stage III is severe periodontitis with potential for additional tooth loss, showing attachment loss of 5 millimetres or more, bone loss extending to the middle or apical third, and up to four teeth lost to periodontitis, often with vertical defects or furcation involvement. Losing three teeth fits within the up-to-four-teeth threshold for Stage III rather than the five-or-more required for Stage IV. Stages I and II both require far less attachment and bone loss than described here.
  11. The correct answer is C (Stage IV): Stage IV is severe periodontitis with potential for loss of the dentition, sharing the attachment and bone loss features of Stage III but with five or more teeth lost to periodontitis and evidence of masticatory dysfunction, bite collapse, drifting or flaring requiring complex rehabilitation. Losing seven teeth exceeds the four-tooth ceiling that defines Stage III. Stages I and II involve far less attachment loss and no tooth loss due to periodontitis.
  12. The correct answer is C (Ratio of radiographic bone loss to the patient's age): grading uses the ratio of radiographic bone loss to age as the primary indicator of progression rate, which is then modified upward by risk factors such as smoking and diabetes. Pocket depth alone reflects current disease extent rather than historical rate of progression. Bleeding on probing and total tooth loss are relevant to stability and staging respectively, not to the grading calculation itself.
  13. The correct answer is A (Grade C, because heavy smoking independently justifies an upgrade): grade C is assigned whenever there is heavy smoking of ten or more cigarettes a day or poorly controlled diabetes, regardless of the calculated bone loss to age ratio. Grade A requires a ratio below 0.25 with no smoking or diabetes, which does not apply here. The ratio of 0.4 would normally indicate Grade B, but the modifying risk factor overrides this calculation immediately rather than after any observation period.
  14. The correct answer is B (Grade A): Grade A represents slow progression, defined by a bone loss to age ratio below 0.25 with no smoking and no diabetes, which matches this patient exactly. Grade B requires a ratio between 0.25 and 1.0, and Grade C requires a ratio above 1.0 or the presence of heavy smoking or poorly controlled diabetes. Sufficient data, the ratio plus smoking and diabetes status, are already available to assign a grade.
  15. The correct answer is C (Probing depths and bleeding on probing): the classification records whether a staged and graded case is currently stable, in remission, or unstable based on probing depths and bleeding on probing, allowing the same underlying diagnosis to be tracked through treatment. Age and total attachment loss inform staging and grading but not the stability descriptor itself. Smoking and diabetic control are grade modifiers rather than stability indicators.
  16. The correct answer is B (A WHO periodontal probe with a 0.5 millimetre ball tip and coloured band): the BPE uses this specific probe design, with the coloured band positioned between 3.5 and 5.5 millimetres, to rapidly screen each of the six sextants. A sharp explorer and a periotest device are not the instruments used for BPE screening. A Williams probe is used for detailed six-point charting rather than the rapid sextant-based BPE.
  17. The correct answer is A (Score 0): a BPE score of 0 indicates health, with no bleeding on probing, no calculus, and no pocketing over 3.5 millimetres, exactly as described. Score 1 requires bleeding on probing with no calculus. Score 2 additionally requires supra- or subgingival calculus or overhangs, and Score 3 requires the coloured band to be partially obscured by pocketing.
  18. The correct answer is D (Score 1): a BPE score of 1 shows bleeding on probing with no calculus or overhangs and no pocketing beyond 3.5 millimetres. Score 0 requires the complete absence of bleeding. Score 2 requires calculus or plaque-retentive overhangs in addition to bleeding, and Score 3 requires pocketing that partially obscures the coloured band.
  19. The correct answer is A (Score 2): a BPE score of 2 records bleeding plus supra- or subgingival calculus or plaque-retentive overhangs, with the coloured band still fully visible, confirming pockets remain under 3.5 millimetres. Score 1 requires the absence of calculus and overhangs. Scores 3 and 4 both require the coloured band to become partially or completely obscured by deeper pocketing.
  20. The correct answer is C (A detailed six-point pocket chart of that sextant): a BPE score of 3 in any sextant warrants a detailed six-point pocket chart of that specific sextant to define the extent of disease more precisely. It does not by itself indicate surgery, which is only considered after non-surgical therapy has failed. A full BPE of 4 or an asterisk anywhere, not a 3, is what triggers a full-mouth chart together with radiographs.
  21. The correct answer is B (A full-mouth six-point pocket chart and radiographic assessment): a BPE score of 4, or an asterisk anywhere, indicates pocketing of 6 millimetres or more and warrants a full-mouth six-point pocket chart together with radiographic assessment before a definitive diagnosis using the 2017 classification is made. A sextant-only chart is the response to a score of 3, not a score of 4. Extraction and simple repeat screening are both premature without full diagnostic information.
  22. The correct answer is C (Six sites per tooth): full periodontal charting records findings at six sites per tooth, mesiobuccal, buccal, distobuccal, mesiolingual, lingual and distolingual, giving a complete picture of pocketing and attachment around each tooth. Recording at only two or four sites would miss disease confined to interproximal or lingual surfaces. Eight sites per tooth is not the recognised convention in UK periodontal charting.
  23. The correct answer is A (7 millimetres): clinical attachment loss is calculated as the sum of the probing pocket depth and the amount of recession, so 5 millimetres plus 2 millimetres gives 7 millimetres of attachment loss. Using pocket depth alone at 5 millimetres would understate the true attachment loss present. It is this combined figure, not pocket depth in isolation, that drives staging under the 2017 classification.
  24. The correct answer is D (Grade I): Miller's Grade I describes mobility of up to 1 millimetre in a horizontal direction, matching the finding described. Grade II requires horizontal mobility exceeding 1 millimetre, and Grade III additionally includes a vertical component of mobility. A grade of 0 is not part of Miller's original scale as described in this classification.
  25. The correct answer is B (Grade III): Grade III on Miller's scale includes vertical mobility in addition to horizontal movement, representing the most advanced degree of mobility. Grade I is limited to horizontal mobility up to 1 millimetre, and Grade II is horizontal mobility beyond 1 millimetre without a vertical component. A vertical component specifically identifies the case as Grade III rather than a lesser grade.
  26. The correct answer is A (Horizontal probing between the roots): furcation involvement is graded clinically by horizontal probing into the area between the roots of a multi-rooted tooth, assessing how far a probe can be passed beneath the crown. Vertical probing along the apex and percussion testing do not evaluate furcation anatomy. Radiographs are a useful adjunct but clinical horizontal probing is the method specifically used to grade furcation involvement.
  27. The correct answer is A (Oral hygiene instruction, removal of plaque-retentive factors, and subgingival instrumentation): non-surgical, cause-related therapy comprises patient education, oral hygiene instruction including interdental cleaning, smoking cessation advice, removal of plaque-retentive factors such as overhangs, and scaling and root surface debridement. Surgery is only considered after this phase has been completed and plaque control demonstrated. Extraction is not a routine first-line response to attachment loss alone.
  28. The correct answer is B (10 to 12 weeks, allowing tissues to heal before reassessment): re-evaluation typically occurs at 10 to 12 weeks because this is the minimum interval required for periodontal tissues to heal and for probing depths to reflect a true response to treatment rather than transient inflammation. Reassessing after only one week or 24 hours would be confounded by ongoing healing. Waiting a full 6 months would unnecessarily delay identifying sites needing further intervention.
  29. The correct answer is B (A 4 millimetre pocket with bleeding on probing): persisting pockets of 4 millimetres or more with bleeding on probing, or any pocket of 6 millimetres or more, indicate a need for further intervention beyond maintenance. A 3 millimetre pocket with no bleeding, a low generalised BPE score, and the complete absence of bleeding are all consistent with a satisfactory response to non-surgical therapy.
  30. The correct answer is A (Consider periodontal surgery such as regenerative osseous surgery): surgical intervention is indicated for persistent deep pockets not responding to non-surgical therapy, particularly where a vertical bony defect could benefit from regenerative techniques, and only once adequate plaque control has been demonstrated, as it has here. Simply repeating hygiene instruction is unlikely to resolve a deep residual defect. Immediate extraction is not indicated when a tooth may still be saved surgically.
  31. The correct answer is D (Continued heavy smoking without willingness to quit undermines healing): surgery is contraindicated where the patient continues to smoke heavily without willingness to quit, since smoking impairs healing and surgical outcomes. This does not mean surgery is categorically impossible for every smoker, only that unmodified heavy smoking is a specific contraindication. Diabetes is a separate risk factor and is not required for smoking alone to make surgery inadvisable.
  32. The correct answer is B (Peri-implant mucositis): peri-implant mucositis is inflammation confined to the soft tissue around an osseointegrated implant, with bleeding on probing and erythema but no progressive bone loss beyond expected initial remodelling, exactly as described. Peri-implantitis additionally requires progressive bone loss beyond that baseline. Periodontitis is a diagnosis for natural teeth, not implants, and osseointegration failure would present very differently, typically with implant mobility.
  33. The correct answer is A (Peri-implantitis, requiring decontamination and possible surgical management): progressive bone loss beyond initial remodelling, together with increasing probing depths and suppuration, defines peri-implantitis, which requires management ranging from non-surgical debridement to surgical decontamination or, in advanced cases, implant removal. Mucositis by definition shows no progressive bone loss. Neither physiological remodelling nor normal osseointegration produces increasing bone loss with suppuration.
  34. The correct answer is B (Kennedy Class I): Kennedy Class I describes bilateral edentulous areas located posterior to the remaining natural teeth, also called bilateral free-end saddles, matching this presentation exactly. Class II is a unilateral free-end saddle rather than a bilateral one. Class III is a bounded saddle with teeth both anterior and posterior, and Class IV crosses the midline anteriorly.
  35. The correct answer is B (Kennedy Class II): Kennedy Class II describes a unilateral edentulous area posterior to the remaining natural teeth, a unilateral free-end saddle, which matches an arch with only one side affected. Class I requires the same pattern bilaterally. Class III requires teeth present both anterior and posterior to the space, which is not the case for a free-end saddle.
  36. The correct answer is D (Kennedy Class III): Kennedy Class III describes a unilateral edentulous area with natural teeth remaining both anterior and posterior to it, known as a bounded saddle, which is exactly the pattern described. Classes I and II both describe free-end saddles with no posterior tooth limiting the space. Class IV is a single anterior space crossing the midline rather than a bounded posterior space.
  37. The correct answer is D (Kennedy Class IV): Kennedy Class IV describes a single edentulous area anterior to the remaining natural teeth that crosses the midline, matching a missing-incisor space with canines and posterior teeth present on both sides. Classes I and II describe posterior free-end saddles rather than an anterior crossing space. Class III is a bounded posterior space rather than an anterior one.
  38. The correct answer is A (The Class III space is a modification, but Class IV can never have one): additional edentulous spaces beyond the class-defining space are described as numbered modifications, except in Class IV, which by definition has no modifications because a further anterior space would change the classification itself. Reclassifying either arch as Class I would be incorrect since neither matches a bilateral free-end pattern. Additional spaces are recorded, not ignored, in every class except Class IV.
  39. The correct answer is D (Support): support resists forces directed towards the underlying tissues, that is, vertical displacement into the mucosa, and is provided by rests on teeth, the denture base contacting mucosa, or a combination of both. Retention instead resists forces that would dislodge the prosthesis away from its bearing surfaces. Stability resists horizontal displacement or rocking, and reciprocation is a specific clasp function rather than a whole-denture mechanical property.
  40. The correct answer is B (Forces that would dislodge the denture away from its bearing surfaces): retention resists forces that would dislodge the prosthesis away from its bearing surfaces, and in removable partial dentures this is provided principally by clasps engaging undercuts. Vertical seating forces relate more to support and insertion rather than retention specifically. Rotational leverage around a free-end saddle is a stability concern addressed by overall design, not solely by clasp retention.
  41. The correct answer is D (Stability): stability resists forces that would displace the prosthesis horizontally or cause rocking, and depends on framework rigidity, the extension and adaptation of the denture base, and how teeth are arranged over the residual ridge. Support instead concerns resistance to vertical displacement into the mucosa. Retention concerns resistance to dislodging forces, and adhesion is only one specific mechanism contributing to complete denture retention.
  42. The correct answer is A (The rigid reciprocal arm): the rigid reciprocal arm sits on the non-retentive side of the abutment tooth, opposing the survey line above the height of contour, and resists forces from the retentive arm during function and insertion. The flexible retentive arm instead engages an undercut below the survey line on the opposite side. The occlusal rest provides support and prevents gingival movement, while the minor connector simply joins the clasp to the major connector.
  43. The correct answer is B (Around 0.25 millimetres): a commonly cited clinical value for a cast cobalt-chromium clasp is around 0.25 millimetres of horizontal undercut, sufficient to resist dislodging forces without permanently deforming the alloy on repeated insertion and removal. A value as small as 0.05 millimetres would provide little meaningful retention. Values of 1.5 or 3.0 millimetres would risk exceeding the elastic limit of a rigid cast alloy clasp.
  44. The correct answer is B (The occlusal or cingulum rest): the occlusal or cingulum rest provides support and prevents the clasp assembly from moving gingivally down the tooth surface during function. The retentive arm resists dislodging forces rather than providing vertical support. The major connector joins the two sides of a partial denture framework, and a guide plane controls the path of insertion rather than providing occlusal support.
  45. The correct answer is D (To control the path of insertion and improve reciprocation and stability): guide planes are parallel surfaces prepared on abutment teeth to control the path of insertion of the denture and to improve reciprocation and stability of the finished prosthesis. They do not increase undercut, which is a separate consideration for the retentive arm. A surveyor is still required to plan clasp position even when guide planes are present.
  46. The correct answer is C (A surveyor): a surveyor is used to determine the survey line, the height of contour relative to the chosen path of insertion, which in turn determines where retentive and reciprocal clasp elements can be placed. A face-bow records the jaw relationship for mounting casts on an articulator, not survey lines. An articulator alone cannot establish undercut relationships without prior surveying, and a pulp tester assesses tooth vitality.
  47. The correct answer is C (Adhesion): adhesion is the physical attraction between the saliva film and the mucosal and fitting surfaces of the denture, contributing directly to retention in the absence of natural teeth. Cohesion instead describes the attraction between molecules within the saliva film itself. Peripheral seal relates to flange extension and atmospheric pressure, and muscular control relates to buccinator and orbicularis oris function against the polished surfaces.
  48. The correct answer is C (Cohesion): cohesion is the attraction between molecules of the saliva film itself, which helps maintain the thin fluid layer that supports retention. Adhesion instead describes attraction between the saliva film and the denture or mucosal surfaces. Residual ridge form and neuromuscular adaptation both contribute to overall denture success but describe anatomical and learned factors rather than a molecular attraction within saliva.
  49. The correct answer is B (Peripheral seal allowing atmospheric pressure to resist displacement): a peripheral seal, achieved by accurate flange extension to the full functional sulcus depth without impinging on muscle attachments, allows atmospheric pressure to resist denture displacement. Cohesion is a separate saliva-related mechanism not dependent on flange extension. Osseointegration applies only to implants, and canine guidance is an occlusal scheme unrelated to denture flange design.
  50. The correct answer is D (It provides better retention than a flat or knife-edge ridge): a well-defined, broad and parallel-sided residual ridge provides better retention than a flat or knife-edge ridge, alongside contributions from muscular control acting against a correctly contoured polished surface. This ridge form is not without effect, contrary to the first option, and it influences overall stability and retention together, not support alone. A good ridge form does not remove the continued need for an accurate peripheral seal.
  51. The correct answer is B (A direct structural and functional connection between living bone and the implant surface): osseointegration, first described by Branemark, is the direct structural and functional connection between living bone and the surface of a load-bearing titanium implant, without an intervening layer of fibrous soft tissue. The presence of fibrous tissue or a periodontal-ligament-like attachment would actually represent failed osseointegration. Luting cement is unrelated to the bone-implant interface itself.
  52. The correct answer is C (Atraumatic surgical technique avoiding thermal bone damage): successful osseointegration depends on implant surface characteristics, atraumatic surgical technique that avoids overheating bone during preparation, primary stability at placement, and an adequate healing period before loading. Deliberately overheating bone would damage it and impair healing rather than aid integration. Immediate heavy loading and placement without primary stability both increase the risk of failure rather than promoting successful osseointegration.
  53. The correct answer is D (An implant-retained or implant-supported overdenture): an implant-retained or implant-supported overdenture is specifically indicated where a conventional complete denture, particularly in the resorbed mandible, provides inadequate retention or stability. A single crown addresses only one missing tooth and does not solve overall denture instability. A resorbed mandible is exactly the scenario where implant support is most valuable, not a contraindication to it.
  54. The correct answer is D (A single dental implant restoration): a single implant crown is indicated precisely where adjacent teeth are unrestored and the patient wishes to avoid preparing them for a conventional bridge, since it does not require reduction of the neighbouring teeth. A conventional bridge would necessarily involve preparing those adjacent teeth. A removable partial denture is a further alternative but does not match a patient's specific wish to avoid any impact on adjacent teeth.
  55. The correct answer is C (Heavy smoking significantly increases implant failure rates): heavy smoking is a recognised risk factor that significantly increases implant failure rates and should be discussed with the patient before proceeding, ideally alongside smoking cessation advice. Smoking is not without effect, and its impact is not limited to natural teeth, since it impairs vascular supply important for osseointegration. Any vasoconstrictive effect of smoking is detrimental to healing rather than beneficial.
  56. The correct answer is B (Risk of medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw): use of certain antiresorptive medications carries a recognised risk of medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw, and this is a specific contraindication or risk factor to discuss and consider before implant placement. Titanium allergy is a separate and much rarer consideration. This medication history represents an additional, specific risk beyond that of a typical implant patient, not an absence of added risk.
  57. The correct answer is C (Centric relation): centric relation is a reproducible, bone-to-bone jaw relationship independent of tooth contact, with the condyles in their most anterosuperior position against the articular eminence and the disc correctly interposed. Centric occlusion instead depends on tooth contact and represents maximum intercuspation. Group function and canine guidance both describe excursive occlusal schemes rather than a static jaw relationship.
  58. The correct answer is C (Centric occlusion (maximum intercuspation)): centric occlusion, also called maximum intercuspation, is the position where the teeth achieve maximum contact and interdigitation, and in most patients this is not identical to centric relation, with a small physiological slide occurring between the two. Centric relation is instead a bone-to-bone position independent of tooth contact. Group function and canine guidance describe lateral excursive contact patterns, not the maximum intercuspal position.
  59. The correct answer is D (Canine guidance): canine guidance is an occlusal scheme in which, during lateral excursive movements, contact occurs only on the canine teeth of the working side, disengaging all posterior teeth. Group function instead involves several working-side teeth sharing the guiding contact. Centric relation is a static jaw position, and occlusal trauma describes a pathological response to force rather than a normal excursive scheme.
  60. The correct answer is A (Group function): group function is a scheme in which several teeth on the working side, typically premolars and sometimes the mesiobuccal cusp of the first molar as well as the canine, share the guiding contact during lateral movement, provided the balancing side remains disengaged. Canine guidance restricts contact to the canine alone. Centric occlusion is a static maximum intercuspal position, not an excursive scheme, and occlusal trauma is a pathological finding.
  61. The correct answer is C (Secondary occlusal trauma): secondary occlusal trauma occurs on a tooth with reduced periodontal support subjected to normal or only slightly increased force, and the described features of mobility, PDL widening and fremitus reflect this. Primary occlusal trauma instead occurs on a tooth with normal periodontal support subjected to excessive force. Gingivitis and peri-implant mucositis are unrelated soft-tissue inflammatory conditions rather than occlusal load findings.
  62. The correct answer is D (Elevators to disrupt the periodontal ligament followed by extraction forceps): simple extraction is achieved using elevators to expand the socket and disrupt the periodontal ligament, followed by extraction forceps applied to the root with controlled movements appropriate to tooth morphology. Raising a flap, sectioning the root, and surgical bone removal are all features of surgical rather than simple extraction, and are unnecessary for a straightforward single-rooted tooth.
  63. The correct answer is D (Surgical extraction, since simple means are unlikely to succeed): surgical extraction is indicated when a tooth cannot be delivered by simple means, for example because of root morphology such as curved or divergent roots, or dense surrounding bone, as described here. Increasing force with forceps alone risks root fracture rather than successful delivery. General anaesthesia is a separate anaesthetic decision, not a defining feature of surgical extraction technique itself.
  64. The correct answer is B (To prevent thermal damage to bone during cutting): copious saline irrigation during bone removal with a surgical handpiece is used primarily to prevent thermal damage to bone from frictional heat generated by cutting. Improved visibility is a secondary benefit rather than the primary purpose. Irrigation does not substitute for local anaesthesia, and its main role here is during cutting rather than solely as a post-extraction disinfectant.
  65. The correct answer is D (Relationship to the inferior alveolar canal, angulation, depth of impaction and available space): assessment of a lower third molar requires evaluation of its relationship to the inferior alveolar canal, its angulation, depth of impaction, available space distal to the second molar, and any associated pathology.
