Blood group, gingival colour alone, and the state of the opposite arch's wisdom teeth are not part of this specific pre-operative surgical assessment.
  66. The correct answer is C (Close proximity to the inferior alveolar nerve, possibly warranting CBCT): darkening of the root where it crosses the canal and interruption of the canal's white line are recognised radiographic signs suggesting close proximity to the inferior alveolar nerve, and cone beam computed tomography may be indicated to further define the three-dimensional relationship before surgery. These signs indicate risk, not the absence of one. Dry socket is a post-extraction complication and cannot be diagnosed from a pre-operative radiograph.
  67. The correct answer is B (No removal, since routine prophylactic extraction is not advised): NICE guidance advises against routine prophylactic removal of pathology-free impacted third molars, since this exposes patients to surgical risk, including nerve injury, without a demonstrated corresponding benefit. Prophylactic removal or surgical exposure would not be justified for an asymptomatic, disease-free tooth. Cosmetic preference alone is not a recognised indication under this guidance.
  68. The correct answer is D (Removal is indicated, given the history of recurrent pericoronitis): removal of an impacted third molar is indicated where there is a clear history of recurrent pericoronitis, which this patient has demonstrated. Declining treatment indefinitely ignores an established indication for extraction. Antibiotics may manage an acute episode but do not prevent recurrence long term, and removal of an opposing tooth is not a prerequisite for treating the affected molar.
  69. The correct answer is D (Dry socket (alveolar osteitis)): dry socket typically presents two to four days after extraction with severe, throbbing pain, a malodorous empty-looking socket, and no significant swelling or systemic signs of infection, exactly matching this presentation. Osteomyelitis and abscess would typically show swelling or systemic signs, which are absent here. An oro-antral communication relates specifically to upper molar extractions and presents with different signs such as nasal regurgitation.
  70. The correct answer is A (Smoking, oral contraceptive use, and traumatic extraction): recognised risk factors for dry socket include smoking, oral contraceptive use, traumatic extraction, extraction of mandibular molars, and poor oral hygiene. Non-smoking status and atraumatic technique would instead reduce risk. Upper anterior extraction is not the classic site of concern, and excellent oral hygiene is protective rather than a risk factor.
  71. The correct answer is C (Socket irrigation, debridement and an obtundent dressing; antibiotics are not routinely indicated): management of dry socket is symptomatic, comprising socket irrigation with warm saline, gentle debridement, and placement of an obtundent, antiseptic dressing, with resolution generally expected over one to two weeks; antibiotics are not routinely indicated since this is not primarily an infective process. Re-extraction is unnecessary and inappropriate. Untreated dry socket typically causes prolonged pain over one to two weeks, not rapid resolution within a day.
  72. The correct answer is A (An oro-antral communication): fluid regurgitation into the nose on drinking and a change in voice resonance are recognised clinical signs of an oro-antral communication, which most commonly arises after extraction of an upper first or second molar because of the proximity of the roots to the antral floor. Dry socket presents with pain rather than nasal regurgitation. Inferior alveolar nerve injury causes altered lip and chin sensation, not nasal or voice changes.
  73. The correct answer is B (Conservative management with advice to avoid nose-blowing, plus antibiotics and decongestant): small communications of a few millimetres, without pre-existing sinus pathology, may be managed conservatively with instructions to avoid nose-blowing and sneezing with the mouth closed, together with antibiotics, decongestant, and encouragement of clot formation. Surgical closure with a flap is generally reserved for larger communications. Sinus removal is a disproportionate response to a small, uncomplicated communication.
  74. The correct answer is D (The inferior alveolar nerve): inferior alveolar nerve injury typically results from direct trauma during bone removal, sectioning, or elevation where the root apices are intimately related to the canal, and presents as altered sensation of the lip, chin and labial gingiva on the affected side, matching this description. Lingual nerve injury instead causes tongue sensation or taste disturbance. The facial and greater palatine nerves are not implicated in this typical extraction-related pattern.
  75. The correct answer is A (The lingual nerve, because it can lie in contact with or above the lingual crest of bone): lingual nerve injury more often results from lingual flap retraction or perforation of the lingual plate, since the nerve can lie in direct contact with, or even above, the lingual crest of bone in some patients, and it presents with altered sensation or taste disturbance affecting the tongue. The inferior alveolar nerve instead causes lip and chin symptoms. The mental and buccal nerves are not the nerves principally at risk from lingual flap retraction.
  76. The correct answer is B (Refer for specialist assessment and possible microsurgical exploration): most nerve injuries after third molar surgery are neurapraxias that resolve within weeks to months, but persistent anaesthesia, paraesthesia or dysaesthesia beyond this period should prompt specialist referral for assessment and possible microsurgical exploration. Reassurance alone is inappropriate once recovery has clearly not occurred within the expected timeframe. Repeating the original surgery would not address a persistent nerve injury and carries further risk.
  77. The correct answer is B (Alveoloplasty): alveoloplasty is the surgical recontouring of a sharp or irregular alveolar ridge, typically performed at the time of extraction, to provide a smoother base for a subsequent denture. Apicectomy addresses periapical pathology at a root apex rather than ridge contour. Frenectomy removes an abnormal frenal attachment, and guided tissue regeneration is a periodontal regenerative technique, neither of which describes ridge recontouring.
  78. The correct answer is C (Removal of the bony undercut): removal of bony or fibrous undercuts that would otherwise prevent denture insertion or cause trauma is a recognised pre-prosthetic surgical procedure aimed at improving the foundation for a subsequent prosthesis. Simply increasing flange length over an undercut would not resolve the insertion problem and could increase trauma. An implant is a separate restorative option rather than a solution to an existing bony obstruction.
  79. The correct answer is A (Reduction of the prominent mylohyoid ridge): reduction of a prominent mylohyoid ridge or genial tubercles is a recognised pre-prosthetic procedure performed where these features interfere with a lower denture flange. Extracting further molars would not address the ridge prominence itself. Apicectomy and surgical exposure for orthodontic traction are unrelated procedures with no role in managing denture flange interference.
  80. The correct answer is B (Apicectomy): apicectomy is indicated for periapical pathology that has not resolved with conventional root canal retreatment, involving surgical access to and resection of the root apex together with retrograde filling. Alveoloplasty addresses ridge contour rather than periapical disease, and frenectomy addresses an abnormal frenal attachment. Extraction may ultimately be needed if apicectomy fails or is not feasible, but it is not the first-line minor surgical option described here.
  81. The correct answer is C (Surgical exposure of the unerupted tooth to allow orthodontic traction): surgical exposure of unerupted teeth to allow orthodontic traction is a recognised minor oral surgery procedure used to bring a tooth such as an unerupted canine into the line of the arch. Apicectomy and alveoloplasty are unrelated procedures addressing different problems. Extraction would remove the tooth entirely rather than support the orthodontic plan to retain and align it.
  82. The correct answer is B (Frenectomy): frenectomy is indicated where an abnormal frenal attachment is contributing to a diastema, gingival recession, or denture instability, matching this presentation exactly. Apicectomy and alveoloplasty address entirely different anatomical problems, periapical pathology and ridge contour respectively. Guided tissue regeneration is a periodontal regenerative procedure unrelated to frenal attachment.
  83. The correct answer is C (Biopsy of the soft tissue lesion): biopsy of soft tissue lesions is a recognised minor oral surgery procedure used to obtain tissue for histological diagnosis when a lesion persists despite removal of obvious local irritants. Frenectomy, alveoloplasty and apicectomy are all unrelated procedures addressing different structures and would not establish a tissue diagnosis for a persistent mucosal lesion.
  84. The correct answer is C (To reduce the force needed for delivery and preserve surrounding bone): sectioning the tooth or roots reduces the force needed for delivery and preserves surrounding bone compared with attempting to deliver an intact, awkwardly angulated tooth. It does not affect the need for local anaesthesia, which is determined separately. Sectioning is intended to minimise, not increase, the bone removal required, and postoperative review remains necessary regardless of technique.
  85. The correct answer is A (Distoangular impactions are generally more difficult to remove because of the path required past the ramus): a distoangular impaction typically requires the tooth to be delivered along a path that must negotiate the ramus of the mandible, generally making it more surgically challenging than a mesioangular impaction. Angulation is a recognised factor in assessing surgical difficulty, not an irrelevant one. Bone removal may well still be required regardless of angulation.
  86. The correct answer is C (Cone beam computed tomography to define the three-dimensional relationship): where radiographic signs suggest close proximity to the inferior alveolar nerve, cone beam computed tomography may be indicated to further define the three-dimensional relationship before surgery. Simply repeating the same two-dimensional panoramic view would not resolve the ambiguity about depth and position. A periapical radiograph of the opposing arch is irrelevant to assessing the lower third molar's relationship to the canal.
  87. The correct answer is A (It can enlarge a small perforation): a positive result on gentle nose-blowing with the nostrils pinched supports a diagnosis of oro-antral communication, but this test should be used cautiously as it can enlarge a small perforation through positive pressure. It is not without diagnostic value, since a positive result is a genuine supportive sign. The caution relates to potential harm from repeated or forceful testing, not an absolute contraindication in every case.
  88. The correct answer is A (This presentation suggests an infective process, unlike the classically non-infective dry socket): dry socket classically presents with severe pain but no significant swelling or systemic signs of infection, since it is not primarily an infective process. Swelling, fever and purulent discharge instead point towards a true infective complication requiring different management, potentially including antibiotics. These are clinically distinct presentations, not the same condition, and dry socket by definition lacks purulent discharge.
  89. The correct answer is C (It increases the risk of lingual nerve injury during flap retraction): because the lingual nerve can lie in direct contact with, or even above, the lingual crest of bone in some patients, this anatomical variation increases the risk of injury during lingual flap retraction or perforation of the lingual plate. It is directly relevant to surgical risk, not irrelevant to it. This variation specifically concerns the lingual nerve's own position, not the inferior alveolar nerve.
  90. The correct answer is C (Removal is indicated where the tooth must be removed to facilitate necessary treatment such as orthognathic surgery): recognised indications for removing an otherwise pathology-free third molar include cases where the tooth must be removed to facilitate other necessary treatment, such as orthognathic surgery. This is a genuine indication, so the first option is incorrect. Recurrent pericoronitis is one indication among several, not the only one, and orthognathic surgery is not simply cancelled because a third molar is present.
  91. The correct answer is D (5 millimetres): the chart shows a probing pocket depth of 3 millimetres and recession of 2 millimetres at the mesiobuccal site of tooth 46, and clinical attachment loss equals probing pocket depth plus recession, giving 5 millimetres. Reading pocket depth alone at 3 millimetres would understate the true attachment loss. The other buccal sites on this tooth show smaller combined values and do not correspond to the mesiobuccal figure requested.
  92. The correct answer is C (Gingival inflammation without attachment loss): bleeding on probing at every site with pocket depths not exceeding 3 millimetres and no recession indicates inflammation confined to the soft tissue rather than periodontitis, since there is no evidence of attachment loss. Stage II periodontitis specifically requires clinical attachment loss of 3 to 4 millimetres, which is not demonstrated here. Peri-implantitis cannot apply to a natural tooth, and Grade C requires periodontitis to be present at all before grading is relevant.
  93. The correct answer is C (6 millimetres): with no recession present, clinical attachment loss equals probing pocket depth alone, so a 6 millimetre pocket at the distobuccal site of tooth 36 gives 6 millimetres of attachment loss at that site. Adding a recession figure would only be appropriate if recession were actually recorded, which it is not here. The other listed values do not match the 6 millimetre pocket depth shown for this specific site.
  94. The correct answer is D (Stage III or IV, given attachment loss of 5mm or more with furcation involvement): an 8 millimetre pocket with no recession gives clinical attachment loss of 8 millimetres, well above the 5 millimetre threshold for Stage III or IV, and the presence of furcation involvement is a recognised feature of these more advanced stages. Stages I and II both require far less attachment loss than shown here. The findings clearly indicate periodontitis is present, not absent, at this tooth.
  95. The correct answer is C (7 millimetres): clinical attachment loss is the sum of probing pocket depth and recession, so a 4 millimetre pocket plus 3 millimetres of recession at the buccal site of tooth 13 gives 7 millimetres of attachment loss. Reading the pocket depth or the recession value in isolation would each understate the true loss of attachment. This combined figure is what determines staging, not either measurement alone.
  96. The correct answer is D (7 millimetres): the 3 millimetre pocket depth plus the 4 millimetres of recession recorded at the lingual site of tooth 43 sum to a clinical attachment loss of 7 millimetres. Reporting the pocket depth of 3 millimetres alone would substantially underestimate the true attachment loss present at this site. Attachment loss, not pocket depth in isolation, is the figure used for staging periodontitis.
  97. The correct answer is D (Attachment loss equals the probing pocket depth alone at each site): clinical attachment loss is the sum of probing pocket depth and recession, so when recession is zero, attachment loss is numerically equal to the probing pocket depth recorded at each site. It is not correct to say attachment loss cannot be calculated, since the pocket depth alone is sufficient information when recession is absent. Assuming zero attachment loss would ignore the substantial pocketing clearly present on this chart.
  98. The correct answer is A (2 millimetres): when the gingival margin lies coronal to the cementoenamel junction, this is recorded as a negative value, so clinical attachment loss is the pocket depth of 3 millimetres reduced by the 1 millimetre of coronal margin position, giving 2 millimetres of true attachment loss. Simply adding the pocket depth and recession as positive numbers would overstate the attachment loss present. This example shows why the sign of the recession measurement matters to the calculation.
  99. The correct answer is B (5 millimetres): with no recession recorded, attachment loss at the deepest lingual site equals the probing pocket depth shown, which is 5 millimetres. Furcation involvement is a separate finding recorded alongside pocket depth and does not itself alter the arithmetic of the attachment loss calculation. There is no basis in the chart for either a much larger or a zero figure given the pocket depth actually recorded.
  100. The correct answer is C (6 millimetres): adding the 4 millimetre probing pocket depth and the 2 millimetres of recession at the mesiobuccal site of tooth 24 gives a clinical attachment loss of 6 millimetres. The recorded mobility grade is a separate periodontal finding and does not enter into the attachment loss calculation itself. Reading either the pocket depth or recession value alone would understate the true combined attachment loss.
  101. The correct answer is B (Health, or at most gingivitis without attachment loss): shallow pocket depths, absence of bleeding on probing, and no recorded recession together indicate periodontal health with no evidence of attachment loss, or at most low-grade gingivitis if inflammation were present elsewhere. Stage I periodontitis specifically requires demonstrable clinical attachment loss, which this chart does not show. Peri-implantitis cannot apply to a natural tooth, and Stage IV requires far more severe findings than are recorded here.
  102. The correct answer is D (8 millimetres): adding the 7 millimetre probing pocket depth to the 1 millimetre of recession at the mesiolingual site of tooth 17 gives a clinical attachment loss of 8 millimetres. Suppuration and grade III furcation involvement are additional supportive findings of advanced periodontal disease but are recorded separately from the numerical attachment loss calculation. Reporting the pocket depth of 7 millimetres alone would understate the true attachment loss present.
  103. The correct answer is D (Close proximity between the root and the inferior alveolar nerve): darkening of the root where it crosses the canal, together with root deflection and narrowing, are recognised radiographic signs suggesting close proximity to the inferior alveolar nerve, and may prompt further three-dimensional imaging before surgery. These findings actively indicate a relationship to the canal, not its absence. Peri-implantitis and a periapical cyst are unrelated diagnoses not suggested by this appearance.
  104. The correct answer is B (Mesioangular): a mesioangular impaction describes a tooth angled mesially against the adjacent second molar, which is the pattern shown on this radiograph. Distoangular impactions tilt distally towards the ramus rather than mesially. Horizontal impactions lie at roughly ninety degrees to the occlusal plane, and vertical impactions are aligned in the normal upright position, neither of which matches an angled mesial presentation.
  105. The correct answer is D (Oro-antral communication): close root proximity to the maxillary antral floor, with only a thin intervening bony septum, is exactly the anatomical situation that increases the risk of an oro-antral communication during extraction of an upper first or second molar. The inferior alveolar nerve lies in the mandible and is not relevant to a maxillary extraction. While dry socket remains a general extraction risk, the specific radiographic finding described relates to antral proximity, not to socket healing.
  106. The correct answer is A (Horizontal): a horizontal impaction describes a tooth lying with its long axis roughly parallel to the occlusal plane, essentially on its side, which matches this description. Mesioangular and distoangular impactions are both tilted at an angle rather than lying flat. A vertical impaction is upright and aligned with the second molar, the opposite orientation to what is shown here.
  107. The correct answer is A (It is a recognised sign suggesting close proximity to the inferior alveolar nerve): interruption of the white line of the canal is one of the recognised radiographic signs, alongside root darkening, deflection and canal narrowing, suggesting close proximity between the root and the inferior alveolar nerve. This sign is relevant before any surgery has taken place and does not itself confirm that damage has already occurred, only that risk is elevated. The inferior alveolar canal is a mandibular structure and this sign is specific to lower third molars.
  108. The correct answer is B (Generalised horizontal bone loss): bone loss affecting the interdental septa at a similar, reduced level around several adjacent teeth describes a generalised horizontal pattern, in contrast to a vertical or angular defect confined to one surface of one tooth. This description does not match an isolated furcation defect, which would be localised to the area between the roots of a single multi-rooted tooth. The presence of reduced bone height around several teeth rules out normal bone levels.
  109. The correct answer is A (A localised vertical (angular) bony defect): a wedge-shaped area of bone loss confined to one root surface and deeper than the adjacent bone level describes a localised vertical, or angular, bony defect, a pattern associated with Stage III or IV periodontitis and potentially amenable to regenerative surgery. Generalised horizontal loss instead affects multiple teeth at a similar level rather than one deep localised area. There is no implant present in this description, so physiological remodelling around one is not relevant.
  110. The correct answer is A (Greater surgical difficulty is likely, potentially requiring bone removal and sectioning): depth of impaction and the amount of available space distal to the second molar are both factors specifically assessed before third molar surgery because a deeply positioned tooth with limited space typically requires more extensive bone removal and sectioning to achieve delivery. This combination makes a straightforward, no-bone-removal extraction unlikely rather than probable. Depth and space are directly relevant to planning, not irrelevant, and should inform rather than prevent further assessment.
EMQ Set EMQ-perio-1

For each periodontal presentation below, select the ONE most appropriate diagnosis from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Gingivitis

B. Stage I periodontitis

C. Stage II periodontitis

D. Stage III periodontitis

E. Stage IV periodontitis

F. Peri-implant mucositis

  1. The correct answer is A (Gingivitis): bleeding and erythema with no clinical attachment loss confirm inflammation confined to the soft tissue, which is reversible with plaque control and does not meet the definition of periodontitis at any stage.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stage I periodontitis): attachment loss of 1 to 2 millimetres with bone loss confined to the coronal third and no tooth loss due to periodontitis exactly meets the definition of initial, Stage I disease.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stage III periodontitis): attachment loss of 5 millimetres or more, bone loss beyond the coronal third with vertical defects, and up to four teeth lost to periodontitis together define severe, Stage III periodontitis.
  4. The correct answer is E (Stage IV periodontitis): losing five or more teeth to periodontitis together with masticatory dysfunction, bite collapse and drifting defines Stage IV, severe periodontitis with potential for loss of the dentition.
  5. The correct answer is F (Peri-implant mucositis): reversible soft tissue inflammation with bleeding but no progressive bone loss beyond expected initial remodelling defines peri-implant mucositis, the implant analogue of gingivitis.
EMQ Set EMQ-perio-2

For each denture design problem below, select the ONE most appropriate design principle or fix from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Prepare a guide plane to control the path of insertion

B. Increase clasp undercut engagement for retention

C. Improve peripheral seal by extending the denture flange

D. Use a rigid major connector to reduce rotational leverage on a free-end saddle

E. Reposition the rest to prevent gingival impingement

F. Survey the cast before finalising clasp position

G. Improve residual ridge form assessment before construction

  1. The correct answer is D (Use a rigid major connector to reduce rotational leverage on a free-end saddle): bilateral free-end saddles rely heavily on mucosal support and are prone to rotational leverage around the terminal abutment, which is minimised by a rigid, well-designed major connector rather than by clasp adjustment alone.
  2. The correct answer is B (Increase clasp undercut engagement for retention): retention depends on the retentive arm engaging sufficient undercut, and a shallow engagement of well under the typical 0.25 millimetre value used for cast clasps will fail to resist dislodging forces adequately.
  3. The correct answer is C (Improve peripheral seal by extending the denture flange): an inadequate flange extension prevents the peripheral seal needed for atmospheric pressure to resist displacement, so extending the flange to the full functional sulcus depth without impinging on muscle attachments is the appropriate fix.
  4. The correct answer is E (Reposition the rest to prevent gingival impingement): the rest should provide support and prevent gingival movement of the clasp without itself traumatising the gingival margin, so an incorrectly positioned rest seat should be corrected rather than left in place.
  5. The correct answer is F (Survey the cast before finalising clasp position): the survey line, the height of contour relative to the chosen path of insertion, determines where retentive and reciprocal clasp elements can be placed, so clasp design must always be planned on a surveyor before construction to avoid this error.
EMQ Set EMQ-perio-3

For each implant scenario below, select the ONE most appropriate indication or contraindication from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Suitable indication: avoid preparing unrestored adjacent teeth

B. Suitable indication: poor retention of a conventional denture in a resorbed mandible

C. Contraindication: uncontrolled diabetes

D. Contraindication: heavy smoking

E. Contraindication: history of head and neck radiotherapy

F. Contraindication: use of high-dose antiresorptive medication

G. Contraindication: insufficient bone volume without grafting

  1. The correct answer is A (Suitable indication: avoid preparing unrestored adjacent teeth): a single implant crown allows replacement of a missing tooth without preparing healthy adjacent teeth, exactly matching this patient's stated preference and clinical situation.
  2. The correct answer is B (Suitable indication: poor retention of a conventional denture in a resorbed mandible): implant-retained or implant-supported overdentures are specifically indicated where conventional complete denture retention and stability are inadequate, particularly in a resorbed mandible.
  3. The correct answer is D (Contraindication: heavy smoking): heavy smoking significantly increases implant failure rates and represents a specific risk factor that must be discussed, and ideally addressed, before proceeding with implant treatment.
  4. The correct answer is F (Contraindication: use of high-dose antiresorptive medication): antiresorptive medications carry a recognised risk of medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw, making this an important contraindication or risk factor requiring careful discussion before implant surgery.
  5. The correct answer is G (Contraindication: insufficient bone volume without grafting): inadequate bone volume without adjunctive grafting is a recognised contraindication to straightforward implant placement at that site until augmentation has been carried out.
EMQ Set EMQ-perio-4

For each post-extraction presentation below, select the ONE most appropriate diagnosis and management from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Dry socket, managed with saline irrigation, debridement and an obtundent dressing

B. Small oro-antral communication, managed conservatively with advice to avoid nose-blowing

C. Large oro-antral communication, managed with a buccal advancement flap

D. Inferior alveolar nerve injury, likely neurapraxia, reviewed over time

E. Lingual nerve injury, reviewed with referral if persistent beyond expected recovery

F. Recurrent pericoronitis, managed with irrigation and consideration of extraction

  1. The correct answer is A (Dry socket, managed with saline irrigation, debridement and an obtundent dressing): this classic presentation of pain without infection two to four days post-extraction is dry socket, treated symptomatically without routine antibiotics.
  2. The correct answer is C (Large oro-antral communication, managed with a buccal advancement flap): a large, persistent communication generally requires surgical closure, most often with a buccal advancement flap, rather than conservative management alone.
  3. The correct answer is D (Inferior alveolar nerve injury, likely neurapraxia, reviewed over time): altered lip and chin sensation with normal tongue sensation localises the injury to the inferior alveolar nerve, most commonly a neurapraxia expected to resolve within weeks to months.
  4. The correct answer is E (Lingual nerve injury, reviewed with referral if persistent beyond expected recovery): tongue sensation and taste disturbance with normal lip sensation indicates lingual rather than inferior alveolar nerve injury, warranting referral if it persists beyond the expected recovery period.
  5. The correct answer is F (Recurrent pericoronitis, managed with irrigation and consideration of extraction): a clear history of recurrent pericoronitis is a recognised indication for removal of the associated third molar, following acute management of any active episode.
EMQ Set EMQ-perio-5

For each occlusal finding below, select the ONE most appropriate concept from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Centric relation

B. Centric occlusion (maximum intercuspation)

C. Canine guidance

D. Group function

E. Primary occlusal trauma

F. Secondary occlusal trauma

  1. The correct answer is A (Centric relation): a reproducible, bone-to-bone jaw relationship independent of tooth contact, with the condyles seated anterosuperiorly, is by definition centric relation.
  2. The correct answer is B (Centric occlusion (maximum intercuspation)): the position of maximum tooth contact and interdigitation, distinct from the bone-to-bone centric relation position, is centric occlusion, also called maximum intercuspation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Canine guidance): contact restricted solely to the working-side canines during lateral excursion, with full posterior disengagement, defines the canine guidance occlusal scheme.
  4. The correct answer is F (Secondary occlusal trauma): trauma developing on a tooth with reduced periodontal support under normal or only slightly increased force is secondary occlusal trauma, often signalling active periodontitis rather than a purely occlusal problem.
  5. The correct answer is E (Primary occlusal trauma): trauma occurring on a tooth with normal periodontal support subjected to excessive force, such as from a high restoration, is by definition primary occlusal trauma.
  6. The correct answer is C (All twenty primary teeth are present with generalised spacing including primate spaces.): By around two and a half to three years the primary dentition is normally complete with all twenty teeth present. Generalised spacing, including the primate spaces mesial to the upper canines and distal to the lower canines, is a normal feature at this stage. This spacing later accommodates the wider permanent incisors. First permanent molars do not erupt until around six years of age.
  7. The correct answer is D (The lower permanent central incisors usually erupt around the same time.): The mixed dentition transition begins around six years of age with eruption of the first permanent molars distal to the second primary molars, alongside the lower permanent central incisors. Upper canines, premolars and lateral incisors erupt later, generally between nine and twelve years for canines and premolars. Recognising this typical timing helps identify delayed or ectopic eruption.
  8. The correct answer is B (It is the space gained because primary canines and molars are wider than their successors.): The leeway space arises because the combined width of the primary canine, first molar and second molar exceeds that of their permanent successors, and this difference is greater in the lower arch. This surplus space allows the first permanent molar to drift mesially or can be preserved to relieve mild crowding. It is unrelated to third molar eruption.
  9. The correct answer is C (Class I occlusion, a normal anteroposterior molar relationship.): Class I occlusion describes a normal molar relationship in which the mesiobuccal cusp of the upper first molar occludes with the mesiobuccal groove of the lower first molar, with lower incisor edges meeting near the cingulum plateau of the upper incisors. Malocclusion such as crowding can still occur within a Class I molar relationship. Class II and III describe distal or mesial displacement of the lower arch respectively.
  10. The correct answer is B (Class II division 1 malocclusion.): Class II division 1 malocclusion features a distal lower arch relationship with proclined or normally inclined upper incisors and an increased overjet, often with incompetent lip seal and lower lip trapping that can perpetuate the overjet. Class II division 2 instead shows retroclined upper central incisors with a reduced or negative overjet and increased overbite. Neither Class I nor Class III fits this proclined, increased-overjet presentation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Class II division 2 malocclusion.): Class II division 2 malocclusion is characterised by retroclined upper central incisors, often with proclined and rotated upper laterals, a reduced or negative overjet, and an increased, sometimes traumatic, overbite. This pattern is associated with a more horizontal growth pattern than Class II division 1. Class II division 1 instead shows proclined incisors and an increased overjet.
  12. The correct answer is A (Combined orthodontic and orthognathic surgical treatment.): Severe skeletal Class III malocclusion with marked mandibular prognathism is usually best managed once growth is complete using a combined orthodontic and orthognathic surgical approach, since orthodontics alone cannot correct a significant underlying skeletal discrepancy. Functional appliances work by redirecting growth and are of no benefit after growth has finished. Observation would leave a functionally and aesthetically significant discrepancy untreated.
  13. The correct answer is C (Arrange a radiograph to investigate the position of the permanent successor.): Prolonged retention of a primary tooth beyond the expected eruption date of its successor can indicate ectopic position, impaction, or absence of the permanent tooth, and warrants radiographic investigation. Extracting the primary tooth or fitting a space maintainer before establishing the cause could worsen the underlying problem. Reassurance without investigation risks missing a genuine developmental issue.
  14. The correct answer is C (A band and loop space maintainer.): The band and loop appliance is indicated when a single primary molar is lost unilaterally, using an adjacent tooth as an abutment to maintain space for the permanent successor. A lingual arch is used for bilateral space maintenance in the lower arch using erupted permanent molars as abutments. A distal shoe is reserved for cases where the first permanent molar has not yet erupted.
  15. The correct answer is B (A distal shoe appliance.): A distal shoe appliance is used when the first permanent molar has not yet erupted and the second primary molar is lost early, since its submerged guide plate directs the erupting first permanent molar into correct position. A lingual arch requires erupted permanent molars as abutments, which are not yet present here. A band and loop is used for a single unilateral loss rather than bilateral early loss before molar eruption.
  16. The correct answer is A (A shift of the dental midline towards the extraction side.): Loss of a primary canine can cause the labial segment to drift towards the extraction side, leading to a shift of the dental midline, and this should be assessed carefully. The leeway space concept relates to molar and premolar width differences rather than canine loss. Canine loss does not accelerate lateral incisor eruption or widen the primate space.
  17. The correct answer is D (Discuss options with orthodontic input as part of the wider treatment plan.): Where a permanent successor is congenitally absent, the decision to maintain space, allow closure, or plan prosthetic or implant replacement should be made in conjunction with the wider treatment plan and ideally with orthodontic input. There is no single default management, since factors like occlusion, aesthetics and space vary between patients. The space will not close or resolve spontaneously without active management.
  18. The correct answer is C (A removable appliance using a spring to tip the tooth labially.): Removable appliances work by tipping teeth through springs, bows or screws, making them suitable for simple movements such as correcting a single anterior crossbite where only tipping is required. Fixed appliances provide three-dimensional control but are not necessary for such a limited, simple movement. Functional and distal shoe appliances address entirely different clinical problems.
  19. The correct answer is B (A fixed pre-adjusted edgewise bracket appliance.): Fixed pre-adjusted edgewise appliances allow full three-dimensional control of tooth movement, including tipping, torque, rotation and bodily movement, because the bracket slot and archwire can apply forces and moments in all three planes. Removable appliances can only tip teeth and cannot achieve this level of control. Functional appliances and space maintainers serve different purposes altogether.
  20. The correct answer is C (Plaque accumulation around the brackets causing decalcification.): Oral hygiene around fixed appliances requires particular attention because plaque readily accumulates around brackets, increasing the risk of decalcification and caries, which presents as white spot lesions. This is a well-recognised drawback of fixed appliance treatment despite its not depending on patient compliance to stay in place. Root resorption and allergic reactions are unrelated to this presentation.
  21. The correct answer is D (A functional appliance such as a twin block.): Functional appliances, such as the twin block, work by altering the postural position of the mandible to harness or redirect growth forces, and are most commonly used for Class II division 1 malocclusion during the adolescent growth spurt. Fixed appliances instead provide precise mechanical tooth movement rather than growth modification. Removable expansion appliances and space maintainers serve different clinical purposes.
  22. The correct answer is B (The Dental Health Component of IOTN.): The Dental Health Component of the Index of Orthodontic Treatment Need grades malocclusion from grade 1, no need for treatment, to grade 5, very great need, based on the single worst occlusal feature such as overjet, crossbite or overbite. The Aesthetic Component instead uses standardised photographs to score anterior tooth appearance from 1 to 10. Angle's classification and leeway space describe different aspects of occlusion.
  23. The correct answer is C (The Aesthetic Component of IOTN.): The Aesthetic Component of IOTN uses ten standardised photographs showing a graded range of dental attractiveness, against which a patient's own anterior appearance is compared and scored from 1 to 10. The Dental Health Component instead grades the single worst occlusal feature from 1 to 5. Angle's classification describes molar and incisor relationships rather than aesthetic appearance.
  24. The correct answer is D (Yes, since Dental Health Component 3 with Aesthetic Component 6 or above qualifies.): For NHS orthodontic treatment in England, a patient generally needs Dental Health Component 4 or 5, or Dental Health Component 3 combined with an Aesthetic Component score of 6 or above, to qualify for funding. This patient's Dental Health Component 3 with Aesthetic Component 7 meets that combined threshold. Age is not itself a determining factor in this eligibility assessment.
  25. The correct answer is A (The patient does not meet NHS eligibility criteria and would need private treatment.): Patients falling below the combined threshold of Dental Health Component 4 or 5, or 3 with an Aesthetic Component of 6 or above, do not meet NHS eligibility criteria and would need to seek private treatment. A Dental Health Component of 2 with a low Aesthetic Component score of 4 clearly falls below this threshold. Neither the Aesthetic Component alone nor an urgent referral overrides these combined criteria.
  26. The correct answer is D (Tell-show-do.): Tell-show-do involves explaining a procedure in child-friendly language, demonstrating it on a safe surface such as the child's finger, and then performing the procedure as described, helping to reduce anxiety through familiarity. Positive reinforcement instead praises desired behaviour to encourage repetition. Voice control and desensitisation are distinct techniques involving tone change and graded exposure respectively.
  27. The correct answer is C (Voice control.): Voice control involves a firm but not frightening change in tone to gain a child's attention during treatment. Tell-show-do is a preparatory explain-demonstrate-perform technique, while modelling involves the child observing another child or a video coping well with treatment. Enhancing control instead gives the child a sense of agency, such as a stop signal.
  28. The correct answer is A (Enhancing control.): Enhancing control gives the child a sense of agency during treatment, such as a stop signal they can raise if they need a break, which can reduce anxiety by giving them some influence over the procedure. Voice control instead involves adjusting tone to gain attention, and distraction diverts the child's focus elsewhere. Positive reinforcement praises desired behaviour rather than granting control.
  29. The correct answer is D (Inhalation sedation with titrated nitrous oxide and oxygen.): When non-pharmacological techniques are insufficient, conscious sedation, most commonly inhalation sedation with titrated nitrous oxide and oxygen, reduces anxiety while keeping the child conscious and cooperative. General anaesthesia is reserved for cases where other techniques, including conscious sedation, have failed or are inappropriate. Physical restraint and routine intravenous sedation are not appropriate first additional steps in this context.
  30. The correct answer is A (Explain that general anaesthesia is a last resort, not used for convenience alone.): General anaesthesia is reserved for cases where other techniques have failed or are inappropriate, and is never used solely for the convenience of extracting teeth that could otherwise be managed under local anaesthesia with appropriate behaviour support. Parental preference alone does not justify bypassing less invasive techniques given the risks associated with general anaesthesia. Alternatives should be discussed and attempted first.
  31. The correct answer is B (Up to four times a year.): Fluoride varnish is recommended at least twice yearly for all children from the eruption of the first primary teeth, but for children assessed as being at higher risk of caries, application up to four times a year is recommended. Twice yearly represents the baseline recommendation for standard risk rather than the higher-risk regimen. Once every two years or a single lifetime application would be markedly insufficient.
  32. The correct answer is C (Placement of a resin-based fissure sealant.): Fissure sealants, most commonly resin-based, are recommended for permanent molars in children at risk of caries, applied as soon as practicable after eruption, and work by physically blocking the deep pits and fissures where plaque would otherwise stagnate. Fluoride varnish alone does not address the physical anatomical risk of deep fissures in the same way. High-strength toothpaste is reserved for high caries risk generally, not this specific anatomical feature, and delaying prevention risks caries developing first.
  33. The correct answer is C (A smear of toothpaste containing at least 1,000 parts per million fluoride.): Children under three years old should use a smear of toothpaste containing at least 1,000 parts per million fluoride, according to Delivering Better Oral Health guidance. A pea-sized amount at 1,350 parts per million is the recommendation from age six onwards, not for children under three. Withholding toothpaste entirely would deny the benefit of topical fluoride during a period of active caries risk.
  34. The correct answer is B (A pea-sized amount of toothpaste containing 1,000 parts per million fluoride or above.): Children aged three to six years should use a pea-sized amount of toothpaste containing 1,000 parts per million fluoride or above. A smear applies to children under three, while 2,800 parts per million toothpaste is reserved for high caries risk from age ten onwards, not routine use at age five. A full brush-head amount is excessive at any age and increases the risk of fluorosis if swallowed.
  35. The correct answer is D (A pea-sized amount of toothpaste containing 1,350 to 1,500 parts per million fluoride.): From the age of six years onwards, and in adults, a normal pea-sized amount of toothpaste containing 1,350 to 1,500 parts per million fluoride is recommended for routine use. Lower concentrations and smears apply to younger children, while 5,000 parts per million preparations are reserved for high-risk individuals aged sixteen or over. A healthy nine-year-old without additional risk factors should use the standard age-appropriate concentration.
  36. The correct answer is B (From the age of ten years.): For children assessed as being at high caries risk, a higher-strength toothpaste containing 2,800 parts per million fluoride may be prescribed from the age of ten years. A 5,000 parts per million preparation is reserved for high-risk individuals from sixteen years onwards. Six years and three years mark thresholds for standard concentration changes, not the higher-strength prescription toothpaste.
  37. The correct answer is A (Do not replant the tooth, since primary teeth are never reinserted.): Avulsed primary teeth are never replanted, because the reinsertion procedure risks driving the root into the developing permanent tooth germ and causing enamel hypoplasia, discolouration, or other developmental disturbance of the permanent successor. This differs fundamentally from management of an avulsed permanent tooth, where prompt replantation is encouraged. Storage medium is therefore irrelevant to the decision for a primary tooth.
  38. The correct answer is D (The periodontal ligament cells are unlikely to have survived.): A permanent tooth that has been out of the mouth and dry for longer than sixty minutes has a periodontal ligament that is unlikely to survive, since survival of these cells determines whether healing occurs by reattachment or by ankylosis and eventual replacement resorption. The long-term approach in such cases shifts towards accepting an ankylosed outcome or planning decoronation. Extraoral dry time is therefore the single most important prognostic factor.
  39. The correct answer is A (Storing the tooth in milk.): If a permanent avulsed tooth cannot be replanted immediately, storage in milk, saline, or the patient's own saliva in a container, or in the mouth without allowing it to dry out, considerably improves the prognosis by preserving periodontal ligament cell viability. Dry storage allows rapid cell death and worsens prognosis. Scrubbing the root surface or exposing it to hot water would further damage the remaining ligament cells.
  40. The correct answer is A (A flexible splint for around two weeks.): Once replanted, a permanent avulsed tooth is typically splinted with a flexible splint for around two weeks, allowing physiological mobility that supports periodontal ligament healing rather than encouraging ankylosis. Prolonged rigid splinting can increase the risk of ankylosis. The patient also requires long-term monitoring for pulp necrosis, inflammatory resorption, or ankylosis after the splint is removed.
  41. The correct answer is B (Indirect pulp capping.): Indirect pulp capping is used where caries is close to but has not exposed the pulp, involving removal of the bulk of caries while leaving a thin protective layer over the pulp before restoring the tooth. Pulpotomy is reserved for cases where the pulp has actually been exposed but remains vital and asymptomatic. Extraction and primary tooth root canal treatment are reserved for non-vital or infected pulps.
  42. The correct answer is B (Pulpotomy, removing the coronal pulp and preserving the vital radicular pulp.): Where the pulp has been exposed but is assessed as vital and asymptomatic with no evidence of infection, a pulpotomy, removing the coronal pulp while leaving the healthy radicular pulp in the root canals, is the treatment of choice. Indirect pulp capping is used only when the pulp has not actually been exposed. Extraction and full root canal treatment are reserved for non-vital or infected primary teeth.
  43. The correct answer is C (Ferric sulfate.): Ferric sulfate is commonly used in primary tooth pulpotomy as a haemostatic agent, achieving haemostasis without the mummifying, cytotoxic effect associated with older formocresol-based techniques, which have fallen out of favour due to toxicity and systemic absorption concerns. Following haemostasis, the chamber floor is typically covered with mineral trioxide aggregate or a zinc oxide eugenol-based dressing. Calcium hydroxide alone is not the standard haemostatic agent described in this technique.
  44. The correct answer is B (A preformed metal crown.): Following pulp therapy, the tooth is usually restored with a preformed metal crown, which provides a durable, well-sealed coronal restoration appropriate for a tooth that has undergone pulpotomy. Simple restorations without full cuspal coverage are more prone to failure or leakage in a tooth already weakened by extensive caries removal. A temporary dressing alone would not provide adequate long-term protection.
  45. The correct answer is A (Dental neglect.): Dental neglect is defined as the persistent failure to meet a child's basic oral health needs, likely to result in serious impairment of oral or general health, and can present as widespread untreated caries and repeated failure to attend despite appropriate support and reminders. Non-accidental injury instead refers to physical harm rather than neglect of care. This presentation reflects a safeguarding concern rather than simple anxiety or normal variation.
  46. The correct answer is A (The Ionising Radiations Regulations 2017 (IRR17).): The Ionising Radiations Regulations 2017, IRR17, primarily protect the safety of staff and the public from the radiation equipment itself, covering equipment safety, controlled areas, staff dose limits, and appointment of a Radiation Protection Adviser and Supervisor. IRMER17 instead specifically governs protection of the patient undergoing a medical exposure. The other options are not the specific legislation governing equipment and public radiation safety.
  47. The correct answer is D (The Ionising Radiation (Medical Exposure) Regulations 2017 (IRMER17).): IRMER17 specifically governs the protection of the patient undergoing a medical exposure, setting out duty holder roles and procedures for every diagnostic radiographic exposure. IRR17 instead protects staff and the public from the radiation equipment itself rather than the patient's own exposure. The other regulations listed are unrelated to ionising radiation exposure of dental patients.
  48. The correct answer is D (ALARP.): ALARP, meaning As Low As Reasonably Practicable, is the guiding principle underlying all use of ionising radiation in dentistry, ensuring doses are minimised consistent with obtaining the required diagnostic information. Justification and optimisation are related but distinct principles built explicitly into IRMER17 that support this overarching aim. Informed consent is a separate ethical and legal requirement unrelated specifically to radiation dose minimisation.
  49. The correct answer is A (Justification.): Justification requires that every exposure must be justified in advance, weighing diagnostic benefit against radiation risk, with no exposure permitted as a matter of routine without individual clinical indication. Optimisation instead applies once an exposure has already been justified, focusing on minimising the dose used. ALARP is the broader overarching principle rather than the specific pre-exposure justification requirement.
  50. The correct answer is C (Optimisation.): Optimisation requires that once an exposure is justified, the dose used must be kept to the minimum necessary to obtain the required diagnostic information, achieved through measures such as sensor speed, correct exposure settings, collimation, and appropriate protective equipment. Justification instead concerns the prior decision of whether the exposure should be carried out at all. Referral and reporting describe separate stages of the imaging pathway.
  51. The correct answer is B (The referrer.): Under IRMER17, the referrer is the registered healthcare professional who refers a patient for an exposure, providing sufficient clinical information to justify the request. The practitioner is the professional who takes responsibility for justifying the individual exposure based on this information. The operator carries out the practical aspects of taking the image, while the employer sets overarching written procedures.
  52. The correct answer is D (The practitioner.): The practitioner is the registered healthcare professional who takes responsibility for justifying an individual exposure, confirming it is appropriate given the clinical information provided, and this role is often, though not always, held by the same dentist who also acts as operator. The referrer instead requests the exposure, and the operator carries out the practical steps of taking the image. The Radiation Protection Adviser is an IRR17 role rather than an IRMER17 duty holder.
  53. The correct answer is C (The operator.): The operator carries out practical aspects of the exposure, which may include positioning the patient, setting exposure factors, pressing the exposure button, or processing the image, provided each task is entitled under the employer's procedures and the person is appropriately trained. A trained dental nurse can act as an operator for the practical taking of an image without necessarily being entitled to justify or clinically report on it. The practitioner and referrer instead hold justification and referral responsibilities.
  54. The correct answer is A (The employer.): The employer is the legal entity under IRMER17 responsible for setting up written procedures, protocols, and quality assurance programmes governing radiographic practice within the organisation. The referrer, practitioner and operator instead hold individual clinical and practical responsibilities relating to specific exposures rather than the overarching organisational framework. This distinction is a common examination point regarding IRMER17 duty holders.
  55. The correct answer is C (It produces a geometrically more accurate image with less distortion.): The paralleling technique is preferred over the bisecting angle technique because positioning the receptor parallel to the tooth's long axis, with the beam perpendicular to both, produces a geometrically more accurate image with less foreshortening and elongation, allowing reproducible comparisons over time. It relies on, rather than eliminates, the use of a beam-aiming device. Dose reduction and specific suitability for young children are not the primary advantages described.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (A bitewing, because it shows approximal contact areas clearly without superimposition.): Bitewing radiographs hold the receptor parallel to the crowns of both arches with a slight downward beam angulation, and are the radiograph of choice for detecting approximal caries because interproximal contact areas are shown clearly without the superimposition problems that can occur on other views. Periapical views prioritise apical detail over interproximal clarity, while panoramic and CBCT imaging are not first-line choices for early approximal caries detection.
  2. The correct answer is C (Panoramic radiography (OPG).): Panoramic radiography, or OPG, produces a single image showing both dental arches, the temporomandibular joints, the maxillary sinuses and the mandible, making it useful for broad overview assessment and for evaluating the position of unerupted or impacted teeth such as third molars. Bitewing and periapical views are limited to smaller anatomical areas and cannot provide this broad single-image overview. Individual periapical radiographs of each arch would not achieve the same efficient overview.
  3. The correct answer is D (It has lower resolution and is subject to magnification and distortion.): Panoramic radiography has lower resolution than intraoral views and is subject to magnification and distortion, particularly in the anterior region, making it unsuitable as the technique of choice for detecting early approximal caries or fine periodontal detail. Its dose is considerably lower than a medical CT scan, and it can be used in children when clinically indicated. Breath-holding is not a requirement of the technique.
  4. The correct answer is B (It must be specifically justified and used only when conventional imaging is insufficient.): Because of its higher radiation dose, CBCT use must be specifically justified for each case and should only be requested where the diagnostic information required cannot be obtained from conventional imaging. It is not appropriate as a routine or first-line screening tool given this dose consideration. Standard periapical-level justification is insufficient given the higher dose involved.
  5. The correct answer is D (Cone beam computed tomography (CBCT).): Common indications for CBCT include assessment of the proximity of impacted lower third molars to the inferior alveolar canal prior to surgical removal, since three-dimensional imaging can clarify a relationship not always clear on two-dimensional views. Bitewing and periapical radiographs are not designed to assess this specific anatomical relationship. A repeat panoramic view alone would not resolve genuine uncertainty about canal proximity in three dimensions.
  6. The correct answer is D (The incisive foramen.): The incisive foramen is a radiolucent landmark seen between the roots of the upper central incisors, and recognising it prevents misdiagnosis as pathology. The maxillary sinus is a larger radiolucency located more posteriorly in the upper arch, while the mental foramen is a lower arch landmark near the premolar apices. Mistaking this normal structure for a periapical lesion is a recognised interpretation pitfall.
  7. The correct answer is B (The zygomatic process and zygomatic buttress.): The zygomatic process and zygomatic buttress can be superimposed over the roots of the upper molars on radiographs and may be mistaken for pathology or a root fracture line if not recognised as normal radiopaque anatomy. The mandibular canal, genial tubercles and mental foramen are all lower arch landmarks and would not appear superimposed over upper molar roots. Correct identification prevents unnecessary further investigation or treatment.
  8. The correct answer is B (The mandibular canal, important when planning extraction of lower molars or third molars.): The mandibular canal, carrying the inferior alveolar nerve and vessels, appears as a radiolucent band often bordered by thin radiopaque lines, and its relationship to the roots of the lower molars and to an impacted lower third molar is of particular clinical importance when planning extractions. The mental foramen, submandibular fossa and genial tubercles are distinct anatomical landmarks with different clinical relevance. Correctly identifying the mandibular canal helps avoid nerve injury during surgical procedures.
  9. The correct answer is C (Recognising this may represent the mental foramen rather than a lesion.): The mental foramen appears as a round or oval radiolucency near the apices of the lower premolars and can be mistaken for a periapical lesion if its typical location and well-corticated outline are not recognised. Assuming pathology without correlating with clinical findings risks unnecessary extraction or endodontic treatment of a healthy tooth. Radiographic findings must always be correlated with clinical examination before committing to a diagnosis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Cervical burnout, an artefactual radiolucency rather than true caries.): Cervical burnout is an artefactual radiolucent band seen at the cervical margin of a tooth due to reduced thickness of tooth structure at that point rather than true caries, and is a frequently tested interpretation pitfall. Diagnosing early caries from this appearance alone without clinical correlation risks unnecessary restorative treatment. The genial tubercles are an unrelated anterior mandibular midline landmark, and this appearance is not typical of periapical pathology.
  11. The correct answer is C (Arrange a radiograph to assess the position of the permanent successor.): Prolonged retention of a primary tooth beyond the expected eruption date of its successor warrants radiographic investigation, since it can indicate ectopic position, impaction, or absence of the permanent tooth. Extraction or fitting a space maintainer before investigating the cause could worsen an underlying problem. Reassurance without investigation risks missing a genuine developmental issue requiring early management.
  12. The correct answer is A (A band and loop space maintainer.): The band and loop appliance is indicated when a single primary molar is lost unilaterally, using an adjacent erupted tooth as an abutment to maintain space for the permanent successor. A distal shoe is reserved for cases where the first permanent molar has not yet erupted, which does not apply here since the first permanent molars are already present. A bilateral lingual arch is used for bilateral rather than unilateral space loss.
  13. The correct answer is D (A shift of the dental midline towards the extraction side.): Loss of a primary canine can cause the labial segment to drift towards the extraction side, leading to a shift of the dental midline, and this should be assessed carefully following any early canine loss. The permanent canine successor will not erupt significantly earlier because of this loss. The leeway space concept relates to molar and premolar width differences in the same arch, not the opposite arch.
  14. The correct answer is D (A distal shoe appliance.): A distal shoe appliance is used when the first permanent molar has not yet erupted and the second primary molar is lost early, since its submerged guide plate directs the erupting first permanent molar into correct position. A band and loop is used once the neighbouring permanent molar has already erupted to act as an abutment. A lingual arch requires erupted permanent molars bilaterally as abutments, which are not yet present here.
  15. The correct answer is D (It represents normal primate spacing that later accommodates larger permanent incisors.): This spacing represents normal primate spacing, present mesial to the upper primary canines and distal to the lower primary canines, and is a normal developmental feature that later helps accommodate the larger permanent incisors. It does not indicate space loss, since no teeth have been lost prematurely. This pattern requires no active intervention or referral at this age.
  16. The correct answer is B (Class III malocclusion.): Class III malocclusion describes a mesial relationship of the lower arch to the upper arch, typically presenting with a reduced or reversed overjet and sometimes an anterior crossbite, consistent with lower incisors positioned labial to the uppers. Class II division 1 and 2 both describe a distal lower arch relationship with an increased or reduced overjet respectively, not a reversed one. Class I represents a normal anteroposterior relationship, which does not fit this reversed pattern.
  17. The correct answer is B (Sealants physically block deep pits and fissures where plaque would otherwise stagnate.): Fissure sealants work by physically blocking the deep pits and fissures of at-risk permanent molars where plaque would otherwise stagnate and caries would otherwise initiate, and are applied as soon as practicable after eruption. They are used alongside, not instead of, fluoride varnish as part of a broader preventive regimen. Sealants act as a physical barrier rather than through any systemic fluoride mechanism, and are placed before caries develops, not after.
  18. The correct answer is D (This represents the normal onset of the mixed dentition stage.): The mixed dentition transition begins around six years of age with eruption of the lower permanent central incisors and the first permanent molars, exactly as shown here, so this represents the normal onset rather than a delayed pattern. No primary teeth have been lost prematurely, since the primary lateral incisors, canines and molars remain present alongside the newly erupted permanent teeth. The late mixed dentition stage involves loss of primary canines and molars, which has not yet occurred here.
  19. The correct answer is D (Discuss space management options with orthodontic input as part of the wider plan.): Where a permanent successor is congenitally absent, the decision on whether to maintain space, allow closure, or plan for prosthetic replacement should be made in conjunction with the wider treatment plan and ideally with orthodontic input. There is no single default management approach that applies regardless of the individual case. The space will not close or resolve on its own without active planning and management.
  20. The correct answer is A (Bilateral distal shoe appliances.): A distal shoe appliance is indicated when the first permanent molar has not yet erupted and the second primary molar is lost early, using a submerged guide plate to direct eruption, and bilateral loss here calls for bilateral distal shoes. A lingual arch requires erupted permanent molars as abutments, which are not yet present bilaterally. A single band and loop would not address the bilateral nature of this space loss.
  21. The correct answer is C (Refer for further assessment and monitoring of the ectopic canine.): An ectopically positioned unerupted permanent canine identified on a panoramic radiograph warrants referral for further assessment and monitoring, since early identification allows interceptive management before the position worsens. Extracting the primary tooth alone without further assessment does not address the underlying ectopic path of the permanent tooth. Surgical exposure would only be considered after appropriate specialist assessment, not as an immediate first step.
  22. The correct answer is A (Cone beam computed tomography (CBCT).): CBCT is indicated to assess the proximity of an impacted lower third molar to the inferior alveolar canal when a panoramic radiograph shows loss of the normal cortical outline, since three-dimensional imaging clarifies a relationship that two-dimensional views cannot reliably resolve. A repeat panoramic view cannot provide the three-dimensional detail needed to plan safely around the nerve. Bitewing and periapical views are not designed to assess this deep anatomical relationship.
  23. The correct answer is D (Signs of pulp necrosis, inflammatory resorption, or ankylosis.): Following replantation, a permanent avulsed tooth requires long-term monitoring for signs of pulp necrosis, inflammatory resorption, or ankylosis, since these complications determine the tooth's eventual prognosis. An immature, open apex gives a better chance of pulp revascularisation than a mature closed apex, but ongoing review remains essential. Approximal caries and dentigerous cyst formation are unrelated to the specific complications of a replanted avulsed tooth.
  24. The correct answer is A (Normal onset of the mixed dentition stage.): Eruption of the first permanent molars and lower permanent central incisors around six years of age, with the remaining primary dentition otherwise intact, represents the normal onset of the mixed dentition stage. This timing is not delayed, since it falls within the expected age range for these specific teeth. No teeth have been lost prematurely and no permanent tooth germs are absent on this description.
  25. The correct answer is A (As the normal mental foramen rather than periapical pathology.): The mental foramen appears as a round or oval radiolucency near the apices of the lower premolars, and its typical location with a well-corticated outline should be recognised as normal anatomy rather than periapical pathology. Diagnosing periapical disease from this appearance alone would risk unnecessary endodontic treatment or extraction of a healthy tooth. Clinical findings must always be correlated with the radiograph before committing to a diagnosis.
  26. The correct answer is B (Interproximal contact areas are shown clearly without superimposition problems.): Bitewing radiographs are the radiograph of choice for detecting approximal caries because interproximal contact areas are shown clearly without the superimposition problems that can occur on other views, allowing early lesions to be identified before they are clinically detectable. This view is not primarily intended to display full root length, which is instead the purpose of a periapical radiograph. It provides two-dimensional, not three-dimensional, detail and does not replace clinical examination.
  27. The correct answer is B (Cervical burnout, an artefactual radiolucency rather than true caries.): Cervical burnout is an artefactual radiolucent band at the cervical margin of a tooth caused by reduced thickness of tooth structure at that point rather than true caries, and is a well-recognised interpretation pitfall. Treating this appearance as caries without clinical correlation risks unnecessary restorative treatment of a sound tooth. A periapical lesion would be located apically rather than at the cervical margin, and there is no clinical evidence here of resorption.
  28. The correct answer is B (Referral for specialist orthodontic assessment.): A displaced permanent successor identified on radiograph following prolonged retention of a primary tooth warrants referral for specialist orthodontic assessment, since early intervention can improve the outcome for the ectopic tooth. Reassurance alone is not appropriate given the confirmed displacement rather than normal development. Extraction without referral, or simply repeating the radiograph with no other action, would delay appropriate specialist input.
  29. The correct answer is A (A better chance of pulp revascularisation following replantation.): A tooth with an immature, incompletely formed root and a wide open apex has a better chance of pulp revascularisation following replantation than a tooth with a mature closed apex, because the wider apical opening allows greater potential for revascularisation of the pulp space. This is an important prognostic difference rather than having no bearing on outcome. Immediate root canal treatment is not an absolute requirement in an immature tooth, since revascularisation may still be possible with monitoring.
  30. The correct answer is A (Whether to maintain, allow closure of, or prosthetically manage the space, with orthodontic input.): Where a permanent successor is congenitally absent and the primary tooth is still functional, the decision on whether to maintain space, allow closure, or plan prosthetic or implant replacement should be made with orthodontic input as part of a wider treatment plan. Extracting the functional primary molar without a plan would create unnecessary space loss. Congenital absence of a successor is a clinically significant finding that requires active planning rather than no management.
EMQ Set EMQ-ortho-1

For each malocclusion description below, select the ONE most appropriate Angle classification from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Class I malocclusion

B. Class II division 1 malocclusion

C. Class II division 2 malocclusion

D. Class III malocclusion, dentoalveolar in origin

E. Class III malocclusion, skeletal in origin

  1. The correct answer is A (Class I malocclusion): a normal molar relationship, as described here, defines Class I occlusion, and malocclusion such as crowding can still exist within a Class I molar relationship without any skeletal discrepancy. Class II and III both describe distal or mesial displacement of the lower arch relative to the upper, which is absent here. This scenario reflects a dentoalveolar crowding problem rather than an anteroposterior classification issue.
  2. The correct answer is B (Class II division 1 malocclusion): a distal lower arch relationship with proclined upper incisors, an increased overjet, and incompetent lip seal with lower lip trapping is the classic presentation of Class II division 1. Class II division 2 instead shows retroclined incisors with a reduced overjet. This lip trapping pattern can perpetuate the increased overjet if untreated.
  3. The correct answer is C (Class II division 2 malocclusion): retroclined upper central incisors with proclined, rotated lateral incisors, a reduced overjet and a deep traumatic overbite are characteristic of Class II division 2. Class II division 1 instead presents with proclined incisors and an increased overjet. This pattern is associated with a more horizontal growth pattern.
  4. The correct answer is D (Class III malocclusion, dentoalveolar in origin): a mild, isolated anterior crossbite with reduced overjet but normal facial proportions and no skeletal features reflects a dentoalveolar Class III presentation rather than an underlying skeletal discrepancy. Skeletal Class III cases typically show more marked features such as mandibular prognathism. Dentoalveolar cases can often be managed with orthodontics alone.
  5. The correct answer is E (Class III malocclusion, skeletal in origin): marked mandibular prognathism with a reversed overjet and a generalised anterior crossbite reflects an underlying skeletal discrepancy rather than a purely dental problem. Once growth is complete, such cases are often best managed with combined orthodontic and orthognathic surgical treatment, since orthodontics alone cannot correct a significant skeletal discrepancy. Dentoalveolar Class III cases are typically milder and more isolated.
EMQ Set EMQ-ortho-2

For each paediatric trauma presentation below, select the ONE most appropriate management option from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Do not replant; primary teeth are never reinserted

B. Replant immediately and splint with a flexible splint for two weeks

C. Accept a guarded prognosis and plan for eventual ankylosis or decoronation

D. Manage conservatively with monitoring, no active intervention required

E. Extraction is preferred over attempted repositioning and splinting

F. Apply calcium hydroxide dressing and review in one week

G. Refer urgently for orthodontic space maintenance only

H. Prescribe systemic antibiotics and review in 24 hours

  1. The correct answer is A (Do not replant; primary teeth are never reinserted): avulsed primary teeth are never replanted because reinsertion risks driving the root into the developing permanent tooth germ, causing enamel hypoplasia or other developmental disturbance. This differs fundamentally from management of an avulsed permanent tooth. The intact condition of the tooth does not change this rule.
  2. The correct answer is C (Accept a guarded prognosis and plan for eventual ankylosis or decoronation): a permanent tooth that has been out of the mouth and dry for longer than sixty minutes has a periodontal ligament unlikely to survive, so the long-term approach shifts towards accepting an ankylosed outcome or planning decoronation. Immediate replantation with standard splinting still occurs, but the prognosis for periodontal ligament survival is poor. Extraoral dry time beyond sixty minutes is the key adverse prognostic factor here.
  3. The correct answer is B (Replant immediately and splint with a flexible splint for two weeks): prompt replantation within a short extraoral time, with appropriate storage in milk, gives a considerably improved prognosis, and the standard approach is a flexible splint for around two weeks with long-term monitoring. An open apex further improves the chance of pulp revascularisation compared with a closed apex. This favourable combination supports standard active management rather than a guarded approach.
  4. The correct answer is D (Manage conservatively with monitoring, no active intervention required): displaced or luxated primary teeth are usually managed conservatively unless there is significant mobility or occlusal interference, and mild mobility without occlusal interference does not require active intervention. Extraction is reserved for cases with more significant mobility or interference. Conservative monitoring avoids unnecessary treatment of the developing permanent successor's overlying structures.
  5.
