The correct answer is E (Extraction is preferred over attempted repositioning and splinting): where a displaced or luxated primary tooth shows significant mobility or occlusal interference, extraction is generally preferred over attempting to reposition and splint the primary tooth. Splinting a markedly mobile primary tooth is less predictable and carries greater risk of complications. This differs from the approach to a similarly injured permanent tooth, where splinting is the standard treatment.
EMQ Set EMQ-ortho-3

For each description below, select the ONE most appropriate IRMER17 or IRR17 role from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Referrer

B. Practitioner

C. Operator

D. Employer

E. Radiation Protection Adviser

  1. The correct answer is B (Practitioner): the practitioner is the registered healthcare professional who takes responsibility for justifying an individual exposure, confirming it is appropriate given the clinical information provided. This role is often, though not always, held by the same dentist who also acts as operator. The referrer instead requests the exposure rather than justifying it.
  2. The correct answer is A (Referrer): the referrer is the registered healthcare professional who refers a patient for an exposure, providing sufficient clinical information to justify the request. The practitioner then uses this information to justify the individual exposure. The referrer does not carry out the practical or justification steps themselves.
  3. The correct answer is C (Operator): the operator carries out practical aspects of the exposure, including positioning the patient, setting exposure factors and pressing the exposure button, provided they are entitled by the employer's procedures and appropriately trained. A trained dental nurse can fulfil this role for the practical taking of an image without necessarily being entitled to justify or report on it. The practitioner and referrer hold different, non-practical responsibilities.
  4. The correct answer is D (Employer): the employer is the legal entity under IRMER17 responsible for setting up written procedures, protocols and quality assurance programmes governing radiographic practice within the organisation. This is a distinct organisational duty from the individual clinical roles of referrer, practitioner and operator. Confusing the employer with an individual duty holder is a common examination pitfall.
  5. The correct answer is E (Radiation Protection Adviser): the Radiation Protection Adviser is appointed under IRR17, which protects staff and the public from the radiation equipment itself, rather than being an IRMER17 patient-protection duty holder. This distinguishes the Radiation Protection Adviser from the referrer, practitioner, operator and employer roles defined under IRMER17. Both sets of regulations work together to govern safe radiographic practice.
EMQ Set EMQ-ortho-4

For each safeguarding presentation below, select the ONE most appropriate concern or action from the list. Each option may be used once, more than once, or not at all.

A. Likely dental neglect; arrange preventive support and monitor

B. Signs of non-accidental injury; discuss with the named safeguarding lead and consider referral to children's social care

C. Immediate danger disclosed; contact the police without delay

D. No safeguarding concern; a plausible explanation is consistent with the injury

E. Torn labial frenum inconsistent with the child's age and activity; raises concern for possible non-accidental injury

  1. The correct answer is A (Likely dental neglect; arrange preventive support and monitor): dental neglect is defined as persistent failure to meet a child's basic oral health needs, and can present as widespread untreated caries with repeated failure to attend despite appropriate support and reminders. This presentation reflects neglect of care rather than a single acute injury. Preventive support and careful monitoring, alongside local safeguarding procedures, is the appropriate initial response.
  2. The correct answer is B (Signs of non-accidental injury; discuss with the named safeguarding lead and consider referral to children's social care): bruising in a pattern suggestive of fingertip marks, inconsistent with the explanation given, is a recognised sign of non-accidental injury. Any dental professional with such a concern has a duty to act, typically by discussing with the practice's named safeguarding lead and referring to children's social care as appropriate. Ignoring the concern or managing only the presenting dental problem is never acceptable.
  3. The correct answer is E (Torn labial frenum inconsistent with the child's age and activity; raises concern for possible non-accidental injury): a torn labial or lingual frenum in a child too young to have caused this through normal activity is a recognised sign that may raise concern about non-accidental injury. An eight-month-old infant is not developmentally capable of causing this injury through typical activity. This finding warrants the same safeguarding response as other suspicious injuries.
  4. The correct answer is C (Immediate danger disclosed; contact the police without delay): where there is immediate danger to a child, contacting the police without delay is part of the statutory safeguarding response, in addition to the usual practice of discussing with the named safeguarding lead and referring to children's social care. A disclosure of ongoing, immediate risk requires urgent action rather than routine referral alone. Delaying action in the face of immediate danger would not fulfil the duty to safeguard the child.
  5. The correct answer is D (No safeguarding concern; a plausible explanation is consistent with the injury): a small bruise on the shin with a history consistent with normal mobile play is a common and expected finding in an active toddler, and does not by itself raise a safeguarding concern. Recognising normal childhood injury patterns is important to avoid unnecessary alarm or referral. Safeguarding concerns arise when the explanation is inconsistent with the injury, the injury pattern is atypical, or there are other associated red flags.
  6. The correct answer is B (Raise the concern informally first, then through clinical governance or the GDC if patient safety remains at risk): Principle 8 of Standards for the Dental Team requires registrants to raise concerns if patients are at risk. Staying silent to protect a colleague is itself a breach of professional obligation. The concern should be escalated proportionately, using internal governance routes before contacting the GDC directly.
  7. The correct answer is C (Decline until adequate training and supervised experience in implant placement have been obtained): Principle 7 requires registrants to work within their professional knowledge and skills and undertake only treatment they are trained and competent to provide. Practising beyond one's competence risks patient harm and a fitness to practise referral, regardless of who instructed it.
  8. The correct answer is D (Refuse, since dishonest documentation breaches the duty to maintain patients' confidence in the profession): Principle 9 requires personal behaviour that maintains patients' confidence in the dentist and the profession. Dishonesty in official documents, even at a patient's request, is a serious probity issue that can lead to a fitness to practise finding.
  9. The correct answer is A (Extend or rearrange the appointment so the patient receives a full, unhurried explanation): Principle 2 requires effective communication with patients so they can make informed decisions. Rushing an explanation undermines both communication and valid consent, so the appointment should be adjusted rather than compromising the discussion.
  10. The correct answer is B (Listen to the concern, investigate it, and address it through the practice's clinical governance process): Principle 6 requires working with colleagues in a way that is in patients' best interests, which includes taking team members' safety concerns seriously. Dismissing a legitimate concern risks patient harm and undermines the practice's safety culture.
  11. The correct answer is D (This is a breach of Principle 7, which requires maintaining and developing professional knowledge and skills): Principle 7 obliges registrants to keep their knowledge and skills up to date through recognised CPD. Failing to meet CPD requirements, particularly in a patient safety area such as infection control, can itself trigger a fitness to practise concern.
  12. The correct answer is A (Explain the risks clearly, refuse to proceed with sedation unless a suitable escort is arranged, and document this): Putting patients' interests first, Principle 1, means the dentist must not provide sedation without a safe recovery and escort plan, since driving after sedation poses a serious and foreseeable risk of harm that overrides the patient's stated preference.
  13. The correct answer is A (Review the affected records, address the deficiency with the locum, and correct the practice's systems): Principle 4 and good clinical governance require accurate, justified radiographic records. A principal dentist has an ongoing responsibility to ensure standards are met across the practice, including addressing shortfalls left by a departed locum.
  14. The correct answer is D (Politely decline to discuss it and redirect the conversation back to the patient's dental care): Principle 9 requires personal conduct that maintains patients' confidence in the profession. Maintaining professional boundaries and focus on clinical care, without being dismissive, best supports the therapeutic relationship.
  15. The correct answer is B (Base recommendations on individual clinical need and full disclosure of appropriate alternatives, not instruction): Principle 1 requires putting patients' interests first, and Principle 3 requires valid, informed consent that includes reasonable alternatives. Treatment recommendations must be driven by clinical need, not commercial instruction from practice management.
  16. The correct answer is D (No, because Montgomery requires disclosure of any risk a reasonable patient would attach significance to): Montgomery v Lanarkshire Health Board replaced the doctor-centred Bolam test with a patient-centred materiality test. Nerve injury is a recognised, foreseeable risk of third molar surgery that a reasonable patient would want to know about, regardless of how commonly other clinicians mention it.
  17. The correct answer is C (No, valid consent requires a specific discussion of the actual proposed treatment, risks and alternatives): Consent is a process, not a signature. A generic form signed before any clinical discussion cannot substitute for the dentist explaining the specific treatment, its material risks and reasonable alternatives, as required following Montgomery v Lanarkshire Health Board.
  18. The correct answer is C (The dentist must disclose this risk because the clinician knows this particular patient would attach significance to it): Montgomery establishes that materiality is assessed both from a reasonable patient's perspective and from what the clinician knows or should know matters to this individual patient. A known occupational sensitivity to a specific risk must be disclosed even if the risk is statistically small.
  19. The correct answer is A (Only proceed with the originally consented tooth, unless it is a genuine emergency threatening life or limb): Consent for one procedure does not extend to another procedure discovered incidentally. Save for genuine emergencies, the dentist must stop and obtain the patient's separate, informed consent for the additional extraction once anaesthesia and communication allow.
  20. The correct answer is B (Respect the patient's informed refusal and proceed with the extraction the patient has chosen): An adult patient with capacity has the right to refuse recommended treatment and choose a reasonable alternative, even if the clinician disagrees with the choice, provided the decision is informed and voluntary. Overriding a capacitous refusal would be unlawful.
  21. The correct answer is C (Explicit, specific consent for that purpose must be obtained separately from clinical consent): Using identifiable patient images for marketing is a separate purpose from clinical care and requires its own specific, informed consent under data protection and confidentiality principles, distinct from consent to treatment itself.
  22. The correct answer is D (Explain the likely consequences of no treatment as part of a full discussion of alternatives): Montgomery requires discussion of reasonable alternatives, which includes the option of doing nothing and its likely consequences, so the patient can make a genuinely informed choice among all reasonable courses of action.
  23. The correct answer is B (Proceed based on the patient's own capacitous consent, since capacity is decision-specific, not decided by family members): The Mental Capacity Act 2005 presumes capacity unless proven otherwise, and capacity is assessed for the specific decision at hand. If the patient can understand, retain briefly, weigh and communicate the decision, their own consent is valid regardless of family objection.
  24. The correct answer is C (Contemporaneous, detailed documentation of the consent discussion, including risks and alternatives covered): Good record-keeping standards require that the consent discussion, including specific material risks and alternatives discussed, be documented at the time. Without this, the dentist cannot demonstrate that valid, informed consent was actually obtained.
  25. The correct answer is B (Respect the patient's right to waive detailed disclosure, while confirming they understand the basic nature of the procedure): A capacitous patient can validly decline detailed information, but the dentist should still confirm basic understanding of what is being done and document that the patient exercised an informed choice not to hear more detail.
  26. The correct answer is C (Use a professional interpreter where possible, since family members may filter or simplify important information): Effective communication under Principle 2 and valid consent under Montgomery both require the patient to genuinely understand material risks and alternatives. A professional interpreter reduces the risk of information being filtered, omitted or misunderstood compared with an informal family interpreter.
  27. The correct answer is A (No, the dentist must still discuss it, since the patient has directly signalled that this matters to them): Materiality under Montgomery is judged partly by what the clinician knows or should know matters to this individual patient. A patient who directly raises a question about a risk has clearly signalled its significance, so it must be addressed.
  28. The correct answer is D (Consent was likely inadequate, since time pressure does not excuse failing to ensure genuine patient understanding): Valid consent requires the patient to actually understand the nature, risks, benefits and alternatives of the proposed treatment. A rushed discussion driven by scheduling pressure that leaves the patient without genuine understanding does not meet the Montgomery standard.
  29. The correct answer is A (Explain why the treatment is not clinically indicated and decline to provide it, while respecting the patient's autonomy generally): Patient autonomy under Montgomery concerns the right to accept or refuse treatment that is clinically appropriate; it does not require a dentist to provide treatment they judge to be clinically inappropriate or harmful, though the reasoning should be explained clearly.
  30. The correct answer is A (No, likely timescale and the possibility of extended treatment duration are also material information for most patients): Material information for informed consent extends beyond clinical risk to include factors a reasonable patient would want to know when deciding whether to proceed, and realistic treatment duration is a significant factor in a long course of orthodontic care.
  31. The correct answer is D (Yes, if she is assessed as Gillick competent, meaning she has sufficient understanding and maturity for this decision): Gillick v West Norfolk and Wisbech Area Health Authority established that a child under 16 can give valid consent if they demonstrate sufficient understanding and intelligence to fully comprehend what is proposed, its purpose, and its risks and benefits, regardless of age alone.
  32. The correct answer is C (A person with parental responsibility, since the child is not Gillick competent for this decision): Gillick competence is assessed individually, not by age alone; a very young or frightened child who cannot understand the proposed treatment is not competent to consent, so a person with parental responsibility must consent, always in the child's best interests.
  33. The correct answer is D (Under the Family Law Reform Act 1969, she is presumed to have capacity to consent similarly to an adult): The Family Law Reform Act 1969 presumes that 16- and 17-year-olds have capacity to consent to their own medical and dental treatment in England and Wales, similarly to an adult, independent of parental agreement. Northern Ireland has its own separate but similarly worded provision, and Scotland is instead governed by the Age of Legal Capacity (Scotland) Act 1991.
  34.   35. The correct answer is B (Discuss the clinical options with both parents and seek to reach agreement, since either can generally consent but disagreement should be resolved): Where two people hold parental responsibility and disagree, the dentist should try to secure agreement through discussion, since consent from one holder of parental responsibility is generally sufficient legally but clinical practice favours resolving significant disagreements before non-urgent treatment.
  36. The correct answer is C (Consent from a person with parental responsibility, acting in the child's best interests, since the child is not Gillick competent): The Mental Capacity Act 2005 generally applies from age 16; for a younger child who is not Gillick competent, consent is obtained from a person with parental responsibility, and the treatment must still be in the child's best interests.
  37.   38.   39.   41.   42.   43. The correct answer is A (Whether the patient can understand, retain, weigh the relevant information, and communicate their decision): The Mental Capacity Act 2005 functional test asks whether the person can understand information relevant to the decision, retain it long enough to make the decision, use or weigh it, and communicate their decision by any means.
  44.   45.   46. The correct answer is A (A person is not to be treated as unable to make a decision unless all practicable steps to help them have failed): This is one of the five statutory principles of the Mental Capacity Act 2005: a person must not be treated as unable to make a decision unless all practicable steps to help them do so, such as simplified explanation or a better environment, have been tried without success.
  47. The correct answer is C (The treating clinician, having consulted family members where practicable, based on the patient's best interests): Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, family and carers should be consulted where practicable about a patient's wishes and best interests, but the final decision rests with the treating clinician, based on a genuine best interests assessment, not family preference alone.
  48. It is entirely possible, and legally correct, for a patient to lack capacity for a complex decision while retaining capacity for a simpler, more easily understood one.
  49. The correct answer is A (The Adults with Incapacity (Scotland) Act 2000): While the Mental Capacity Act 2005 applies in England and Wales, Scotland has its own equivalent legislation, the Adults with Incapacity (Scotland) Act 2000, governing decisions for adults who lack capacity.

  51.   119.   120. The correct answer is D (Examination, diagnosis and prevention): Under the UDA banding system, Band 1 commonly covers examination, diagnosis and preventive care, while Band 2 covers treatments such as fillings and extractions, and Band 3 covers more complex treatment such as crowns, bridges and dentures.
  121.   122.   123.   124.   125. The correct answer is A (The commissioner may review, adjust or take contractual action regarding the practice's NHS contract): A significant shortfall against an allocated UDA target is a contractual matter that commissioners can review and act upon, since UDAs represent the contracted volume of NHS activity the practice has agreed to deliver.
  126. The correct answer is C (The two systems differ mainly in funding structure and choice of materials or techniques, not in the required standard of clinical care): GDC Standards for the Dental Team apply equally to NHS and private treatment. The key differences between the systems relate to funding structure, patient charges, and the range of materials or techniques available, not a difference in the required standard of professional care.
  127. The correct answer is B (Raise the complaint with the practice itself in the first instance): The NHS complaints procedure requires a patient to first raise the complaint with the practice itself, and if unresolved, escalate to NHS England or the relevant integrated care board, before any further escalation to the Ombudsman.
  128. The correct answer is D (Refer the complaint to the Parliamentary and Health Service Ombudsman): If a patient remains dissatisfied after the local practice and NHS England or integrated care board stages of the NHS complaints procedure, they can refer the complaint to the Parliamentary and Health Service Ombudsman, which independently reviews unresolved NHS complaints.
  129. The correct answer is A (The Dental Complaints Service, a free and independent service for complaints about private dental treatment): For private dental treatment, the appropriate route if a complaint cannot be resolved directly with the practice is the Dental Complaints Service, a free and independent service operating under the auspices of the GDC but functioning at arm's length, specifically for private treatment complaints.
  130. The correct answer is A (No, it can facilitate resolution such as refunds or further treatment but does not investigate fitness to practise): The Dental Complaints Service can facilitate resolution of private treatment complaints, including recommending refunds or further treatment, but it does not have the power to strike a dentist off and does not investigate fitness to practise, which remains a matter for the GDC.
  131. The correct answer is C (Yes, a complaint disclosing a serious safety or probity concern may lead to a separate GDC fitness to practise referral): While most complaints are resolved locally or through the Dental Complaints Service, a complaint disclosing a serious concern about patient safety, dishonesty, or a significant departure from expected standards may in due course lead to a separate GDC fitness to practise referral.
  132. The correct answer is B (It independently reviews complaints about NHS care that have not been resolved locally): The Parliamentary and Health Service Ombudsman independently reviews complaints about NHS care, including NHS dental treatment, that have not been resolved through the local NHS complaints procedure, acting as a final independent review stage.
  133. The correct answer is A (A complaint in itself is not a fitness to practise referral, and most complaints are resolved locally): It is important for candidates and patients to understand that a complaint, in itself, is not a fitness to practise referral, and most complaints are resolved locally without ever reaching the GDC or resulting in any disciplinary action.
  134. The correct answer is D (Identify which elements of treatment were NHS-funded and which were private, and raise concerns with the practice covering both): Since NHS and private complaints routes differ, a patient with mixed funding should first raise concerns with the practice, which can help clarify which elements relate to NHS-funded care and which to private treatment, guiding the correct subsequent escalation route for each.
  135. The correct answer is D (Considering escalation of the pattern of concern to the GDC if internal handling has not resolved the risk to patients): Principle 8 requires registrants to raise concerns if patients are at risk. Where a pattern of complaints suggests an ongoing risk that local handling has not resolved, escalation to the GDC as a fitness to practise concern may be the appropriate next step.
  136. The correct answer is B (No material risks, such as nerve injury, are documented as having been discussed with the patient): The consent entry omits any mention of material risks such as inferior alveolar or lingual nerve injury, infection, dry socket or bleeding, which Montgomery v Lanarkshire Health Board requires to be discussed and documented for a lower third molar extraction.
  137. The correct answer is D (Any documented assessment of Gillick competence for this patient): Where a patient under 16 attends without a person with parental responsibility, good practice requires a documented assessment of Gillick competence, confirming the young person's understanding of the procedure, risks and benefits, which this record does not contain.
  138. The correct answer is A (The response was issued well outside the statutory time limit for responding to the request): Under the UK GDPR and Data Protection Act 2018, a subject access request must be responded to within a statutory time limit. A response issued significantly beyond this limit fails to meet the practice's obligations as a data controller.
  139. The correct answer is C (There is no record of considering the patient's past wishes or of consulting those close to the patient): A best interests decision under the Mental Capacity Act 2005 must take into account the patient's past and present wishes, beliefs and values, and consult those close to them where practicable, none of which is documented in this extract.
  140. The correct answer is B (Any documentation that the patient was told what happened and given an explanation): The duty of candour requires the dentist to inform the patient of what happened, explain the clinical significance, apologise, and document this discussion, none of which appears in this extract despite the incident being recorded internally.
